                                 Preface

ATP 3-39.30 is aligned with FM 3-39 of the Military Police Corps Regiment and provides Army military
police commanders, staffs, and Soldiers at all levels a foundation for conducting security and mobility support
during Army operations. To fully understand the doctrine in this manual, readers must first grasp the concept
of multidomain operations as described in ADP 3-0. Additionally, readers should be familiar with the
protection principles discussed in ADP 3-37, the fundamentals of the operations process found in
ADP 5-0, the principles of command and control detailed in ADP 6-0, the Army profession and moral
principles outlined in ADP 6-22, the conduct of Army operations described in FM 3-0, and military police
operations covered in FM 3-39.

The primary audience for ATP 3-39.30 is military police commanders and staffs; however, all members of
the military may use this manual to understand the capabilities and tasks of military police. Commanders and
staffs of joint task forces or multinational headquarters should also consult relevant joint or multinational
doctrines related to various military operations and joint or multinational forces. Additionally, trainers and
educators throughout the Army will find this manual useful.

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates must ensure that their decisions and actions comply with applicable
United States, international, and, in some cases, host-nation (HN) laws and regulations. Commanders at all
levels are responsible for ensuring that their Soldiers operate according to the law of war and the rules of
engagement (ROE). See FM 6-27 for additional information.

ATP 3-39.30 uses joint terms where applicable. Selected joint and Army terms and definitions are included
in the glossary and the main text. Terms for which ATP 3-39.30 serves as the proponent publication (the
authority) are italicized in the text and marked with an asterisk (*) in the glossary. Terms and definitions for
which ATP 3‑39.30 is the proponent publication are boldfaced in the text. Other definitions found within the
text will have the term italicized, followed by the number of the proponent publication.

ATP 3-39.30 applies to the Active Army, Army National Guard/Army National Guard of the United States
and United States Army Reserve unless otherwise stated.

The proponent of ATP 3-39.30 is the United States Army Military Police School (USAMPS). The preparing
agency is the Fielded Force Integration Directorate, Doctrine Division, Maneuver Support Center of
Excellence. Send comments and recommendations on Department of the Army (DA) Form 2028
(Recommended Changes to Publications and Blank Forms) to Director, FFID, Doctrine Division, MSCoE,
ATTN: ATZT-FFD, 14000 MSCoE Loop, Suite 270, Fort Leonard Wood, MO 65473-8929; by email to
usarmy.leonardwood.mscoe.mbx.mpdoc@army.mil; or submit an electronic DA Form 2028.

                              Introduction

ATP 3-39.30 focuses on the military police discipline of security and mobility support. It outlines how
military police assist Army, joint, and multinational forces by carrying out tactical tasks and activities related
to security and mobility. ATP 3-39.30 serves as a foundation for military police commanders, staffs, and
Soldiers to effectively conduct security and mobility support across various military operations. The primary
emphasis is on the tactical tasks and activities facilitated by military police technical capabilities that enable
commanders to generate, project, and maintain combat power. ATP 3-39.30 describes the role of military
police in protecting both the force and noncombatants, and how they help ensure that the commander’s
freedom of action is preserved.

The security and mobility support discipline is enhanced by and contributes to the other military police
disciplines, specifically police operations and detention operations. Each of these disciplines is viewed
through the lens of policing or corrections and is guided by the overarching function of police intelligence.
Police intelligence operations (PIO) assist commanders at all levels by integrating police intelligence
activities into all military police operations. These disciplines represent interdependent areas of expertise
within the Military Police Corps Regiment.

The tasks in this discipline focus on military police operations typically carried out in a tactical environment.
While military police are primarily responsible for many of these tasks, other members of the combined arms
team may also perform some of them. Military police engaged in security and mobility support tasks usually
serve in a support role. These tasks primarily focus on applying military police combat power to support the
movement and maneuver and protection warfighting functions; however, security and mobility tasks also
support the conduct of populace and resources control (PRC).

Military police play a crucial role in security and mobility support tasks within the support area, making them
vital for the sustainment warfighting function. Their ability to provide security and mobility support is
enhanced by the technical tasks associated with the military police disciplines of police operations and
detention operations. These disciplines and the integrating function of PIO are discussed in detail in other
military police manuals and will be referenced when relevant to discussions on security and mobility support.
ATP 3-39.30 includes significant changes. It—

     • Discusses military police security and mobility support to Army operations during competition
          below armed conflict, crisis, and armed conflict.

     • Includes additional discussion on military police security and mobility support throughout the
          operational framework.

     • Introduces discussion on counter-unmanned aircraft systems (C-UASs).
     • Includes additional discussion on support to security operations.
     • Includes additional discussion on dislocated civilian (DC) operations.
     • Introduces an appendix on battlefield confinement of U.S. military prisoners.

This ATP is divided into five chapters and five appendices:
     • Chapter 1 describes the military police discipline of security and mobility support and how it is
          affected by and affects the operational environment. While focused on the movement and maneuver
          and protection warfighting functions, this discipline finds application among other warfighting
          functions and provides support across the range of military operations.
     • Chapter 2 examines how military police conduct reconnaissance and surveillance to collect
          information and monitor avenues of approach, potential landing zones (LZ), and drop zones.
     • Chapter 3 focuses on military police support to mobility operations as part of the movement and
          maneuver warfighting function.
     • Chapter 4 focuses on military police support to security operations as part of the protection
          warfighting function. Military police security and mobility tasks and activities associated with
          security operations are focused on area and local security and associated tasks and activities.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  vii

Introduction

          • Chapter 5 describes how military police support to PRC can minimize civilian (CIV) interference
                in military operations while also ensuring the security of DCs. The chapter further describes how
                security and mobility facilitates resources control. Resources control regulates the movement or
                consumption of material resources, mobilizes material resources, and denies materiel to the enemy.
                Resources controls target specific sectors of the material wealth and economy of a nation, including
                natural resources, food and agriculture, immoveable property, finances, and cultural and critical
                infrastructure.

          • Appendix A describes how assessment is an essential, continuous activity that occurs throughout
                the operations process and how assessment assists commanders and staffs in comparing forecasted
                outcomes to events to determine the effectiveness of security and mobility support tasks.

          • Appendix B discusses the options for DC camps and the role of military police in this portion of
                PRC.

          • Appendix C provides an overview of battlefield confinement of U.S. military prisoners.
          • Appendix D describes the route classification system and the types of route signing.
          • Appendix E provides fundamental considerations, formats, and instructions for developing the

                security and mobility support (area security, physical security, antiterrorism [AT], and PRC
                appendixes).

                                                 Chapter 1

      Security and Mobility Support to Army Operations

    Military police support for Army operations is complex and requires a thorough understanding
    of the operational environment, the commander’s intent, the concept of operations, and the
    capabilities and limitations of military police in these operations. Military police assist each of
    the Army warfighting functions while performing their three main disciplines: police
    operations, detention operations, and security and mobility support. Through these disciplines,
    military police units offer commanders a range of customizable and focused capabilities across
    various military operations. The security and mobility support discipline emphasizes protecting
    both the force and noncombatants while preserving the commander’s freedom of action. This
    chapter explains how security and mobility support enable mobility, security, and PRC for the
    joint force commander. Further details on all military police disciplines and their relationships
    to defense, offense, and stability are in FM 3-39.

SECURITY AND MOBILITY SUPPORT DISCIPLINE

     1-1. The security and mobility support discipline primarily involves the technical capabilities and tactical
     tasks of military police that support mobility operations (a focus on movement over maneuver). Security
     responsibilities concentrate on area and local security, which includes key tasks such as AT, physical security,
     logistics security (LOGSEC), and PRC. This includes managing the safety and security of DCs,
     noncombatant evacuations, and the protection of resources and infrastructure. Mobility operations are
     integral to the movement and maneuver warfighting function, while security tasks belong to or reinforce the
     protection warfighting function.

     1-2. The security and mobility support discipline works in conjunction with other military police disciplines,
     including police operations and detention operations. Each of these disciplines is enhanced by the role of
     PIO. PIO supports commanders at all levels by integrating police intelligence activities into all military police
     operations. For more detailed information on PIO, see ATP 3-39.20.

     1-3. During security and mobility support, military police units provide essential protection to command
     headquarters for equipment and services that are critical for mission success, as directed by the joint force
     commander or the geographic combatant commander (CCDR). The missions and tasks categorized under the
     security and mobility support discipline apply across the full spectrum of military operations, which includes
     offensive, defensive, and stability operations as outlined in FM 3-39. While many of these missions and
     subordinate tasks can also be carried out by other members of the combined arms team, they are often best
     performed by military police. This is particularly true in support areas and along ground lines of
     communication (GLOC) throughout the area of operations (AO), especially as operations transition from
     large-scale combat to competition or cooperation.

     1-4. Military police have specialized technical skills and capabilities that are developed through training,
     education, and hands-on experience gained from policing and corrections missions associated with the police
     operations and detention operations disciplines. See table 1-1, page 2, for major subtasks that are associated
     with security and mobility support.

ASSURED MOBILITY FRAMEWORK

     1-5. Assured mobility is a framework—of processes, actions, and capabilities—that assures the ability of a
     force to deploy, move, and maneuver where and when desired (ATP 3-90.4). The fundamentals of assured
     mobility (predict, detect, prevent, avoid, neutralize, and protect) support implementing the assured mobility
     framework. This framework is designed to help a force achieve the commander’s intent. Assured mobility
     focuses on proactive mobility and countermobility (and supporting protection) actions and integrates them
     to accomplish its goals. While it is primarily associated with the movement and maneuver warfighting
     function, assured mobility also connects to each warfighting function through complementary and reinforcing
     capabilities. The principles of assured mobility are outlined in ATP 3-90.4.

     1-6. The assured mobility framework is utilized at the theater strategic, operational, and tactical levels of
     warfare to facilitate freedom of action for the joint force and to provide options for the joint force commander.
     While engineers serve as the principal staff integrator for assured mobility, other staff sections also play
     essential roles in effectively implementing mobility, countermobility, and related protection tasks. For
     example, they may be responsible for regulating traffic in an AO or managing DCs to facilitate movement
     and maneuver. The senior engineer staff officer's role within the assured mobility framework involves
     integrating the various warfighting functions. Military police commanders and provost marshals (PMs)
     recognize and use this framework to assist in planning and executing missions performed as part of support
     to security and security support.

     1-7. The assured mobility framework consists of a continuous cycle of planning, preparing, executing, and
     assessing operations. It relies on six fundamental principles (Predict, Detect, Prevent, Avoid, Neutralize,
     Protect) that allow friendly forces to move and maneuver while preventing the enemy from doing the same.
     Commanders and their staffs implement assured mobility by applying these six fundamentals throughout the
     planning and integration processes. The military police focus within the fundamentals of assured mobility
     includes:

          • Predict. This fundamental involves incorporating police intelligence into the intelligence
                preparation of the operational environment (IPOE). It is essential for planners to recognize that
                combat situations (or natural or human-made disasters) can turn the local populace into obstacles to
                movement. Planners must assess what assets (military police and other) will be necessary to manage
                DCs, stragglers, and other issues related to mobility and security.

          • Detect. Military police play a unique role in the collection plan. Unlike many other collection assets,
                they approach situations from a policing perspective, which allows them to offer valuable insights
                into the synchronization and integration of surveillance assets and reconnaissance missions. Due to
                their training and experience in law enforcement, military police can help inform commander’s
                critical information requirement (CCIR) that might be overlooked by other forces.

          • Prevent. This fundamental ensures the unhindered movement of friendly forces while denying the
                adversary the ability to disrupt it. This encompasses mitigating disruptions caused by CIV activities
                within the AO. Understanding and monitoring CIV movement patterns are crucial. Proactive
                measures like patrolling help identify and address potential threats before they impact operations.
                This includes detecting enemy activity, such as improvised explosive device (IED) emplacements
                and ambush sites, and recognizing and mitigating potential hazards posed by CIV presence. By
                proactively managing the CIV factor, we enhance our movement control and traffic flow,
                safeguarding friendly freedom of maneuver.

25 March 2026                ATP 3-39.30                                    3


---

Chapter 1

          • Avoid. This fundamental is used if prevention fails; it includes the development of branches and
                sequels to plans to avoid impediments to mobility (including traffic congestion, crowds, and DCs).
                Adjusting movement and traffic control plans and rerouting traffic may be necessary to prevent or
                mitigate the effects of interference or obstacles.

          • Neutralize. CIV interference (traffic congestion, crowds, and DCs) is mitigated through military
                police, civil affairs (CA), and other assets and capabilities. This may include supporting civil
                administration and ensuring public safety and transportation. Key actions involve managing civil
                disturbances and crowd control, establishing roadblocks and obstacles, creating alternate routes, and
                developing bypasses (including tactical bridging) to improve traffic flow. Additionally, there may
                be efforts to persuade the public to avoid certain areas during specific times, provide information to
                enable a controlled response to catastrophic events, and counter adversarial attempts to manipulate
                the population.

          • Protect. Protection is a key component of assured mobility. Protection tasks that support assured
                mobility involve facilitating trafficability and movement through a designated AO; safeguarding
                critical sites such as bridges on main supply routes (MSRs); and establishing convoy support centers,
                patrol bases, or other critical locations along MSRs, routes, or road networks. Military police should
                also consider the applications of countermobility and survivability in the context of area and local
                security.

OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENT

     1-8. Within the broader strategic security environment, Army forces conduct operations in specific
     operational environments. An operational environment is the aggregate of the conditions, circumstances, and
     influences that affect the employment of capabilities and bear on the decisions of the commander (JP 3-0).
     For Army forces, an operational environment includes areas within the land, maritime, air, space, and
     cyberspace domains, which are analyzed through three dimensions (human, physical, and information). The
     land, maritime, air, and space domains are characterized by their physical characteristics, while cyberspace
     (being a man-made network) connects the other domains (see figure 1-1). For more information on the
     domains and dimensions within an operational environment, see FM 3-0.

                 Figure 1-1. Domains and dimensions of an operational environment

   1-9. The operational environment model helps account for all of the factors, specific circumstances, and
   conditions that influence operations. This understanding allows leaders to better identify problems, anticipate
   potential outcomes, and comprehend the effects of various friendly or threat actions on achieving military
   objectives. A thorough understanding of the operational environment is crucial for executing successful

4  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                               Security and Mobility Support to Army Operations

     operations. Commanders must recognize and appreciate the evolving nature of an operational environment,
     which encompasses local variables and the interplay of global or regional influences, including political,
     social (such as crime), and economic factors that affect conditions and operations.

     1-10. Commanders typically consult specialists across various fields to gain a comprehensive understanding
     of the operational environment. Military police are among these specialists, contributing essential insights
     that enhance the overall understanding of the situation. The connection between criminal activity and
     irregular threats has become increasingly intertwined. Criminal elements are now regarded as a subcategory
     of irregular threats, as their organizational structures, tactics, and activities overlap those of various types of
     irregular threats. Most criminal activity occurs in densely populated urban areas, where disorder and fear of
     physical or financial harm impact CIV populations. This can marginalize security forces, destabilize
     governments, and undermine military operations. The skills and technical abilities developed through
     policing and corrections missions provide military police a unique perspective on the operational
     environment, allowing them to view it through the lens of law enforcement and correctional practices.

     1-11. Irregular and criminal threats persist in attacking, manipulating, exploiting, and intimidating vulnerable
     and frustrated populations. These actions aim to discredit governments, gain power and influence, create
     disorder and instability, and generate illicit profits. Crime, disorder, and the fear of crime persist as
     debilitating factors that contribute to instability, particularly in densely populated urban areas and in weak,
     failing, or failed states. For more information on criminal threats, see FM 3-39.

CONTESTED LOGISTICS ENVIRONMENT

     1-12. A contested logistics environment is an environment in which the armed forces engage in conflict with
     an adversary that presents challenges in all domains and directly targets logistics operations, facilities and
     activities in the United States, abroad, or in transit from one location to another (10 USC § 2926[h]). In this
     environment, military police commanders and staffs must plan and execute support to logistics, operating
     under the assumption that sustainment forces are constantly monitored, engaged, and contested.

     1-13. Military police planners should recognize that sustainment forces will be a primary target for enemy
     actions across all domains, making them vulnerable to detection, targeting, and destruction. To enhance their
     survival on the battlefield, sustainment forces must be mobile and operate in a dispersed manner, which adds
     complexity to their protection and security. Given the limitations of available protection assets, logistics and
     military police planners must identify and prioritize critical sustainment items and missions for protection.
     To reduce the risk from enemy long-range fire, special purpose forces, and other threats, planners must
     strategize and prepare for opportune moments to effectively move logistics. The role of the military police
     in protecting logistics starts at forts, camps, aerial ports of debarkation, seaports of debarkation, and other
     essential sites at which U.S. forces can organize and project forces. Throughout the logistics process—
     moving from fort to port, port to port, and port to theater—military police must coordinate with local, state,
     federal, and HN authorities to address deployment challenges and meet security requirements.

     1-14. Logistics are a priority target for enemy forces and will be challenged across all domains to disrupt our
     ability to sustain Army operations. Now more than ever, military police forces must plan to protect logistics—
     from the initial points of force projection to the logistics release points, where sustainment is distributed to
     maneuver forces. For more information on the contested logistics environment, see FM 4-0.

SUPPORT TO ARMY OPERATIONS

     1-15. The Army conducts operations across multiple domains and the information environment. An
     operation is a sequence of tactical actions with a common purpose or unifying theme (JP 1). Army tactical
     formations typically operate within a context dominated by a single strategic relationship at a time. Army
     doctrine outlines the strategic situation through three contexts that Army forces conduct their operations:

          • Competition below armed conflict.
          • Crisis.
          • Armed conflict.

---

Chapter 1

     1-16. Competition below armed conflict occurs when two or more state or nonstate adversaries have
     incompatible interests, yet neither seeks to engage in armed conflict. Nation-states compete with one another
     by using all available instruments of national power to gain and maintain advantages that help them achieve
     their objectives. Low levels of lethal force can be involved in this competition. Adversaries often utilize
     cyberspace capabilities and information warfare to damage or disrupt infrastructure, interfere with
     government processes, and conduct operations in a manner that does not provoke a military response from
     the United States or its allies. This competition allows military forces to prepare for potential armed conflict,
     provides opportunities to reassure allies and partners of their commitment, and creates the necessary time
     and space to prevent crises or conflicts from escalating.

     1-17. A crisis is an emerging incident or situation involving a possible threat to the United States, its citizens,
     military forces, or vital interests that develops rapidly and creates a condition of such diplomatic, economic,
     or military importance that commitment of military forces and resources is contemplated to achieve national
     and/or strategic objectives (JP 3-0). Commanders must consider that taking overt military action could
     escalate a crisis into armed conflict. Instead, utilizing space and cyberspace capabilities offers alternatives
     that are less likely to provoke, such an escalation. It is important to note that a crisis is relative to the adversary
     involved, which differs from a crisis response, typically triggered by natural or human-made disasters. During
     a crisis, armed conflict has not yet broken out, but it is either imminent or a distinct possibility, necessitating
     a rapid response from forces that are prepared to engage if deterrence fails.

     1-18. Armed conflict occurs when a state or nonstate actor primarily uses lethal force to pursue its interests.
     This conflict can take many forms, from irregular warfare to conventional warfare or a combination of both.
     In the context of irregular warfare, proxy warfare represents a significant threat. The decision to enter or
     terminate armed conflict is fundamentally a political one. Military forces may enter a conflict with advanced
     warning during a prolonged crisis or with little warning during periods of competition. Ultimately, the
     readiness of military forces for armed conflict is determined by the decisions and preparations made during
     competition and crisis.

     1-19. Multidomain operations is the combined arms employment of joint and Army capabilities to create and
     exploit relative advantages to achieve objectives, defeat enemy forces, and consolidate gains on behalf of
     joint force commanders (ADP 3-0). In large-scale combat operations, military police play a crucial role in
     helping Army forces defeat enemy organizations, control terrain, protect CIV populations, and preserve their
     own forces. They achieve this by providing complementary and reinforcing capabilities through three key
     disciplines (police operations, detention operations, and security and mobility support). Large-scale combat
     operations are extensive joint combat operations in terms of scope and size of forces committed, conducted
     as a campaign aimed at achieving operational and strategic objectives (ADP 3-0). Army forces attack, defend,
     conduct stability tasks, and consolidate gains to attain national objectives.

     1-20. During large-scale combat operations, commanders focus on seizing, retaining, and exploiting the
     initiative. This involves coordinating multiple unit actions in highly challenging operational environments.
     The scale, scope, speed, and lethality of large-scale combat intensify the dynamic and uncertain nature of
     war, making it difficult to accurately determine cause-and-effect relationships. Military police are crucial in
     helping Army forces defeat enemy organizations, control territory, protect populations, and sustain the joint
     force. They achieve this by conducting tasks across all three military police disciplines (police operations,
     detention operations, and security and mobility support). For more information on large-scale combat
     operations, see FM 3-0.

     1-21. During armed conflict, military police play a crucial role in protecting forces, CIV populations, and
     critical infrastructure. They utilize manned and unmanned teams, including military working dog (MWD)
     teams, to conduct reconnaissance and surveillance of bases, routes, facilities, and storage sites. Their mission
     is to detect, deter, defeat, and delay enemy forces, criminals, irregular threats, and conventional forces that
     have been bypassed.

6  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026

                                                                               Security and Mobility Support to Army Operations

     1-22. Military police, in coordination with domestic and foreign law enforcement authorities, joint partners,
     and Army counterintelligence field offices, enhance their security and mobility support operations to protect
     power projection platforms. This ensures that friendly forces can effectively conduct movement and
     maneuver operations. In addition to these efforts, military police perform physical security inspections, crime
     prevention assessments, and vulnerability assessments. These assessments help evaluate the effectiveness of
     current security measures and identify potential gaps in security posture. Based on these inspections and
     assessments, military police provide unit commanders and facility managers police intelligence assessments
     bolstered by biometric and forensic data. This information is crucial for strengthening crime prevention,
     physical security, and AT measures.

     1-23. Consolidate gains are activities to make enduring any initial operational success and to set the
     conditions for a sustainable security environment, allowing for a transition of control to other legitimate
     authorities (ADP 3-0). Consolidating gains is an integral and continuous part of competition, essential for
     success in various military operations. Effective consolidation requires a realistic assessment of strategic
     conditions, the legitimacy of allies and partners, the relative advantages of friendly forces and adversaries,
     and the sustainability of a political outcome. Military police supporting the consolidation of gains should
     focus on the six primary stability tasks as the security situation stabilizes. For more details on consolidating
     gains, see FM 3-0.

     1-24. Military police carry out missions as part of an integrated combined arms effort. Although some tasks
     may be performed solely within the scope of a functional unit, all missions must align with and support the
     overall goals of the combined arms effort. The technical capabilities and tactical tasks involved in security
     and mobility support can be utilized across a wide range of military operations. Major operations, crisis
     responses, and contingency operations may involve close combat. These operations depend on integrating
     military police and other supporting elements to facilitate the movement and maneuver (mobility) of friendly
     forces while restricting the freedom of action of adversaries (countermobility).

SUPPORT DURING COMPETITION BELOW ARMED CONFLICT

     1-25. Operations during competition below armed conflict involve security cooperation and military
     engagement, including training and operations to enhance the cohesion and large-scale combat readiness of
     the United States, its allies, and partners. U.S. forces, allies, and partners conduct these operations with
     forward-stationed forces to promote mutual interests. Regionally aligned and engaged Army forces are
     crucial for achieving objectives that strengthen the global network of multinational partners and help prevent
     conflict.

     1-26. Army operations conducted during competition below armed conflict encompass various activities to
     promote regional stability and create conditions for a favorable outcome of a military confrontation. These
     operations are designed to deter adversarial actions, prepare for armed conflict under advantageous
     circumstances if deterrence fails, and shape the operational environment alongside allies and partners to align
     with U.S. strategic interests and policy objectives. Military police commanders, PMs, and planners must
     understand common adversary methods and objectives to effectively plan, prepare, and execute military
     police capabilities to support Army operations. Military police play a crucial role in enabling competition
     below armed conflict through three military police disciplines.

     1-27. During competition below armed conflict, security and mobility support are crucial in facilitating long-
     term military engagements, security cooperation, and deterrence missions. These efforts aim to assure allies,
     enhance partner capacity and capability, and promote regional stability. Commanders, PMs, and military
     police planners must collaborate to synchronize, integrate, and organize military police capabilities and
     resources throughout operations. This coordination is essential for protecting U.S. interests and fostering
     partnerships. Security and mobility support during competitions below armed conflict include LOGSEC,
     which ensures the integrity of the logistics system. This is achieved by preventing, identifying, and
     investigating criminal acts committed by terrorists, criminal elements, or insider threats, ranging from U.S.
     Army logistics providers to military forces on the ground.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  7

Chapter 1

     1-28. During competition, some tasks may be initiated and continue through crisis depending on the
     commander’s requirements and mission variables, such as—

          • Protective-services operations to protect Service members, high-risk personnel (HRP), CIV
                employees, Family members, Department of Defense (DOD) facilities, information, and equipment.

          • Physical measures designed to safeguard personnel and prevent unauthorized access to equipment,
                installations, material, and documents, protecting them against espionage, sabotage, damage, and
                theft.

          • Area security to protect critical capabilities, assets, and activities within a specific area, preventing
                and mitigating threats from special forces, criminals, and irregular threats intended to harm friendly
                forces and disrupt deployment and sustainment operations.

          • Patrol and explosive detector dogs to detect hazards in and around areas, routes, and roadways.
          • PRC to protect CIV networks or groups identified by Army forces as critical in facilitating essential

                services and overall governance.
          • Reconnaissance and surveillance to disrupt adversary counterintelligence efforts and threats from

                special forces, criminals, and irregular threats that endanger friendly forces.
          • Checkpoints and roadblocks to control the movement of vehicles, personnel, and materiel,

                preventing illegal actions that may aid the enemy and deter terrorist activities and other threats.
          • Police intelligence activities to enhance situational understanding and provide a holistic common

                operational picture.
          • Traffic control plans to complement movement control and highway regulation, include

                implemented traffic enforcement measures, establishing speed control, conducting safety inspection
                checkpoints, and ensuring only authorized traffic uses controlled routes.
          • LOGSEC to ensure the integrity of the logistics system by preventing, identifying, and investigating
                criminal acts committed by terrorists, criminal elements, or insider threats affecting Army logistics
                providers and military forces.

SUPPORT DURING CRISIS

     1-29. Army operations during a crisis are activities aimed at deterring the undesired actions of an adversary.
     These operations are intended to prevent the adversary from exploiting advantages by increasing the potential
     costs of their continued aggression. Commanders typically concentrate on actions that protect friendly forces,
     assets, and partners while signaling the U.S. intent to carry out subsequent phases of a planned operation.

     1-30. Military police capabilities play a crucial role in supporting operations to prevent armed conflict during
     mobilization and during the transit of Army forces and cargo. They are essential along movement routes,
     initial staging areas, and subsequent assembly areas where uncertain threat conditions require a careful
     balance between ensuring protection and building combat power. Army operations to consolidate gains
     during a crisis should align with, stabilize, and support the civil authority phases of a joint operation.
     Commanders must continually assess the coordination, synchronization, and integration of military police
     capabilities necessary to consolidate these gains and achieve the desired end state. Military police contribute
     to crisis management through three distinct disciplines.

     1-31. During a crisis, commanders, PMs, and military police planners must work together to synchronize,
     integrate, and organize military police security and mobility support. This coordination is essential to enable
     friendly forces to deter undesirable actions from adversaries, protect U.S. interests, and strengthen
     partnerships and partner capacities. See paragraph 1-28, which outlines tasks that begin in operations during
     competition and continue throughout the crisis phase of an operation.

8  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026

                                                                               Security and Mobility Support to Army Operations

SUPPORT DURING ARMED CONFLICT AND LARGE-SCALE
COMBAT OPERATIONS

     1-32. Armed conflict refers to a strategic relationship where adversaries use lethal force as the primary
     method to achieve their objectives and impose their will on others. During armed conflict, military operations
     may involve a combination of irregular and conventional warfare. Irregular warfare includes methods such
     as counterinsurgency and unconventional warfare. Leaders apply the doctrine of large-scale combat
     operations in limited situations that necessitate conventional warfare strategies. For more information, see
     ADP 3-05 for irregular warfare, ATP 3-05.1 for unconventional warfare, and FM 3-24/MCWP 3-33.5 for
     counterinsurgency.

     1-33. Large-scale combat operations are the focus of Army readiness. These operations involve extensive
     joint combat activities characterized by the significant scope and size of forces involved and the high levels
     of destructive violence. Large-scale combat introduces complexities, lethality, ambiguity, and rapidity not
     typically found in other military operations. The fast pace of these operations can create gaps and seams,
     leading to opportunities and risks as enemy formations break apart or reposition.

     1-34. Large-scale combat operations typically occur in scenarios associated with state-on-state conflicts.
     These operations involve combat between large formations (such as divisions and corps) across all domains.
     A peer enemy engaged in large-scale combat can effectively challenge the joint forces. Their capabilities,
     including integrated air defense systems, long-range fire systems, cyberspace and electromagnetic warfare
     capabilities, weapons of mass destruction, and extensive surveillance and reconnaissance networks, can
     create a balance or near balance of power across all domains. Army forces operating as part of a joint force
     focus on defeating and destroying enemy ground forces during these large-scale combat operations. They
     continuously work to consolidate their gains to achieve objectives that support an effective political outcome.

     1-35. Support from military police during large-scale combat operations is complex. It requires a thorough
     understanding of the operational environment, the commander’s intent, the concept of operations, and the
     capabilities and limitations of military police. While the various operations involved in large-scale combat
     are discussed separately in the following paragraphs, they are executed simultaneously. The mission
     determines the significance of each operation in relation to the others. Military police planners must
     continually assess and anticipate changes in mission requirements and the necessary military police
     capabilities as operations transition between phases or as conditions or the commander’s priorities change
     within the operational environment.

     1-36. In support of large-scale combat operations, PIO are conducted as an integral part of all military police
     missions. During both offensive and defensive operations, police intelligence helps staff identify
     organizations and networks—both police and criminal—within the AO. This identification can provide early
     warnings of disruptive activities and conditions that require corrective actions to establish stability. As
     operations become more prolonged and conventional threats diminish, transitioning to hybrid threats, the
     distinctions between criminal, terrorist, and insurgent activities often associated with stability operations
     become less clear. Police intelligence operations, when planned and integrated by military police staff and
     PM sections, contribute to the overall operations process and enhance the commander's situational awareness.
     This integration is critical during stability operations, where the role of the military police centers on
     developing the ability of a country to protect its communities and establish or reestablish civil security and
     control. For more detailed information on PIO, see ATP 3-39.20.

     1-37. Security and mobility support are essential functions of military police that facilitate movement and
     maneuver on the battlefield. They provide mobility support to conserve the combat power of maneuver forces
     and ensure efficient movement throughout the AO. By managing the movement of forces, military police
     enhance the momentum of the force, optimizing the use of space and time to achieve speed and mass while
     hindering enemy maneuvers. By improving maneuverability, military police accelerate the concentration of
     combat power, which is vital for increasing the velocity, tempo, and operational reach of the forces necessary
     to exploit critical vulnerabilities in the enemy. Operational reach is the distance and duration across which
     a force can successfully employ military capabilities (JP 3-0). Military police are trained, equipped, and
     organized to neutralize enemy harassment attacks in the rear area. This capability allows them to prevent the
     diversion of maneuver forces, thereby preserving combat power for the essential tasks and purposes of an
     operation.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  9

Chapter 1

     1-38. Several security and mobility support tasks are directly related to the protection aspect of the
     warfighting function. In an operational environment, the protection scheme includes safeguarding personnel
     (U.S. forces, unified action partners, and noncombatants) and critical assets. This involves safeguarding
     bases, base camps, and other essential fixed sites, while also securing routes and ensuring the safety of forces
     operating within sustainment areas. Protection considerations include supporting battle positions, combat
     outposts, and vital HN infrastructure. On modern battlefields, commanders must be well-versed in
     survivability tactics and techniques to ensure protection. For additional information on protection, see
     ADP 3-37.

     1-39. Security and mobility support operations involve distributing military police forces throughout the AO.
     These forces conduct aggressive patrolling and surveillance to protect military units, critical facilities, and
     HRP while ensuring the security and control of CIV populations. During these patrols, military police
     Soldiers interact with various friendly units, CIVs, and governmental and nongovernmental organizations
     (NGOs). The information gathered from these interactions—whether passively or actively—significantly
     contributes to the overall information collection effort. In addition to collecting information, security and
     mobility support operations often promote stability, as PRC fosters lawfulness and order. Police operations
     (see ATP 3-39.10) and the associated skills and capabilities inherent to these functions are the fundamental
     basis for how military police operations are structured and executed.

     1-40. Planning is crucial for effectively utilizing military police combat power. Understanding how the
     security and mobility support discipline and its tasks and capabilities align with the elements of defense,
     offense, and stability helps planners organize military police forces to achieve mission success. Swiftly
     transitioning between defense, offense, and stability is vital. Military police planners provide essential
     planning considerations to the supported commander and staff at every level. They also advise on military
     police capabilities, methods of employment, and limitations. For further details on military police
     participation in the planning process, see FM 3-39.

SECURITY AND MOBILITY SUPPORT TO DEFENSE

     1-41. A defensive operation is an operation to defeat an enemy attack, gain time, and develop conditions
     favorable for offensive or stability operations (ADP 3-0). Defensive operations are designed to counter enemy
     offensive actions. Their primary goal is to defeat enemy attacks by destroying as many attacking forces as
     possible. Additionally, these operations focus on retaining territory, protecting CIV populations, and
     safeguarding critical assets against enemy strikes. Defensive operations can also buy time and conserve
     resources, allowing offensive actions to be executed in other areas. While a defense alone typically cannot
     secure a decisive victory, it can set the stage for a counteroffensive operation, enabling Army forces to regain
     the initiative. Moreover, defensive operations can create a protective barrier that allows stability tasks to be
     carried out effectively. For more detailed information on defensive operations, see FM 3-90.

     1-42. Defensive operations are categorized into mobile defense, area defense, and retrograde. The main goal
     of these defensive efforts is to create conditions that enable a counteroffensive, allowing Army forces to
     regain the initiative. Other reasons for performing defensive tasks include—

          • Retaining decisive terrain or denying a critical area to the enemy.
          • Attriting or fixing the enemy as a precursor to offensive operations.
          • Countering enemy action.
          • Increasing the vulnerability of the enemy by compelling their commander to concentrate subordinate

                forces.

     1-43. Military police units that support defense operations perform various tasks related to the security and
     mobility support discipline. Military police leaders conducting security and mobility support tasks supporting
     the defense must—

          • Exercise disciplined initiative while operating within the commander's intent.
          • Anticipate operational changes or transitions and prepare the military police efforts accordingly.
          • Conduct security and mobility support to aid a force in maneuvering and massing. Military police

                should anticipate transitions from defense to offense and assist in the movement of reserves or
                reaction forces.
          • Provide security and mobility support to prevent enemy reconnaissance elements from gathering
                intelligence among the CIV population regarding the location of defending forces. Military police

                                                                         Security and Mobility Support to Army Operations

          units must be positioned strategically to control key terrain and enhance the defensive capabilities
          of bases and base clusters.
     • Deny threat elements, including special forces, criminals, and irregular threats, the opportunity to
          attack or take actions that could harm friendly forces or disrupt sustainment operations.
     • Prevent the theft of military equipment and supplies by criminals or other threat actors.
     • Mitigate, resolve, and prevent traffic congestion, accidents, and other safety-related issues along
          MSRs and alternate supply routes (ASRs).
     • Target threat actors, groups, organizations, and networks to prevent their ability to cause harm to
          forces, populations, and critical infrastructure. This will help drive disorder and instability while
          ensuring freedom of action.
     • Understand how operations impact security functions in a support area, in a joint security area, or
          along lines of communication (LOC) and how these operations protect vital command, control, and
          sustainment infrastructures or systems.
     • Know the location and composition of probable response forces or tactical combat forces (TCFs) to
          effectively coordinate and assist in securing the support area or joint security area.
     • Protect sustainment resources while facilitating the lateral, forward, and rearward movement of
          combat forces.
     • Coordinate the security and treatment of DCs in collaboration with HN or multinational forces.
     • Understand the IPOE process, the CCIR, and priority intelligence requirements. This knowledge
          will help integrate police intelligence activities to support those requirements.
     • Consider the type and size of the AO, the security requirements of the LOC, and the threat level to
          plan for detainee operations and the management of DCs. Assess how their presence may impact
          the movement of forces.

1-44. Successful military police operations in defense rely on the understanding of the leader of the
commander’s intent and the effective use of military police capabilities and assets. In all three types of
defensive operations, the military police force focuses on supporting the movement of repositioned or
counterattacking forces and assisting in evacuating captured or detained individuals. Defensive missions
require a concentrated effort to ensure freedom of movement for repositioning forces and reserves when they
are committed. These units are given priority of movement along MSRs. Additional activities in defense
include protecting sustainment activities, which encompasses critical headquarters, communication facilities,
convoys, and supply sites. Examples of typical missions include—

     • Securing the movement of resources to ensure that commanders have the necessary forces, supplies,
          and equipment to support defensive operations.

     • Conducting area security operations, including response force operations, to detect, deter, and defeat
          Level I and Level II threats while delaying Level III threats within the AO. See table 4-1, page 76,
          for more details on Level I–III threats.

     • Conducting convoy escorts.
     • Supporting PRC to minimize the impact of DCs on combat forces.
     • Integrating patrol and explosive detection dogs to clear routes and roadways of hazards.

1-45. Similar to offensive operations, military police units prepare for defensive operations by integrating
and coordinating their efforts with supported units. They coordinate with AO and base camp commanders to
establish effective command and support relationships. The PM or military police commander at the
appropriate echelon organizes military police capabilities to support these defensive operations. This
preparation may involve implementing protection control measures. The activities of military police units are
conducted near and are carefully aligned with the preparations of maneuver forces. Figure 1-2, page 12,
illustrates various types of military police support, such as area security, MWD support, response force
operations, LOC security, and PRC in defense scenarios.

SECURITY AND MOBILITY SUPPORT TO OFFENSE

    1-46. The main objective of offensive operations is to defeat or destroy enemy forces while gaining control
    over terrain, resources, or population centers. (For more information on offensive and security operations,
    see FM 3-90.) The military police discipline of security and mobility support primarily focuses on capabilities
    that facilitate movement and maneuver (mobility operations), provide protection (security operations), and
    support PRC.

12                                  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

               Security and Mobility Support to Army Operations

1-47. The types of offensive operations outline the general sequence of the offense and recommended
formations. Each type of offensive operation serves a distinct purpose (see FM 3-0 for more information on
the purpose of each type). The four types of offensive operations are:

     • Movement to contact.
     • Attack.
     • Exploitation.
     • Pursuit.

1-48. Military police units supporting offensive operations perform various tasks within the security and
mobility support discipline. (For information on military police disciplines related to police operations and
detention operations, refer to ATP 3-39.10, FM 3-39, FM 3-63, and other relevant military police doctrines.)
Military police leaders conducting security and mobility support tasks during offensive operations must—

     • Exercise disciplined initiative in alignment with the commander’s intent.
     • Anticipate that selected elements of the offensive force may pause, defend, resupply, or reconstitute

          while other forces continue to attack.
     • Anticipate changes in the tempo of operations and adapt the military police effort accordingly.
     • Understand how operations impact security functions in a joint security area or along LOC, ensuring

          the protection of vital command and control and sustainment infrastructure or systems.
     • Consider the type and size of the AO, the security requirements for LOC, potential threats, and the

          plans for detainee operations, including how DC may impact the movement of forces.
     • Understand the IPOE process, the CCIR, and related information needs, and integrate police

          intelligence activities into all military police operations to support these requirements.
     • Coordinate the care and security of DCs with HN or multinational forces during PRC.
     • Assist the commander in expediting the deployment of forces to support the main effort and improve

          overall trafficability.
     • Anticipate a transition to a pursuit or exploitation by positioning military police forces to support

          follow-on units.
     • Support the movement of maneuver forces, ensuring their ability to concentrate and maintain

          momentum. Military police also protect command nodes, such as the main command post and the
          tactical command post. The security and mobility support tasks help coordinate efforts to
          concentrate forces and sustain the offensive operation.
     • Conduct reconnaissance and surveillance according to the commander’s information collection plan
          and perform counterreconnaissance to neutralize, disrupt, or defeat enemy reconnaissance forces
          attempting to gather intelligence on the protected forces.
     • Know the location and composition of probable response forces or TCFs to coordinate and assist in
          securing the support areas and the joint security area.

1-49. Military police missions related to security and mobility support during offensive operations typically
include—

     • Securing resource movement by ensuring that commanders receive the necessary forces, supplies,
          and equipment to support offensive operations.

     • Conducting area security by detecting, deterring, and defeating Level I and Level II threats while
          delaying Level III threats operating within the AO by—
           Disrupting and countering threat intelligence agents, special forces, criminals, and irregular
                threats involved in reconnaissance and surveillance of friendly forces.
           Denying the abilities of threat special forces, criminals, and irregular threats to attack or
                undertake actions that could harm friendly forces or disrupt deployment and sustainment
                operations.
           Preventing the theft of military equipment and supplies by criminals or other threat actors.
           Mitigating, resolving, and preventing traffic congestion, accidents, and other safety-related
                issues along MSRs and ASRs.
           Targeting threat actors, groups, organizations, and networks to prevent their ability to cause
                harm to forces, populations, and critical infrastructure; drive disorder and instability; and
                impede freedom of action.

     • Conducting security and mobility support tasks, such as aiding in gap crossings (including river
          crossings), the passage of lines, breaching operations, securing convoys, and HRP security.

          • Executing noncombatant evacuation operations (NEO), which include enrolling and tracking
                individuals during the evacuation process.

          • Providing regulation and enforcement of main and ASRs to enable freedom of movement.
          • Supporting PRC to mitigate the impact of DCs on maneuvering forces.
          • Integrating police intelligence activities across military police operations to enhance situational

                awareness and create a holistic common operational picture.
          • Assisting in forcible-entry operations (planning of detainee operations).
          • Supporting cordon-and-search tasks (performing outer cordon security and managing detainees).
          • Integrating patrol and explosive detection dogs to identify hazards on routes and roadways.

     1-50. The PM or military police commander at the appropriate echelon integrates military police capabilities
     to support offensive operations (see FM 3-39 for more details on PM responsibilities). Military police units
     prepare for these operations by establishing early linkups with the maneuver units they support. As they
     prepare for offensive actions, military police units focus on conducting inspections, rehearsing combined
     arms maneuvers, facilitating the movement of combined arms forces into position for the attack, and
     managing the evacuation and control of captured and detained individuals. For more detailed information on
     offensive tasks and security operations, see FM 3-90.

     1-51. Military police units participate in combined arms exercises, specifically focusing on breaching and
     gap crossing operations. They conduct rehearsals for the breach, assault, and support forces. Preparation may
     involve establishing protection measures and holding areas for tactical units moving along MSRs to their
     assembly areas. When route and area clearance operations are expected, military police units join engineer;
     chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear (CBRN); explosive ordnance disposal (EOD); and other forces
     to conduct route reconnaissance, inspections, and clearance activities. The preparation activities of military
     police units are closely aligned with and integrated into the preparations of maneuver forces. Figure 1-3
     illustrates examples of the security and mobility support of the military police, including gap crossing
     assistance, area security, MWD support, LOC security, and PRC for offensive operations.

SECURITY AND MOBILITY SUPPORT TO STABILITY

1-52. Stability is an overarching term encompassing various military missions, tasks, and activities conducted
outside the United States. These efforts are coordinated with other national power instruments to maintain or
restore a safe and secure environment. Additionally, they aim to provide essential government services,
emergency infrastructure reconstruction, and humanitarian relief. (For more information, see ADP 3-07 and
JP 3-0.) A stability mechanism is the primary method through which friendly forces affect CIVs in order to
attain conditions that support establishing a lasting, stable peace (ADP 3-0). As with defeat mechanisms,
combining different stability mechanisms can create complementary and reinforcing effects that enhance the
overall effectiveness and efficiency of a mission more so than relying on a single mechanism. For
organizations assigned an AO, there will always be essential stability tasks that are either implied or explicitly
specified.

1-53. The four stability mechanisms are compel, control, influence, and support. Compel is the use or threat
of lethal force to establish control and dominance; effect behavioral change; or enforce compliance with
mandates, agreements, or civil authority. Control involves imposing civil order to maintain stability within a
given area. Influence is about altering the opinions, attitudes, and ultimately the behaviors of foreign
populations, whether they are friendly, neutral, adversarial, or hostile. This is accomplished by synchronizing
information-related capabilities, presence, and conduct. Finally, support focuses on establishing, reinforcing,
or creating the necessary conditions for the effective functioning of instruments of national power.

1-54. It is essential to maintain initiative during stability activities by pursuing objectives that address the
root causes of instability. The combination of tasks during these activities will vary depending on the
situation. Stability consists of six primary tasks: establishing civil security, establishing civil control,
restoring essential services, supporting governance, supporting economic and infrastructure development,
and conducting security cooperation. (For more detailed information, see FM 3-07.) The purposes of stability
are to—

     • Provide a secure environment.
     • Secure land areas.
     • Meet the critical needs of the populace.
     • Gain support for the HN government.
     • Shape the environment for interagency and HN success.

1-55. Planning and preparing for stability activities can be difficult due to the technical nature of the
requirements and the wide range of potential military police missions associated with them. Conducting an
early on-the-ground assessment is crucial for tailoring the military police force with the needed specialties
and resources. The findings from this assessment are communicated to planners to ensure that an adequate
military police unit promptly arrives in the AO. The military police force conducts continuous surveillance
and combat patrols to help further this associated assessment or survey to identify—

     • Basic security requirements and establish police intelligence within the AO.
     • The needs of the HN and necessary military police capabilities to address specific requirements.
     • Other special considerations that affect the military police force.

1-56. Military police capabilities can provide specialized technical support integrated into the CA plan,
mainly focusing on PRC. (For information on CA planning, see ATP 3-57.60.) This integration is especially
relevant for security and mobility support. Integration occurs through the operations process and is facilitated
by coordination between military police planners and the CA staff assigned to the supported command. The
Civil-Military Operations Center supports the CA staff at echelon, helping to achieve the integration of PRC
measures. (For more details on the Civil-Military Operations Center, see ATP 3-57.70.) For additional
information on CA operations and PRC, see ATP 3-57.10 and FM 3-57.

25 March 2026                         ATP 3-39.30                                      15


---

Chapter 1

     1-57. Military police tasks must be prioritized to maximize mission effectiveness. The specific discipline
     carried out depends on the needs of the supported commander and the availability of military police resources.
     The supported commander, taking into consideration the recommendations of the PM, establishes the
     priorities for military police operations. The PM frequently needs to prioritize tasks to ensure optimal military
     police support.

     1-58. Stability tasks typically require a longer duration compared to other offense and defense tasks.
     Consequently, the effort exerted by military police is initially very high but tends to decrease as the HN
     capabilities mature. Preparation activities involve assessing the level of the civil rule of law within policing
     and correctional services and identifying significant infrastructure projects. These projects may include
     constructing police stations, training centers, and correctional institutions. The highest priority projects might
     be carried out using general engineering resources, while other projects may compete for contingency funding
     and be executed through contracted services. Military police forces may engage in counterinsurgency
     operations as the security framework of the HN develops.

     1-59. Military police are uniquely suited for stability operations. Their skills and capabilities are essential for
     achieving the desired outcomes and transition criteria during stabilization efforts. They possess strong
     abilities in shooting, moving, and communicating but are trained to use judgment and resolve issues with the
     lowest level of force possible, according to the use-of-force continuum. Military police are trained to resort
     to deadly force only after all other options have been exhausted. Military police units must project a
     professional image of law enforcement and policing. This professional presence is vital for a force that needs
     significant capabilities while maintaining a low political profile.

     1-60. Military police can support stability tasks through all three disciplines. Conducting policing activities
     to support civil security and civil control efforts is critical for establishing the rule of law. PIO are integrated
     and continuously executed throughout all military police operations, which may involve establishing,
     utilizing, and transferring HN PIO capabilities. This typically includes a range of activities aimed at
     establishing or reestablishing the rule of law and preparing for the handover of responsibilities to an HN to
     carry out those same tasks. For more information on police operations and stability support, see
     ATP 3-39.10 and FM 3-39.

     1-61. Support for military police detention operations during stability activities with an HN is essential.
     Detainees must be effectively managed and transferred to HN police, who are appropriately trained and
     disciplined. Military police can aid stability operations by collaborating with the HN to establish or
     reestablish detention or correction systems. For more information on detainee operations and support for
     stability, see FM 3-39 and FM 3-63.

     1-62. Military police security and mobility support in stability is often associated with police and detention
     operations, but it also resembles tasks performed in support of offense and defense. The main objective of
     security and mobility tasks is to establish civil control and restore essential services. Figure 1-4, page 17,
     shows that security and mobility support encompass various activities, such as area security, MWD support,
     response force operations, LOC security, and PRC. Police operation and detention operation support are most
     likely to require activities and skills associated with security and mobility support, including the following:

          • Establishing, operating, and transitioning police stations. This involves transferring control to
                trained and skilled HN police, contributing to local and area security tasks as discussed in chapter 3
                on security operations.

          • Controlling the movement of DCs. This includes providing relief to those affected by crises. The
                topic of DC operations is part of PRC, which is addressed mainly in chapter 2 (mobility operations)
                and chapter 5 (DC operations).

          • Establishing and training regional and urban police patrol operations. This includes managing traffic
                control and emergency first responder operations, which are primarily related to mobility operations,
                as discussed in chapter 2.

          • Establishing indigenous highway patrol capabilities. This activity is also primarily linked to
                mobility operations, as explained in chapter 2.

          • Conducting facility security and protection efforts. This contributes to local and area security tasks,
                which are covered in detail in chapter 3 (security operations).

16  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                   Security and Mobility Support to Army Operations

• Establishing and supporting DC camps. This supports the commander’s civil-military operations
     (CMO) plan. Chapter 5 addresses the establishment of DC camps, which are further detailed in
     appendix B.

• Denying the abilities of criminal and irregular threats. This involves preventing these actors from
     attacking or carrying out actions intended to harm friendly forces or disrupt sustainment operations.

• Preventing the theft of military equipment and supplies. This aims to protect against theft by
     criminals or other threat actors.

• Mitigating traffic issues. Efforts focus on solving and preventing traffic congestion, accidents, and
     other safety-related problems along MSRs and ASRs.

• Targeting threat actors, groups, organizations, and networks. This strategy is designed to prevent
     their ability to harm forces, local populations, and critical infrastructure; drive disorder and
     instability; and impede freedom of action.
