25 March 2026                         ATP 3-39.30                                17


---

Chapter 1

Establish Civil Security

     1-63. Establishing civil security involves providing the safety of the HN and its population, including
     protection from internal and external threats. This is essential for creating a safe and secure environment.
     Civil security encompasses various activities, including restoring order and conducting disarmament,
     demobilization, and reintegration. When a legitimate civil government cannot take responsibility for the
     security sector, military forces handle civil security tasks. Simultaneously, they assist in developing the
     security and police forces of the HN. Typically, the responsibility for establishing and maintaining civil
     security falls to military forces from the beginning of operations until the transition phase; at this point, the
     HN security and police forces take over this role.

     1-64. Operations to restore order are conducted to stop violence and to support, reinstate, or establish civil
     authorities. These operations provide security and stability following a conflict while setting the conditions
     for transitioning to the rule of law. It is crucial to provide effective security for the local population by
     reducing their exposure to the threat of violent conflict, as this creates a stable environment for ongoing
     stability tasks and reconstruction efforts. Military police are particularly well-suited for engaging in
     operations that establish civil order. Military police Soldiers are trained and experienced in using graduated
     response techniques and applying the minimum force necessary to control a situation. In environments that
     lie between major conflict and instability, the ability to manage potentially violent events, establish order,
     and disperse crowds without resorting to significant force can have a profound impact. Successful efforts to
     maintain order with minimal violence facilitate the transition from instability to stable peace and establish
     effective governance under the rule of law.

Establish Civil Control

     1-65. Civil control focuses on justice reform and the rule of law, with efforts to rebuild the HN judiciary,
     police, and correctional systems. It involves strengthening key institutions essential for a functioning justice
     system, including law enforcement, investigative agencies, prosecution services, and public defense. Civil
     control also includes assisting the state in selecting an appropriate body of laws to enforce, typically using
     the most recent HN criminal code, which has been revised to remove blatantly abusive statutes. These
     initiatives are carried out by Army capabilities during stabilization efforts in support of the local government
     following a U.S. military intervention.

     1-66. Military police conduct border control, perform border security, and facilitate civil freedom of
     movement. These tasks are part of a broader mission to establish civil security. While some of these
     responsibilities overlap police operations, the tactical aspects of military police support are typically covered
     in the context of mobility operations (chapter 2) and security operations (chapter 3). Activities supporting
     PRC are discussed in chapter 5.

     1-67. A central component of civil security is the ability of the state to monitor and regulate its borders.
     Typically, border and coast guard forces are responsible for securing national boundaries, while customs and
     immigration officials manage the movement of people, animals, and goods across designated entry points.
     These border controls are essential for several reasons: they regulate immigration, oversee the movement of
     local and foreign populations, collect excise taxes and duties, prevent the import or export of controlled
     materials, limit smuggling, and help control the spread of diseases through quarantine. When these
     capabilities have broken down or no longer exist, U.S. forces may need to step in to fill this gap and maintain
     the security of the affected nation. Military police may play a significant role in this initiative. The essential
     tasks involved may include:

          • An initial response where military forces—
                 Establish border control and boundary security.
                 Establish and disseminate rules relevant to movement.
                 Dismantle roadblocks and establish checkpoints.
                 Promote stability and CIV freedom of movement.
                 Ensure freedom of maneuver.

          • A transformation where military forces train and equip border control and boundary security forces.

18  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                               Security and Mobility Support to Army Operations

Protect Key Personnel and Facilities

     1-68. Military forces may extend protection and support to key CIV personnel when necessary to ensure their
     ongoing contributions to the overall operation. In the interest of transparency, military forces specifically
     request and carefully negotiate this protection. The long-term success of interventions often depends on the
     ability of external actors to safeguard and maintain critical infrastructure until the HN can assume that
     responsibility. Protecting key facilities may be required immediately or over the long term. Essential tasks
     may include both an initial response and subsequent transformation, described as follows:

          • An initial response where military forces—
                 Protect government-sponsored CIV reconstruction and stabilization personnel.
                 Safeguard contractor and CIV personnel involved in reconstruction and stabilization efforts and
                     their resources.
                 Provide emergency logistics support as needed.
                 Protect and secure places of worship and cultural sites.
                 Safeguard critical infrastructure, natural resources, civil registries, and property ownership
                     documents.
                 Protect strategically important institutions, such as government buildings, medical facilities and
                     public health infrastructure, the central bank, the national treasury, integral commercial banks,
                     museums, and religious sites.
                 Secure military depots, equipment, ammunition caches, and communication systems.
                 Establish vetting and screening protocols.
                 Identify, secure, protect, and coordinate the disposition of munitions stockpiles and CBRN
                     material, including precursors and facilities, utilizing EOD technical expertise.

          • A transformation where military forces build HN capacity to protect—
                 CIV reconstruction and stabilization personnel.
                 Public infrastructure and institutions.
                 Military infrastructure.

Restore Essential Services

     1-69. The activities related to the primary stability task extend beyond merely restoring local civil services
     and addressing the impacts of humanitarian crises. While military forces typically focus on the immediate
     response to the urgent needs of the population, other CIV agencies and organizations address broader
     humanitarian issues and the overall well-being of society. Transition tasks lay the groundwork for long-term
     development by tackling the root causes of conflicts that lead to famine, the displacement of CIVs, and human
     trafficking. Efforts to foster sustainability ensure that these changes are lasting by institutionalizing positive
     societal transformations.

     1-70. During these efforts, military forces support HN agencies and CIV relief organizations. However, when
     the HN cannot fulfill its roles, military forces may need to undertake these tasks directly or assist other CIV
     agencies and organizations. These activities must be appropriately scaled to match the local capacity for
     sustainability. Proper scaling maximizes the opportunities for the local population to establish small-scale
     enterprises that can deliver as many essential services as possible through the private economy. Large-scale
     projects that demand complex support from the HN should not be initiated until the necessary infrastructure
     is in place to sustain such efforts.

     1-71. Military police play a crucial role in supporting the restoration of essential services in various ways.
     Their efforts, which involve activities and capabilities related to security and mobility, focus on addressing
     DCs, ensuring the safety of convoys and personnel, and maintaining necessary controls during missions
     aimed at preventing famine and providing emergency aid such as food relief, nonfood assistance programs,
     and public health initiatives. PRC is dedicated to supporting DCs and offers detailed guidelines for planning
     and executing these missions. (See chapter 5 and ATP 3-57.10 for more information.) For details on the
     mobility activities associated with managing DCs, see chapter 2.

     1-72. International organizations, NGOs, and other humanitarian actors are the most qualified and trained to
     address the humanitarian crises DCs face. Notable international organizations include the United Nations and
     the International Organization for Migration. Relevant NGOs might include groups such as Cooperative

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  19


---

Chapter 1

    Assistance for Relief Everywhere. The International Committee of the Red Cross, a prominent international
    organization, also plays a crucial role in humanitarian efforts.

    1-73. The presence and uncontrolled movement of DCs can jeopardize the success of operations. DCs often
    indicate broader issues such as conflict, insecurity, and social inequalities. How these displaced populations
    are treated can either build trust and confidence—laying the groundwork for stabilization and reconstruction
    among a traumatized community—or foster resentment and lead to further chaos. Local and international aid
    organizations are typically best equipped to address the needs of the local population but require a secure
    environment to operate effectively. By working closely together, military forces can help ensure the success
    of these organizations by providing essential support to the community.

    1-74. DCs often mainly consist of women and children. Many members of these communities may
    experience some form of posttraumatic stress, and all of them require essential resources such as food, shelter,
    and medical care. In the aftermath of a major disaster, humanitarian crisis, or conflict, providing adequate
    support to DCs can be a challenge that exceeds the capabilities of available military forces. Therefore,
    through coordination with other agencies and organizations, military forces play a vital role in providing
    humanitarian assistance to affected populations. The list of essential tasks includes—

         • Assisting DCs.
         • Supporting assistance to aid DCs.
         • Providing security for camps housing DCs.

    1-75. Military police play a crucial role in carrying out various essential tasks. While they often lead these
    activities, they more commonly support other Army units, joint forces, and CIV organizations. Managing
    DCs can require substantial effort and military police resources. Their responsibilities include ensuring the
    safety and security of DCs from the moment they are discovered until they are taken to collection points and
    camps and ultimately assisting in their resettlement or return to their homes.

Assist Dislocated Civilians

     1-76. When assisting DCs, military forces—
          • Ensure that humanitarian aid organizations have access to populations in need.
          • Estimate food and other aid needs for affected populations.
          • Assess the adequacy of local physical transport, distribution, and storage.

Support Assistance to Dislocated Civilians

    1-77. When supporting efforts to assist DCs, the essential tasks can be divided into two main phases  (initial

    response and transformation). These phases are described as follows:

         • An initial response, where military forces—
               Estimate food aid needs for DCs.
               Assess the adequacy of local transport, distribution systems, and storage facilities.
               Establish camps to accommodate DCs.
               Provide emergency food, water, shelter, sanitation, and medical care to DCs.

         • Transformation, where military forces—
               Ensure access to basic services, including education and healthcare.
               Clear damaged and destroyed housing and assess the extent of the damage.

Support Security to Dislocated Civilian Camps

     1-78. When providing security for DC camps, the list of essential tasks may include an initial response and
     transformation, described as follows:

          • An initial response, where military forces—
                 Assess the conditions of temporary shelters and camps for DCs.
                 Ensure adequate protection and monitoring of these camps.
                 Facilitate access for humanitarian aid organizations and security forces to the camps.

          • Transformation, where military forces assist in establishing and maintaining order in camps.

20                                          ATP 3-39.30                                     25 March 2026


---

                                                                               Security and Mobility Support to Army Operations

SECURITY AND MOBILITY SUPPORT TO DEFENSE SUPPORT OF CIVIL AUTHORITIES

     1-79. Military police first responder capabilities are essential to the defense support of civil authorities
     (DSCA) mission. DSCA refers to the support provided by U.S. federal military forces, DOD CIVs, DOD
     contractor personnel, DOD component assets, and National Guard forces. The use of National Guard forces
     occurs when the Secretary of Defense (SecDef), in coordination with state governors, requests their activation
     under Title 10, United States Code (10 USC) in response to requests for assistance from civil authorities
     during domestic emergencies, law enforcement support, or other domestic activities and special events.
     DSCA missions are also known as civil support missions (see DODD 3025.18). Military police support for
     domestic operations is subject to various legal constraints (see ADP 3-28 for more information on DSCA).
     While most military police tasks performed in domestic support are similar to those conducted in overseas
     operations, they are carried out under very different conditions. The following core tasks of DSCA provide
     domestic support for:

          • Disasters.
          • CIV law enforcement.
          • CBRN incidents.
          • Other incidents.

     1-80. The list of security and mobility support activities is only slightly reduced from those conducted in
     offense, defense, and stability. Significant support for mobility operations, and possibly for security
     operations, will likely be necessary to assist civil authorities. DSCA restrictions only minimally impact this
     support, which may substantially affect other military police disciplines.

     1-81. DSCA has several distinct features that set it apart from other Army operations. DSCA uses
     nondestructive methods to save lives, alleviate suffering, and protect property. Typically, the Army National
     Guard is the first military force to respond to domestic emergencies. Federal military forces usually step in
     to support a federal agency, often after a Presidential declaration, to enhance the efforts and resources of state
     and local governments. Each state governor has the authority to activate the National Guard forces of the
     state (both the Army National Guard and Air National Guard) to respond to an incident. State resources fund
     this activation and fall under the command and control of the governor. These activated forces operate in
     state active duty status and carry out missions based on the needs of the state, adhering to state laws and
     regulations. This type of response is termed National Guard Civil Support rather than DSCA. DSCA
     operations involve the Army National Guard (in 10 USC or 32 USC status), Army Reserve, and active duty
     Army forces.

     1-82. Military police operations in DSCA may involve the simultaneous use of all military police disciplines
     and specialized capabilities as needed. Military police support might be essential for Army forces that provide
     movement, maneuver, command and control, protection, and sustainment to government agencies at various
     levels until those agencies can resume normal operations. In martial law, extensive military police assistance
     may be necessary. Military police Soldiers are equipped with the training and technical skills required for
     relief operations, which focus on restoring civil order. This includes support for mobility and security
     operations. The restoration of civil order requires—

          • Operational unity.
          • Effective coordination.
          • Public acceptance.
          • Threat awareness.
          • Minimal use of force.

     1-83. In DSCA, there are few unique military police missions that differ from those conducted during other
     operations. The primary distinction lies in the context that these missions are executed. Section 1385, Title
     18, USC (18 USC 1385), known as the Posse Comitatus Act, restricts the actions that military forces—
     especially Regular Army units, including federalized National Guard units—can undertake within the United
     States and its territories. Unlike federal (active duty) forces, National Guard units, which remain under the
     control of their respective state governors, are not subject to the same limitations. Army forces work closely
     with these state-controlled units to coordinate their efforts. These agencies are trained, resourced, and
     equipped more extensively than similar agencies involved in stability activities overseas. Federal government
     policies outline the essential services that Army forces provide in response to disasters. Within this

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  21


---

Chapter 1

    framework of support to federal agencies, the primary focus for military police during DSCA operations is
    to assist these agencies in restoring essential services. Essential services of concern for military police
    include—

         • Police services.
         • Corrections and detention operations.
         • Law and order.

    1-84. Beyond their core duties, military police can support federal agency personnel  by contributing a range
    of essential support services, to include—

         • Rescues.
         • Food and water.
         • Emergency shelter.
         • Basic sanitation, including sewage and garbage disposal.
         • Minimum-essential access to affected areas.

EMPLOYMENT CONSIDERATIONS

    variables. Table 1-2 is a baseline for military police leaders and staff to use when planning security and

MARITIME ENVIRONMENT CONSIDERATIONS FOR SECURITY
AND MOBILITY SUPPORT

     1-86. Previous conflicts have proven the critical role of land forces in maritime theaters. In nearly all
     instances, the land plays a crucial role in determining the strategies importance of a maritime area for a
     CCDR. Land masses near or surrounded by water create critical choke points, facilitate the projection of
     forces into and out of maritime areas, and are home to the majority of the population of the world. The
     ultimate goal of conflict is usually not to control vast expanses of open water but rather the land and the
     people who inhabit it. Therefore, planning and training for the unique challenges of operations in these
     environments is crucial, as is an integrated planning approach with the rest of the joint force.

     1-87. Maritime operational environments introduce specific coordination and synchronization requirements
     for military police units, which typically train and operate in land environments. Planning for the Army and
     joint forces must encompass the dynamic nature of threats and constraints faced by land forces in maritime
     regions. The movement and maneuver of Army units between land masses largely depend on joint
     capabilities. Control over critical land areas is essential for sustaining and protecting joint operations in a
     maritime operational environment. This interdependence has historically been vital for achieving success in
     armed conflicts against peer threats in maritime theaters, and it will continue to be crucial in the foreseeable
     future. For more information on maritime environments, see FM 3-0.

25 March 2026                            ATP 3-39.30                                        25


---

Chapter 1

MARITIME OPERATIONAL FRAMEWORK

     1-88. Applying the operational framework in maritime environments requires military police commanders
     and staffs to consider the effects of maritime surface areas and the integration with maritime forces. It also
     necessitates a different understanding of what defines deep, close, and rear operations in relation to each
     other (see figure 1-5). The physical separation of forces by bodies of water impacts considerations for mutual
     support, as many operations are likely to involve geographically distinct areas. Military police echelons may
     gather information on maritime surface areas to support other Services. They may also need to defend against
     enemy amphibious assaults, which requires tactical-level coordination with Navy and Marine Corps forces.

Legend:  brigade support area       LR           long range
BSA      corps support area         LZ           landing zone

DC       detainee collection point  MSR          main supply route

    Figure 1-5. Notional military police support in a maritime-dominated environment

26                                  ATP 3-39.30                         25 March 2026


---

                                                                               Security and Mobility Support to Army Operations

     1-89. Military police forces might support amphibious landings or airborne and air assault operations over
     water as part of joint operations. Operations previously considered close, such as forcible entry, can extend
     into deep operations depending on the distance between islands or sea bases. Tactical support areas may be
     widely separated and require watercraft or other joint capabilities to ensure tactical-level sustainment or
     enable tactical maneuver forces to secure sea LOC.

Deep Operations

     1-90. Deep operations in a maritime environment aim to defeat enemy anti-access and area denial
     capabilities, thereby creating conditions conducive to joint offensive operations. This can be achieved
     through information collection, special operations forces, and fires. Additionally, these operations may
     include reconnaissance and security activities to support joint defensive efforts utilizing the same capabilities.
     While military police forces typically do not directly affect deep operations with their capabilities, they
     contribute by providing essential support. This support involves protecting long-range fires and information
     collection assets and supporting special operations forces.

Close Operations

     1-91. Security and mobility support in close operations in a maritime environment can resemble traditional
     land-based approaches discussed earlier in this chapter. Seizing key terrain requires Army forces to conduct
     offensive operations, including airborne operations, air assaults, and amphibious assaults, all supported by
     military police. Close operations also involve the defense of islands and island-based nodes that enable joint
     operations, such as airfields and ports. Due to the isolated nature of some forward-deployed Army forces,
     which are critical to the success of joint operations, these forces may need to conduct defensive operations
     for extended periods until additional reinforcements arrive. Sustainment planning should assume potential
     isolation lasting several weeks or longer. While most security and mobility support tasks will remain
     consistent during close operations, military police forces must be ready to operate in smaller teams, often
     isolated from leadership and sustainment and dismounted for longer durations.

Rear Operations

     1-92. Rear operations in a maritime environment include those necessary to set and sustain the theater and
     facilitate combat operations for the joint force. These operations involve conducting protected reception,
     staging, onward movement, and integration and theater sustainment. Reception, staging, onward movement,
     and integration may occur thousands of miles from where a unit will be employed in combat. Similar to land
     operations, military police will continue to provide security and mobility support in the rear area. However,
     there will be increased joint integration during the planning process to prioritize critical joint resources and
     mitigate risks to vulnerable and extended air and sea LOC.

     1-93. Many security and mobility support missions are linked to specific support areas, which helps build
     and maintain combat power. While ground forces have their designated support areas, large bodies of water
     may necessitate waterborne or airborne transport for the staging and movement of personnel and equipment
     between various echeloned support areas. These support areas can be located at sea or on land, such as on
     islands, and are interconnected by sea or air LOC. These strategic and tactical support areas are critical assets
     but can be vulnerable to enemy attacks, requiring heightened security measures.

POLICE INTELLIGENCE OPERATIONS INTEGRATION

     1-94. PIO must be integrated into all aspects of security and mobility support planning and execution. The
     information collected by military police contributes to police intelligence, which helps inform decision-
     making processes for commanders, military police staff, Soldiers, and leaders involved in security and
     mobility support missions. This intelligence feeds into the broader intelligence process, enabling
     commanders to make informed decisions regarding the security of convoys transiting through the area. It also
     provides military police the necessary information to mitigate risks during staff planning. During large-scale
     combat operations, military police supporting maneuver forces rely significantly on traditional military
     intelligence (MI) processes to drive combat operations against conventional enemy forces. Additionally,
     military police conducting security and mobility support operations during these large-scale operations also
     take on tasks to counter irregular threats that could jeopardize friendly operations or hinder freedom of

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  27


---

Chapter 1

     movement. As military operations shift from offensive and defensive strategies to stability missions, the role
     of PIO becomes increasingly important. It supports situational awareness of criminal threats that destabilize
     society and affect police effectiveness, helping to control crime and enable governance according to the rule
     of law.

     1-95. The mobility and communication capabilities of military police units allow them to identify potential
     threats and rapidly report these contacts. Their involvement in security and mobility support operations
     enables military police Soldiers to regularly interact with the local population, fostering police engagement
     and facilitating information gathering. Integrating PIO throughout security and mobility support missions
     can create opportunities for collecting intelligence that meets requirements and enhances situational
     awareness.

     1-96. The collection of police information and the development of police intelligence products enable
     military police to plan and implement measures that reduce crime-conducive conditions and counter the
     impacts of criminal activity on military operations. Figure 1-6, page 29, illustrates the integration of security
     and mobility support tasks within the police intelligence framework. These countermeasures may include—

          • Identifying high-threat areas to focus military police reconnaissance and surveillance efforts and
                recommending bypass routes to movement control teams.

          • Conducting offensive operations to dismantle criminal networks and organizations.
          • Hardening likely targets by implementing physical security and AT measures.
          • Developing procedures to prevent, detect, and respond to criminal and terrorist actions before they

                occur.
          • Implementing recommendations to reduce vulnerabilities to criminal and other irregular threats.
