                                                 Chapter 2

                  Reconnaissance and Surveillance

    Reconnaissance and surveillance are part of information collection. Military police engage in
    these activities by using their policing capabilities, enhanced through the application of PIO
    (see ATP 3-39.20). Reconnaissance involves actively collecting information, while
    surveillance refers to the systematic gathering of information, which should be continuous and
    include both active and passive methods. Both reconnaissance and surveillance generate raw
    data and information, which may constitute combat information that satisfies one or more CCIR
    or other information requirements. This chapter discusses how military police conduct
    reconnaissance and surveillance to monitor likely high-speed avenues of approach and
    potential LZ and drop zones.

MILITARY POLICE SUPPORT TO INFORMATION COLLECTION

     2-1. Information collection is an activity that synchronizes and integrates the planning and employment of
     sensors and assets as well as the processing, exploitation, and dissemination systems in direct support of
     current and future operations (FM 3-55). This activity implies a function, mission, or action and identifies
     the organization that performs it. Information collection activities work as a cohesive system, emphasizing,
     synchronizing, and integrating all components and systems. This process combines the intelligence and
     operations staff functions to address CCIR. For more detailed information on information collection, see
     FM 3-55.

     2-2. Information collection is the acquisition of information and the provision of this information to
     processing elements. This includes:

          • Manage collection.
          • Direct and task collection.
          • Execute collection.

     2-3. Military police support information collection requirements during the conduct of military police
     operations that may contribute to CCIR. They support intelligence-led, time-sensitive operations or shape
     policing strategies necessary to forecast, anticipate, and preempt crime or disruptive behavior to maintain
     order. Military police also focus their efforts on collecting police information. Police information is
     information collected during military police operations concerning crime, disorder, criminal activity, and
     criminal threats (FM 3-39). Police information encompasses various data regarding crime, law enforcement,
     and police institutions, along with their effectiveness, collected actionable materials, and additional general
     information in the operational environment.

     2-4. Collecting and analyzing police information enhances situational awareness, protection, civil control,
     and law enforcement effectiveness. Before assigning military police to gather police information,
     commanders and staffs utilize police intelligence analysts to carry out activities that generate knowledge
     about the crime environment or to meet specific information requirements without deploying collection
     assets. These activities include—

          • Performing searches and queries in police or detention databases, such as the Army Law
                Enforcement Reporting System, Army Corrections Information System, or Detainee Reporting
                System (DRS).

          • Using analytical software to mine police databases and generate initial statistical data that directly
                answer information needs without requiring further analysis.

          • Coordinating and liaising with other military units (both U.S. and multinational), including CA,
                special operations forces, and elements involved in reconnaissance and security operations.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  31


---

Chapter 2

          • Coordinating the use of MI personnel (when authorized) to leverage the Distributed Common
                Ground System (Army) capabilities for querying intelligence databases and integrating information
                from intelligence sources.

          • Sharing information and collaborating with unified action partners, such as local law enforcement
                organizations, federal law enforcement agencies, and law enforcement personnel from partner
                nations.

     2-5. Generally, PIO focus on military police activities related directly to crime and criminal behavior,
     including law enforcement, corrections, and criminal investigations. PIO complements, but does not replace,
     traditional MI processes during Army operations. Military police utilize reconnaissance and surveillance
     capabilities as their primary means for collecting information. When conducting reconnaissance in tactical
     environments, military police primarily aim to identify the presence of criminal groups, terrorist
     organizations, enemy special operations teams, long-range reconnaissance units, mounted or dismounted
     combat reconnaissance teams, and partially attrited or reconstituted small combat units. They also monitor
     other irregular threats in an AO. For more detailed information on PIO, see ATP 3-39.20.

     2-6. Military police emphasize reconnaissance and surveillance to better understand crime environments,
     police and detention capabilities, and destabilizing elements within society. This information supports U.S.
     stability operations and helps consolidate gains after large-scale combat. While surveillance is a component
     of reconnaissance, the two have key differences. Surveillance is systematic, is typically passive in
     information collection, and may be conducted continuously. In contrast, reconnaissance is generally limited
     to the duration of a specific mission and actively gathers information. It involves a focused collection effort
     that utilizes various tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTP) throughout the mission, which may include
     extended surveillance periods.

     2-7. Military police units conduct surveillance to gather information that helps protect against unexpected
     threats in an AO. This information is crucial for understanding, planning, and executing missions related to
     offense, defense, and stability. In populated areas, military police patrols may be more acceptable as a
     collection asset because they are perceived as a law enforcement organization rather than a purely combat
     unit. Additionally, military police receive specialized training in nonlethal use of force techniques and the
     graduated escalation of force.

     2-8. Commanders and staffs must comprehensively understand the capabilities and limitations of available
     military police assets. This prevents collection asset tasking that does not match capability at a particular
     echelon or organization. Some reconnaissance and surveillance tasks require specialized equipment, training,
     or expertise to be completed successfully. As a result, there may be a need to make specific requests for
     technical capabilities that are not part of the standard military police organization. Understanding the
     capabilities and limitations of various military police organizations is crucial when planning and requesting
     the appropriate echelon and type of military police technical support to meet mission requirements. See
     FM 3-39 for details on the different military police organizations and their technical capabilities.

RECONNAISSANCE

     2-9. Reconnaissance is a mission undertaken to obtain, by visual observation or other detection methods,
     information about the activities and resources of an enemy or adversary, or to secure data concerning the
     meteorological, hydrographic, or geographic characteristics of a particular area (JP 2-0). Reconnaissance
     conducted by small units primarily depends on human dynamics rather than technical resources. It is a
     targeted collection effort carried out before, during, and after operations. The information gathered is
     essential for commanders and staffs as it supports the IPOE process. This information enables them to
     formulate, confirm, or adjust courses of action (COAs).

     2-10. The responsibility for conducting reconnaissance does not rest solely with cavalry units. Every unit has
     an implied duty to gather and report information regarding the terrain, CIV activities, and disposition of
     friendly and enemy forces, regardless of location or primary function. Troops engaged in close combat and
     reconnaissance patrols from maneuver units at all levels collect information about enemy units they
     encounter. In echelon support areas, reserve maneuver forces, functional and multifunctional support and
     sustainment elements, and other governmental and multinational agencies observe and report on CIV and
     enemy activity and on significant changes in terrain and trafficability. Certain branches, such as the Corps of
     Engineers, CA, Military Police, and the Chemical Corps, have specific reconnaissance tasks that enhance the

32         ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                Reconnaissance and Surveillance

overall reconnaissance efforts of the force. However, brigade combat team (BCT), division, and corps
commanders primarily rely on their organic or attached reconnaissance elements—ground or air—and
intelligence units to conduct reconnaissance.

2-11. Military police conduct reconnaissance to answer general and specific information requirements
supporting the overall collection effort. They serve as the eyes and ears of the commander, particularly in
support areas and along ground lines of communication, by locating the enemy and reporting information
gathered from reconnaissance patrols. The areas of focus include MSRs, bridges, tunnels, depots, terminals,
sustainment bases, ammunition supply points, communication centers or nodes, critical routes, command
posts, and facilities deemed critical by the commander. Additionally, military police familiarize themselves
with towns, populated areas, ridgelines, wooded areas, and important terrain features that could affect
movements along road networks.

2-12. Military police play a crucial role in this effort by gathering route reconnaissance information for
MSRs, ASRs, airfields, seaports, and potential LZ within the operational area. Their reconnaissance
capabilities encompass a range of tasks, from tactical assessments to more technical evaluations related to
investigative and forensic operations.

2-13. During stability tasks, military police conduct reconnaissance with a specialized focus on policing and
investigative aspects of the environment (see figure 2-1). As the demand for technical capabilities from
military police increases—typically when stability tasks become more prominent—it may be necessary to
consolidate military police assets and capabilities under the command of military police battalions and
brigades within the division, corps, and theater echelons. This consolidation ensures the effective integration
and synchronization of military police technical capabilities across the AO. For details on police information
operations, see ATP 3-39.20; for more information on military police reconnaissance, see FM 3-39.

                        Figure 2-1. Military police reconnaissance capabilities

2-14. Successful reconnaissance operations share seven fundamental principles that leaders must follow
during the planning, preparation, and execution phases. These principles include—

     • Ensuring continuous reconnaissance.
     • Refraining from holding reconnaissance assets in reserve.
     • Focusing on the reconnaissance objective.
     • Reporting all information quickly and accurately.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  33


---

Chapter 2

          • Maintaining freedom of maneuver.
          • Gaining and maintaining contact with the threat.
          • Developing the situation rapidly.

     2-15. Regardless of the type of reconnaissance (see ADP 3-90, ATP 3-34.81, and FM 3-98 for specific
     reconnaissance information), the unit comprises three key elements: command, reconnaissance, and security.
     When a reconnaissance mission is received, the mission leader assesses the situation and organizes the
     reconnaissance force according to the mission variables. The mission leader develops the plan based on
     several important considerations:

          • Obtaining accurate intelligence.
          • Conducting rehearsals.
          • Carrying out inspections.
          • Utilizing deception measures.
          • Using the smallest possible unit to accomplish the mission.
          • Minimizing audio and electronic communications.
          • Employing surveillance, target acquisition, and night observation devices.
          • Remaining undetected.

     2-16. This manual focuses on three forms of reconnaissance: route, area, and zone. It also covers the specific
     focus of civil reconnaissance and the details associated with reconnaissance and presence patrols. Military
     police typically use two types of patrols during reconnaissance operations: reconnaissance patrols and
     presence patrols. When carried out in a tactical environment, the guidelines for patrol operations outlined in
     ATP 3-39.10 remain valid.

ROUTE RECONNAISSANCE

     2-17. Route reconnaissance is a form of reconnaissance operation to obtain detailed information of a
     specified route and all terrain from which the enemy could influence movement along that route (ADP 3-90).
     That route may be a cross-country mobility corridor. Military police conduct route reconnaissance to gather
     new or updated information on the conditions of the route, including obstacles, bridge classifications, and
     enemy or CIV activity in the area. Typically, the commander assigns this mission when there is a need to use
     a specific route for the movement of friendly forces.

     2-18. The commander assigns a route reconnaissance as a separate mission or a specific task for a unit already
     conducting zone or area reconnaissance. Usually, a platoon size unit will conduct a route reconnaissance one
     route at a time. For larger organizations, the number of available platoons or other designated reconnaissance
     assets directly affects how many routes can be covered simultaneously. By integrating ground, air, and
     technical resources, route reconnaissance can be completed more quickly and thoroughly. For more detailed
     information on route reconnaissance, see ATP 3-34.81 and FM 3-90.

     2-19. Route reconnaissance tasks include the following:
          • Identify, report and, based on engagement criteria, clear all enemy forces within capabilities that
                could impact movement along the route.
          • Assess the trafficability of the route to determine whether it can support friendly forces.
          • Survey all terrain the enemy can use to control movement along the route, including choke points,
                ambush sites, pickup zones, LZ, and drop zones.
          • Reconnoiter all urban areas, CBRN contaminated zones, and lateral routes along the route.
          • Evaluate and classify all bridges, defiles, overpasses, underpasses, and culverts along the route.
          • Identify fords, crossing sites, or bypasses around existing and reinforcing obstacles, including urban
                areas.
          • Locate all obstacles and establish lanes as specified in the execution orders (see appendix C for
                guidance on developing security and mobility support attachments that assist in the base operation
                order [OPORD]).
          • Report all gathered route information to the headquarters that initiated the route reconnaissance
                mission. This report should include a sketch map or route overlay.

34  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                      Reconnaissance and Surveillance

AREA RECONNAISSANCE

     2-20. Area reconnaissance is a form of reconnaissance operation that focuses on obtaining detailed
     information about the terrain or enemy activity within a prescribed area (ADP 3-90). Area reconnaissance is
     vital to maintaining area security and contributes to the commander’s information collection plan.

     2-21. Military police area reconnaissance involves a series of coordinated actions. It begins with observations
     and reports collected over time by military police patrols and information gathered through collaboration
     with HN police and other allied forces. Military police area reconnaissance plans focus on locations near
     facilities deemed critical by the commander. These locations include—

          • Named areas of interest.
          • Airbases.
          • Bases and base clusters.
          • Communications centers.
          • Logistic support clusters.
          • Key terminals, depots, and bridges.
          • Critical terrain features.
          • High-value assets.

     2-22. When conducting an area reconnaissance mission, military police leaders follow these principles:
          • The unit should move to the designated area as quickly as possible, typically by traveling along
                existing roads using appropriate movement techniques. During this transit, the unit reports enemy
                opposition and bypasses it according to the commander’s reconnaissance guidance.
          • Upon reaching the designated area, the unit halts and establishes an objective rally point. After the
                rally point is set, the unit reconnoiters the objective in one of the following ways:
                 If the terrain allows, the security element moves to an overwatch position to observe the
                     reconnaissance element. The mission leader may direct small reconnaissance teams to each
                     surveillance or vantage point around the objective instead of having the entire reconnaissance
                     element move as a unit from point to point.
                 If the terrain does not permit securing the objective area, the unit will leave a security element
                     at the objective rally point. In this case, reconnaissance and security teams will move to various
                     surveillance or vantage points to reconnoiter the objective.
          • After reconnoitering the objective and gathering the necessary details, all elements return to the
                objective rally point (see appendix C for information on developing security and mobility support
                attachments that support the base OPORD). Teams will then share information, consolidate it, and
                report it before returning to the patrol headquarters or proceeding to the next mission.

ZONE RECONNAISSANCE

     2-23. Zone reconnaissance is a form of reconnaissance operation that involves a directed effort to obtain
     detailed information on all routes, obstacles, terrain, and enemy forces within a zone defined by boundaries
     (ADP 3-90). The commander assigns a zone reconnaissance mission when additional information about a
     specific area is needed before deploying other forces. This type of mission is suitable when the enemy
     situation is unclear, there is limited terrain knowledge, or combat operations have changed the landscape.
     Zone reconnaissance techniques can involve moving elements, stationary teams, or a combination of area
     reconnaissance efforts. The three methods that military police use to conduct a zone reconnaissance are the
     fan, converging routes, and successive sectors. See ATP 3-39.4 for more details on zone reconnaissance.

CIVIL RECONNAISSANCE

     2-24. Civil reconnaissance is a targeted, planned, and coordinated observation and evaluation of specific
     civil aspects of the environment such as areas, structures, capabilities, organizations, people, or events
     (JP 3-57). Civil reconnaissance specifically targets the civil component, which can be remembered using the
     acronym ASCOPE—areas, structures, capabilities, organizations, people, and events. This type of
     reconnaissance can be carried out by CA units or other forces as needed. Like CBRN or engineer
     reconnaissance, civil reconnaissance is not a standalone form of reconnaissance but rather includes specific
     tasks that enhance the overall reconnaissance efforts of the force. For more detailed information on civil
     reconnaissance, see FM 3-57.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  35


---

Chapter 2

     2-25. Military police may also be involved in civil reconnaissance, a focused type of reconnaissance that is
     performed by leveraging the specifics of PIO (see ATP 3-39.20), police operations (see ATP 3-39.10), and
     other skills typically associated with police officers. The presence of military police patrols is particularly
     effective for various civil reconnaissance missions.

RECONNAISSANCE PATROLS

     2-26. A reconnaissance patrol is typically conducted by military police in a tactical role. The purpose of a
     reconnaissance patrol is to gather information that confirms or disproves previously obtained intelligence.
     The intent of this type of patrol is to move stealthily, avoid enemy contact, and complete the mission without
     engaging in close combat. Reconnaissance patrols strive to carry out their objectives without being detected.
     However, since detection cannot always be avoided, a reconnaissance patrol carries the necessary arms and
     equipment to protect itself and break contact with the enemy.

     2-27. A reconnaissance patrol typically travels light, with as few personnel, arms, ammunition, and
     equipment as possible. This increases stealth and cross-country mobility in close terrain. Regardless of how
     a patrol is armed and equipped, the leader must always prepare for the possibility of direct-fire contact with
     a hostile force. Military police leaders should anticipate potential observation points and implement strategies
     to mitigate risks (see ATP 5-19). If the patrol is detected or unexpected opportunities arise, reconnaissance
     teams must be capable of rapidly transitioning to combat.

PRESENCE PATROLS

     2-28. A presence patrol is a form of reconnaissance typically used in stability or DSCA activities. Its main
     objective is to gather information about the conditions within the AO of the unit. To achieve this, the patrol
     collects specific and general critical information as determined by the commander. The patrol actively seeks
     out this information, observes it, and reports it. In addition to its reconnaissance role, the presence patrol is a
     tangible representation of U.S. military forces that projects an image supporting the commander’s intent.
     These patrols are also designed to demonstrate to the local population the presence and intentions of U.S.
     forces. Ultimately, presence patrols aim to convey determination, competence, confidence, concern and,
     when necessary, the overwhelming power of the force.

     2-29. Military police Soldiers routinely observe and interact with the people and environments that they
     operate. This consistent contact makes military police patrols effective for collecting passive and active
     information. Passive collection occurs whenever military police Soldiers engage with their surroundings and
     the local population. Through these interactions, military police patrols may help fulfill general information
     and intelligence needs relevant to the entire AO. Additionally, they may uncover valuable information that
     was not specifically requested. This information is then shared with commanders and staffs, complete with
     the contextual details and circumstances surrounding its discovery.

     2-30. Military police use various patrol methods to effectively gather police information based on specific
     circumstances. In densely populated areas, such as urban environments, or during special events, military
     police may choose foot or dismounted patrols. This approach allows for direct interaction, observation, and
     engagement with the community, increasing the likelihood of receiving valuable information about crime or
     potential threats. In rapidly evolving situations, such as combat or active-shooter events, military police may
     prefer vehicle or mounted patrols. These options provide greater mobility, improved communication, and
     enhanced protection. The ability to quickly switch between different patrol methods makes military police
     highly effective in uncertain, dynamic, and fast-changing situations. For more details on patrol methods, see
     ATP 3-39.10.

     2-31. Military police patrols are often tasked with carrying out deliberate collection missions to gather
     specific information about an area or target. These requests are usually tied to a commander’s priority
     intelligence requirements, the PM intelligence needs regarding the AO, or specific police investigations.
     Before executing their mission, military police Soldiers receive briefings that outline the collection
     requirements. Preparing specifically for the mission is essential. Postmission debriefings are critical to ensure
     that the information gathered by military police patrols is passed on to the appropriate staff elements for
     timely analysis and dissemination.

     2-32. Before deploying presence patrols, the commander should carefully consider the message they wish to
     convey and clearly communicate their intent to the patrol leader. A presence patrol is designed to visibly

36  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                      Reconnaissance and Surveillance

     reinforce the impression the commander wants to share with the community. Factors such as where the patrol
     goes, what activities it engages in, how it handles its weapons, what equipment and vehicles it uses, and how
     it interacts with the public all contribute to that impression. Upon the return of the patrol to the main body,
     the commander should conduct a thorough debriefing. This debriefing should focus not only on gathering
     hard information but also on the patrol leader’s perceptions of the impact of the patrol on the community.
     This feedback helps the commander decide whether to adjust the strategies for future patrols. Considerations
     include—

          • Planning and rehearsing actions in and around large crowds of CIVs or noncombatants. (See
                ATP 3-39.4 and ATP 3-39.33 for additional information regarding civil disturbances.) While U.S.
                forces or the local population may have basic language skills, interpreters are essential for all but
                the most basic interactions.

          • Briefing the patrol element on collection priorities.
          • Assigning specific Soldiers the responsibility of maintaining all-around and high-low security on

                each floor and building rooftops.
          • Periodically occupying rooftops during the patrol to enhance observation and security.
          • Using urban city maps, by grid reference for navigation in urban areas. (This can be challenging.

                Maps that display street names, neighborhoods, and similar details are far more helpful).
          • Keeping track of the number and locations of translators or interpreters available during the patrol.

                (Understand the ethnicity of the translators and how it might influence the local population in the
                patrol area or along the route.) See ATP 3-39.4 for additional guidance on employing translators.
          • Monitoring recent activities or trends within the local population or urban areas.

             Note. During DSCA, military police conduct presence patrols in support of local, state, or federal
             agencies. See ADP 3-28 for additional information on DSCA.

SURVEILLANCE

     2-33. Surveillance enhances reconnaissance by directing reconnaissance assets to specific locations or
     targeted enemy units. Military police surveillance primarily aims to provide early warnings about enemy
     reconnaissance elements rather than establishing and maintaining contact with the main body of the enemy
     or engaging them directly. Surveillance is the systematic observation of aerospace, surface, or subsurface
     areas, places, persons, or things by visual, aural, electronic, photographic, or other means (JP 3-0). See
     ATP 3‑55.4, ATP 3-55.6, and FM 3-55 for more information on surveillance. For the specialized but related
     aspects associated with law enforcement investigation, see ATP 3-39.12-1.

     2-34. Military police conduct continuous surveillance of the assigned areas of interest and potential enemy
     reconnaissance routes within an assigned AO. To achieve this, they establish a series of listening posts (LPs)
     or observation posts (OPs), which can be mounted or dismounted. Additionally, military police carry out
     active mounted patrols to extend their observation capabilities and cover areas between LPs and OPs that
     may have blind spots. Special considerations must be considered when establishing LPs or OPs in urban
     areas. For further details on LPs and OPs, see ATP 3-39.4.

     2-35. The ability of a unit to communicate effectively is crucial during surveillance operations. For successful
     early warning, detailed planning for continuous communications is essential. The mission leader assesses
     communication ranges and important terrain features to identify potential wireless communication issues. If
     problems are identified, support is requested from higher headquarters.

LISTENING AND OBSERVATION POSTS

     2-36. LPs and OPs are positions that military police use to monitor enemy activity and coordinate indirect
     fire support against them. They are the primary method for maintaining surveillance over a designated avenue
     of approach or area of interest. The LP/OP is generally used—

          • On key terrain to observe a specific area.
          • As an early warning security measure within a defensive perimeter.
          • To monitor likely enemy avenues of approach.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  37


---

Chapter 2

TYPES OF LISTENING AND OBSERVATIONS POSTS

     2-37. LPs and OPs can be either mounted or dismounted:
          • Mounted LPs/OPs: These posts have the advantage of vehicular optics, weapon systems, and
                displacement speed. However, the enemy can more easily detect them than dismounted posts.
                Mounted LPs/OPs should be used when rapid movement or displacement is expected.
          • Dismounted LPs/OPs: These posts provide maximum stealth and are less likely to be detected by
                the enemy. However, they lack the agility that mounted LPs/OPs offer. A significant drawback of
                dismounted LPs/OPs is the time required to remount and move if necessary.

SELECTION OF A LISTENING OR OBSERVATION POSTS SITE

     2-38. Based on an analysis of METT-TC (I) factors and guidance from the commander, the platoon leader
     decides how many LPs and OPs to establish and where to position them. This positioning facilitates long-
     range observation along the avenues of approach assigned by the commander, ensuring depth throughout the
     sector. Squad and team leaders will then select the locations for each LP/OP on the ground. LPs/OPs must
     have the following characteristics:

          • Covered and concealed routes: The routes to and from each LP/OP must be covered and
                concealed, allowing military police to enter and exit without being detected by the enemy.

          • Unobstructed observation: Positions should provide clear observation of the assigned area.
                Ideally, the fields of observation for adjacent LPs/OPs should overlap to guarantee complete
                coverage.

          • Effective cover and concealment: Leaders should choose sites that offer both cover and
                concealment to minimize vulnerability on the battlefield. Military police may need to prioritize
                positions that provide better survivability, even if it means sacrificing some observation capability.

          • Low-profile locations: LPs and OPs should be placed in areas that do not attract attention, such as
                avoiding water towers, isolated groves of trees, or single buildings and trees. These locations can
                draw enemy fire or serve as artillery reference points. Moreover, LPs and OPs should be situated
                away from natural lines of drift, where enemy forces are likely to move, such as routes in valleys or
                near major highways.

          • Skyline positions: Avoid placing observers at topographical crests. Instead, LPs and OPs should be
                positioned farther down the slope of a hill or on the side, ensuring covered and concealed routes into
                and out of the location.

OPERATION OF A LISTENING OR OBSERVATION POST

     2-39. A military police team should ideally operate an LP/OP to maintain team integrity. Duties at LPs/OPs
     are rotated among team members: one member observes, another records and reports the observed
     information, and a third member rests or provides backup security. The team should rotate these duties every
     20 to 30 minutes, as the visual efficiency of the observer significantly decreases after that time. The team
     leader communicates enemy observations up the chain of command using the SALUTE (size, activity,
     location, unit, time, and equipment) report format. When the LP/OP team is part of a defensive perimeter,
     it—

          • Ensures that it has rearward cover.
          • Builds fighting positions for protection and concealment.
          • Uses trip flares, noisemaking devices, and night-vision optics to detect the enemy.
          • Sets up M18A1 claymores and/or networked munitions for added security.
          • Coordinates with the perimeter on reentry procedures from the withdrawal route.

     2-40. The squad leader prepares a sector sketch that includes—
          • A rough sketch of key and significant terrain highlighting named areas of interest and avenues of
                approach.
          • A north-pointing arrow indicating orientation.
          • The location of the LP/OP.
          • The location of the hide position.
          • Locations for vehicle fighting and observation positions.
          • Alternate positions for hiding, fighting, and observation.

38  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                            Reconnaissance and Surveillance

          • Routes to and from the OP and fighting positions.
          • Designated sectors of observation.
          • Preplanned artillery targets.
          • Target reference points for direct fire.
          • Prepared spot reports.

OTHER INFORMATION COLLECTION METHODS SUPPORTING
SECURITY AND MOBILITY SUPPORT

     2-41. Military police gather information from various sources while carrying out their operations. Their
     ability to operate in different areas allows them to interact with diverse populations to collect essential
     information. This information is crucial for addressing intelligence requirements related to crime, disorder,
     public fear, destabilizing factors, and the effectiveness of police efforts to maintain safe and secure
     environments. These sources help understand the networks, trends, patterns, and associations vital for
     supporting mobility, security, and PRC and for combating criminal and other irregular threats.

UNMANNED AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS

     2-42. A unmanned aircraft system (UAS) comprises everything needed to operate an unmanned aircraft
     vehicle (UAV). The UAV is the actual flying device, while the UAS includes the following components:
     personnel, UAV, payload (such as sensors or weapons), control station, communication links, launch system,
     and recovery system. Small unmanned aircraft systems (small UASs) are specifically designed for
     reconnaissance, surveillance, and remote monitoring tasks. They can locate and identify enemy forces,
     moving vehicles, weapons systems, and other targets that stand out from their surroundings. Additionally,
     small UASs can confirm the locations of friendly forces, detect noncombatant CIVs, monitor detainee
     operations, support border operations, and search for missing persons.

     2-43. Military police commanders and staffs can utilize small UASs to conduct reconnaissance and
     surveillance of specific locations and routes during tactical or law enforcement operations. This support
     enhances the security of posts, camps, and stations and helps clarify and verify facts and assumptions in the
     operational environment. Military police can launch and recover an small UASs from unprepared terrain
     within minutes and do not require special equipment. The system can be operated via a ground control unit
     or carry out fully autonomous missions by using global positioning system waypoint navigation for launch
     and recovery. For more information on the employment of UASs, see ATP 3-04.1.

MILITARY POLICE PATROLS

     2-44. Military police patrols are typically deployed throughout the AO while carrying out their assigned
     missions across the military police disciplines. The strategic dispersion of these patrols (single team, law
     enforcement unit, or squad or platoon size elements) enhances their effectiveness as collection assets. The
     observation and evaluation skills developed during police training further bolster the capabilities of military
     police patrols, enabling them to meaningfully contribute to collection efforts in support of PIO and other
     requirements.

     2-45. Military police use various patrol methods to effectively gather information based on the situation. In
     densely populated areas, such as urban environments or special events, military police may choose to conduct
     foot or dismounted patrols. This approach allows for direct interaction, observation, and engagement with
     the community, facilitating the collection of valuable information about crime or potential threats. In contrast,
     during rapidly evolving situations that require swift mobility, such as combat scenarios or active-shooter
     incidents, military police often rely on vehicle or mounted patrols. This method provides greater mobility,
     communication capabilities, and protection. The ability to quickly adapt patrol methods to meet information
     collection needs makes military police highly effective assets in uncertain, dynamic, and rapidly changing
     situations. For further details on patrol methods, see ATP 3-39.10.

     2-46. Military police patrols are often tasked with conducting deliberate collection missions to gather specific
     information about an area or target. These missions are linked to a commander's priority intelligence
     requirements, the PM intelligence needs regarding the AO, or specific police investigations. Intelligence
     requirements are typically communicated to military police Soldiers as part of their patrol briefs before
     mission execution. Thorough preparation for the mission is essential. Additionally, postmission debriefs are

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  39


---

Chapter 2

     crucial to ensure that the information collected by military police patrols is relayed to the appropriate staff
     elements for timely analysis and dissemination.

CORDON AND SEARCH, LAW ENFORCEMENT SEARCHES, AND RAIDS

     2-47. Cordon and search are a variation of movement to contact where a friendly force isolates and searches
     a target area (FM 3-90). Cordon-and-search operations are conducted throughout the range of military
     operations. When commanders carry out a cordon and search, they organize their units into four key elements:
     command, security, search or assault, and support. The security element must be large enough to establish an
     inner and an outer cordon around the target area for the search.

     2-48. In operational areas, military police typically conduct cordon and search at the company level and
     below while carrying out area security tasks. The information, documents, media, materials, observations,
     and detainees obtained during these operations provide valuable police information. This information is
     crucial for immediate, time-sensitive dissemination or use in criminal investigations and analysis to produce
     police intelligence. The data gathered during cordon-and-search efforts is essential for effectively disrupting
     criminal activity, advancing investigations, and providing evidence for prosecution under the rule of law.

     2-49. Maneuver formations generally conduct cordon-and-search operations at the maneuver battalion level
     and below. Military police who are attached to or supporting maneuver commanders can provide essential
     assistance, particularly in securing or conducting the search. Military police assets are particularly valuable
     in supporting the search element. Advanced technical capabilities that military police may offer for combined
     arms cordon and search operations include evidence response teams, MWDs, detention specialists, and
     forensic experts. Additionally, all military police Soldiers receive training in general law enforcement
     practices that equip them to protect crime scenes, collect forensic materials, and process evidence according
     to rigorous evidentiary standards.

     2-50. Law enforcement searches and raids are carried out by trained officers, including military police
     Soldiers and Department of the Army CIV police. These officers search to secure evidence for judicial
     proceedings or recover stolen property. They perform raids to apprehend offenders, gather evidence of illegal
     activities, or confiscate illegal weapons and contraband. Due to the inherent dangers of high-risk searches
     and raids, investigative units often request support from special reaction teams. These teams are specifically
     trained for high-risk law enforcement operations in high-threat environments. For more information on
     special reaction teams and law enforcement raids, see ATP 3-39.11.

     2-51. Law enforcement searches involve more than just finding evidence or locating criminal suspects; they
     also require strict procedures to ensure that the search is legal and to build a strong case for prosecution. Law
     enforcement officers typically need a search authorization or a search warrant to conduct a search. These are
     granted based on a finding of probable cause, indicating that a person, property, or evidence is likely to be
     found in the area being searched. A search authorization is explicit permission granted by a competent
     military authority, while a civil authority issues a search warrant. If a search is executed improperly or without
     proper authority, evidence collected may be considered inadmissible in a court-martial or a federal judicial
     proceeding due to the exclusionary rule. Evidence obtained due to an illegal act may also be excluded under
     the fruit of the poisoned tree doctrine. Military police conducting a law enforcement search must follow the
     guidelines specified in the search authorization. Failing to do so could compromise the value of the
     information or evidence gathered. For more details on search and seizure during police operations, see
     ATP 3-39.10.

     2-52. Law enforcement raids are used to conduct searches or apprehend suspects, especially when multiple
     individuals are involved or there is a risk of significant resistance. Requests for support from special reaction
     teams for these raids usually occur with ongoing operations by the Department of the Army Criminal
     Investigation Division (CID) special agents. These agents are highly trained in executing raids and making
     arrests and are familiar with evidence collection and preservation. Such raids often generate a wealth of
     information, gathered through active and passive methods, for law enforcement. This information may
     include statements from apprehended suspects, forensic evidence, biometric signatures, media devices,
     photographic or audio recordings, and testimonies from witnesses or bystanders at the scene.

40  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                      Reconnaissance and Surveillance

SITE EXPLOITATION AND EVIDENCE COLLECTION

     2-53. Site exploitation is the synchronized and integrated application of scientific and technological
     capabilities and enablers to answer information requirements, facilitate subsequent operations, and support
     host-nation rule of law (ATP 3-90.15). A site is a location designated by a commander that may contain
     materials relevant for collection and the positive identification of individuals. Site exploitation plays a crucial
     role in the broader context of information collection. It involves maximizing the use of information obtained
     for tactical, operational, or strategic purposes.

     2-54. Site exploitation is guided by the information collection plan. This plan helps the commander focus
     resources on gathering information to answer specific information requirements. When a site is designated
     for exploitation, the staff establishes clear objectives and tactical tasks that support the information collection
     plan. The plan also ensures that the staff requests and coordinates all necessary resources before the site
     exploitation begins. (For more detailed information on site exploitation, see ATP 3-90.15.) Tactical units
     conducting an operation appoint both a site exploitation team and an evidence collection team to plan, detect,
     collect, receive, and handle all evidence obtained during the operation.

     2-55. Evidence collection involves gathering a diverse range of physical objects, testimonies, electronic data,
     and analyses. It is a crucial task in successful military police operations. Evidence refers to objects, materials,
     or data demonstrating or strongly suggesting that an incident, association, or pattern can lead to a conclusion
     or judgment. It is important to note that the thoughts, intuition, and opinions of an analyst or investigator are
     not considered evidence. However, they can be vital in forming a conclusion or judgment. Effective evidence
     collection requires careful planning, preparation, execution, and training. When investigating a site suspected
     to be or to have been a detention location for captured or detained individuals, investigators and evidence
     collectors should be aware that these individuals often leave proof of life at their detention sites. Evidence
     collection teams can be chosen in advance to focus their training and resources effectively. To properly
     collect evidence, various tools are necessary, including digital cameras, rubber gloves, paper bags, boxes,
     tape, and marking supplies. The evidence collection process should be deliberate and methodical unless the
     situation necessitates a hasty effort. To avoid contamination and maintain the chain of custody, as few
     personnel as possible should handle the evidence. For additional guidance on collecting evidence on the
     battlefield, see ATP 3-39.21.

     2-56. The most recognizable evidence consists of physical items associated with crimes or incidents, such as
     firearms, illegal drugs, and blood-stained clothing. While these items hold evidentiary value, their
     significance increases when trained forensic analysts examine them. For example, weapons seized at the
     scene of an attack on U.S. forces can yield valuable information (such as fingerprints or deoxyribonucleic
     acid [DNA] evidence) if they are properly handled and analyzed. The barrel and firing pin of a weapon may
     precisely match with one used in previous attacks against U.S. forces or their multinational partners.
     Additionally, an evaluation of drugs and related materials may also uncover fingerprints or DNA evidence,
     while chemical analysis can reveal where the drugs were cultivated or how they were processed.

     2-57. The continuous growth of electronic devices, such as cellular phones, digital cameras, laptops, and
     global positioning systems, has broadened the range of evidence that can be collected. Photographs, video
     and audio recordings, recording equipment, computers, and portable data storage devices (diskettes, thumb
     drives, memory cards, media players) can provide a wealth of information about criminal or terrorist
     organizations. This information may include identities, training techniques, weapons capabilities, targets, and
     locations. Photographic evidence may be obtained from U.S. or HN security forces, including manned and
     unmanned aircraft.

             Note. Information obtained from electronic devices, photographs, video and audio recordings, and
             written or printed documents related to law enforcement investigations can be analyzed by trained
             digital forensic examiners in CID. If the information in these items is not relevant to law
             enforcement investigations, they should be forwarded to document and media exploitation teams,
             provided this is permitted by applicable laws and policies, to support intelligence analysis (see
             ATP 2-91.7).

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  41


---

Chapter 2

     2-58. Hardcopy documents are valuable sources of police information. Fingerprints and DNA can be
     extracted from sheets of paper or envelopes. The type of paper or printing used may provide clues regarding
     the system employed or the age of the document. Additionally, word choices and spelling can offer insights
     into the background and education of a person. Handwriting analysis can serve as another method
     investigators can use to identify specific individuals. Lists found near a computer may be particularly
     valuable, as they might contain passwords, website addresses, access codes, e-mail addresses, and aliases.
     This category of evidence also encompasses identity documents, such as passports, visas, licenses, property
     ownership documents, and shipping papers. Analyzing written and printed documentation can reveal
     locations an individual has visited, suppliers used, funding sources, and associates. For more information on
     evidence collection, see ATP 3-39.12-1.

BIOMETRIC IDENTIFICATION SUPPORT AND FORENSIC ANALYSIS

     2-59. Forensic analysis and biometric identification support through various modalities have significantly
     enhanced the ability of CID and police intelligence analysts to clarify events and identify individuals
     involved. Biometric identification tools and forensic capabilities are valuable assets for distinguishing
     between friendly, neutral, and threatening forces, effectively denying anonymity and impunity to criminals,
     terrorists, and other irregular threat actors. These tools are also essential in criminal investigations for
     identifying individuals, establishing their presence at specific locations in relation to time and space,
     determining physical contact with materials pertinent to an investigation, and identifying indicators of
     deception. Military police and CID organizations extensively utilize biometric and forensic capabilities
     during law enforcement operations on military bases and base camps or in support of offense, defense, and
     stability.

     2-60. Biometrics collection capabilities require—
          • Approved biometrics collection devices that can collect fingerprints, iris images, voice samples, and
                facial photographs to DOD standards.
          • Personnel trained in the operation of these biometrics collection devices.
          • Software that can collect, store, and manipulate biometrics for comparison and analysis.
          • Training in biometrics watch listing to empower local commanders to create and manage local watch
                lists.

     2-61. Forensic capabilities require military police personnel, law enforcement investigators, or trained
     Soldiers who can recognize, preserve, and collect potential forensic evidence. Forensic laboratory examiners
     are also needed to extract usable information from the collected materials.

Biometrics

     2-62. Biometrics is the process of recognizing an individual based on measurable anatomical, physiological,
     and behavioral characteristics (JP 2-0). Biometric applications measure biological characteristics, which are
     stored in databases for future comparisons. In addition to the biological data saved in these databases,
     biographical information and personal behavioral traits are also collected for later use.

     2-63. Biometric characteristics and traits are valuable for tracking individuals, making positive
     identifications, establishing security procedures, and detecting deception through measurable biological
     responses to stimuli. Biometric data can enhance protection and security efforts and serve as evidence in
     investigations and criminal prosecutions. This identification data is combined with claimed biographical
     information to match an individual against DOD authoritative databases. During screening, military police
     compare the claimed identity of a subject with the database to verify or discover their identity or to enroll
     them as a new identity. The data collected includes biometric information such as fingerprints, voiceprints,
     facial photographs, iris images, and DNA.

42  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                      Reconnaissance and Surveillance

     2-64. Military police, CID personnel, investigators, and police intelligence analysts can use biometric data
     to identify trends, patterns, and associations among individuals. Biometric data that confirms the identity of
     an individual, indicates their presence at specific times and locations, and assesses truthfulness or deception
     is invaluable for establishing connections within groups, cells, or organizations. Law enforcement can
     enhance criminal investigations and directly support military operations by integrating biometric data with
     the forensic analysis of evidence collected from crime scenes or attacks. Collaborating with the assistant chief
     of staff for intelligence (G-2) and battalion or brigade intelligence staff officers (S-2) to nominate persons of
     interest for biometric watch lists allows law enforcement to track these individuals globally. This capability
     enables all biometrically equipped unified action partners to reliably identify persons of interest during
     encounters on the battlefield.

             Note. Biometric data collected during military police operations can be highly valuable for MI
             personnel in developing biometrics-enabled intelligence. When there are no restrictions from
             military police investigations or legal or policy limitations, all efforts should be made to share this
             data with MI (see ATP 2-22.85).

Forensics

     2-65. Forensics is the application of various scientific processes from multiple disciplines to establish facts.
     Collecting forensic material allows for a systematic analysis of evidence, helping to identify connections
     between people, objects, or data. While it is most commonly associated with evidence collected at crime
     scenes or incident sites, forensics encompasses methodologies for analyzing computers and networks,
     accounting, psychiatry, and other specialized fields. This discipline primarily supports legal proceedings that
     may lead to criminal prosecution. Additionally, forensic analysis plays a role in processing and exploiting
     collected material to answer CCIR; provide situational awareness; and support criminal and crime analysis
     and other mission requirements related to offense, defense, and stability.

     2-66. CID supports Army forensics requirements through the Forensic Exploitation Department, Gillem
     Enclave, Georgia. This facility is stationary due to the specialized equipment and operational needs required
     for its functions. The Forensic Exploitation Department deploys teams of trained forensic examiners to assist
     operational commanders in the field. This expeditionary capability allows for timely forensic analysis across
     various areas, including latent fingerprints, tool marks, firearms, DNA, digital forensics, electronics
     exploitation, and explosive and drug chemistry. CID laboratory capabilities can be used with forensic
     laboratories from sister Services, leveraging complementary strengths to aid the operational commander. For
     more details on multi-Service forensic capabilities and roles, see ATP 3-39.21.

     2-67. Forensic analysis expands the ability of police intelligence analysts to establish trends, patterns, and
     associations by providing scientific facts about relationships between persons, objects, or data. Criminals,
     terrorists, or other threat actors tend to operate in predictable ways. For example, the analyses may focus on
     explosive hazards. This includes material at IED incidents to identify similarities in materials, the fuzing
     system, and other variables. (For additional information on EOD, see ATP 4-32.) These analyses can identify
     patterns linking the material to the bomb maker. Information from the materials analyses, including chemical
     characteristics, can enable police intelligence analysts to associate specific suppliers with those materials.
     These efforts can lead investigators to solving criminal investigations and assist operational commanders in
     executing targeting strategies.

     2-68. Proper handling of materials from crime scenes or incident sites is essential for successful forensic
     examination by scientists and technicians. Military police receive training on effectively identifying,
     collecting, and preserving evidence, whether during crime scene processing or at an incident site. For detailed
     information on evidence collection and preservation, see ATP 3-39.12-1.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  43

                                                 Chapter 3

                            Support to Mobility

    Military police support to the movement and maneuver warfighting function primarily focuses
    on facilitating mobility operations. This support involves utilizing technical capabilities and
    tasks related to PIO, managing traffic and enforcement, restoring and maintaining order, and
    supporting civil security and control, including border control, boundary security, freedom of
    movement, and detention operations. Military police units play a crucial role in expediting the
    secure movement of resources in the theater. This ensures that commanders receive the
    necessary forces, supplies, and equipment to support operational plans and respond to changing
    tactical situations. They conduct proactive measures to detect, deter, and defeat potential threats
    within the AO, ensuring the safe and orderly movement of U.S. forces while enabling freedom
    of movement for the maneuver commander. The framework of assured mobility, as outlined in
    ATP 3-90.4, clarifies the role of military police in supporting these mobility operations.

MOVEMENT AND MANEUVER

     3-1. Military police must understand the framework for providing support for mobility operations. This
     framework combines movement and maneuver, and its focus may vary between movement and maneuver.

     3-2. The movement and maneuver warfighting function is the related tasks and systems that move and employ
     forces to achieve a position of relative advantage over the enemy and other threats (ADP 3-0). Direct fire is
     inherent in maneuver and close combat. This function includes tasks related to force projection to gain a
     positional advantage over the enemy.

     3-3. Maneuver is the employment of forces in the operational area, through movement in combination with
     fires and information to achieve a position of advantage in respect to the enemy (JP 3-0). Maneuver refers to
     how commanders concentrate the effects of combat power to achieve surprise, shock, and momentum. For
     effective maneuvering, it is essential to closely coordinate with fires. Movement is vital for dispersing and
     repositioning the entire force or parts of it during maneuvers.

     3-4. Typically, military police are focused on supporting movement or enabling military police to concentrate
     on supporting maneuver at the tactical level. While they may engage in maneuvering, their main focus during
     mobility operations is to enhance the movement of other forces. This includes assisting convoys or maneuver
     units in navigating routes during breaching, gap-crossing operations, or other mobility-related missions.

MOBILITY

     3-5. Mobility is a quality or capability of military forces which permits them to move from place to place
     while retaining the ability to fulfill their primary mission (JP 3-36). According to ADP 3-90 and FM 3-90,
     mobility is crucial for successful military operations. Its primary purpose is to allow friendly forces to move
     and maneuver freely on the battlefield or within a designated AO. Commanders aim to have the ability to
     move, exploit, and pursue the enemy across a broad front. During an attack, they seek to concentrate combat
     power at specific locations. This may necessitate improving or constructing combat trails where established
     routes do not exist. The surprise gained by attacking through regions considered impassable may justify the
     resources spent on building these trails. For offensive operations, forces should bypass known obstacles
     rather than attempt to breach them when possible. However, this must be approached with caution, as it could
     play into the strategy of the enemy. Bypassed obstacles should be reported to higher headquarters and marked
     when the situation permits.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  45


---

Chapter 3

     3-6. Mobility tasks are those combined arms activities that mitigate the effects of obstacles to enable freedom
     of movement and maneuver (ATP 3-90.4). They are conducted to enable friendly forces to move and
     maneuver freely on the battlefield. They heavily rely on intelligence gathered from surveillance and
     reconnaissance. The six primary tasks involved in mobility operations are:

          • Conducting breaching.
          • Conducting area and route clearing.
          • Conducting gap crossings.
          • Constructing and maintaining combat roads and trails.
          • Constructing and maintaining forward airfields and LZ.
          • Conducting traffic management and control.

     3-7. Military police primarily focus on supporting the first three primary tasks and the last primary task of
     conducting traffic management and control. These four tasks provide a tactical framework for military police
     support in mobility operations.

BREACHING

     3-8. A breach is a synchronized combined arms activity under the control of the maneuver commander
     conducted to allow maneuver through an obstacle (ATP 3-90.4). Breaching starts when friendly forces
     identify an obstacle and implement breaching fundamentals. It concludes when control is transferred from
     the breaching unit to follow-on forces. The breaching process involves reducing obstacles and may require
     support from combat engineers.

     3-9. Obstacle breaching is the employment of a combination of tactics and techniques to advance an attacking
     force to the far side of an obstacle that is covered by fire. Most combined arms breaching is carried out by a
     BCT or a battalion size task force (TF) as a tactical mission. However, higher echelons may also perform
     operational-level combined arms breaching tasks. Details about combined arms breaching operations can be
     found in ATP 3-90.4.

     3-10. There are three types of breaching operations: the deliberate breach, the hasty breach, and the covert
     breach. Amphibious breaching is an adaptation of the deliberate breach, and an in-stride breach is a specific
     type of hasty breach. Basic information includes the following:

          • Deliberate breach. A deliberate breach involves the systematic planning and execution of a lane
                through a barrier or fortification. This type of breach is utilized against strong defenses or complex
                obstacle systems. It resembles a deliberate attack and requires in-depth knowledge of defense and
                obstacle systems. A deliberate breach is characterized by the extensive prior planning, preparation,
                and buildup of combat power on the near side of the obstacles. Subordinate units are organized into
                TFs to accomplish the breach. Additionally, securing the far side of the obstacle with an assault
                force often occurs before or during the reduction of the barrier.

          • Hasty breach. A hasty breach is the creation of lanes through enemy minefields by expedient
                methods such as blasting with demolitions, pushing rollers or disabled vehicles through the
                minefields when the time factor does not permit detailed reconnaissance, deliberate breaching, or
                bypassing the obstacle (JP 3-15). A hasty breach is an adaptation of a deliberate breach carried out
                when time is limited. It can occur during a deliberate or hasty attack, especially when there is
                uncertainty about enemy obstacles or when the enemy situation changes, such as the deployment of
                scatterable mines.

          • Covert breach. Covert breaching involves creating pathways through minefields or other obstacles
                in a way that is planned and executed without detection by the adversary. The main purpose of this
                tactic is to remove obstacles stealthily, allowing maneuver forces to pass through undetected. A
                covert breach is carried out when surprise is essential or advantageous, especially in situations with
                limited visibility and terrain that provide opportunities to eliminate enemy obstacles without being
                observed. This approach combines elements of both deliberate and hasty breaching as needed.

46  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                    Support to Mobility

3-11. Military police support for breaching operations is crucial in ensuring a smooth and organized flow of
forces into and through the breach area. While the primary focus of breaching operations is typically on
supporting maneuver elements, military police concentrate on facilitating movement within these maneuvers.
The support provided by military police during breaching operations (deliberate, hasty, or covert) depends
on an analysis of the mission variables. At a minimum, military police support typically includes—

     • Establishing traffic control posts (TCPs) along routes leading to and from the breach lanes to
          expedite traffic flow.

     • Setting up holding areas.
     • Collaborating with engineers to deploy temporary route signage.
     • Conducting straggler-control activities.
     • Performing area and local security measures to assist the passage of follow-on forces.
     • Expanding area and local security around the reduction area once the assault force has moved

          through.
     • Facilitating the transfer of security and movement control of routes and lanes to follow-on forces.

3-12. The commander of the force conducting the breaching operation organizes into three forces to conduct
breaching–support, breach, and assault forces. Military police typically play a role in the breach force (part
of the security element) or the support force during a breaching operation. Table 3-1 outlines the
responsibilities of each organization involved in the breach. Within the breach force, the military police focus
on facilitating the passage of the assault force and follow-on forces. As with other types of operations,
military police ensure that DCs do not interfere with breaching activities or impede movement throughout
the breach area. In their role within the support force, military police concentrate on assisting follow-on
forces. Regardless of the specific role, their primary responsibility is to ensure smooth movement into and
out of the breach lanes. The involvement of military police is closely tied to the echelon conducting the
breach and the number of lanes needed to accommodate the vehicles passing through. Military police units
maintain close coordination with the commanders of the support, breach, and assault forces and the breaching
force commander executing the operation.

                 Table 3-1. Support, breach, and assault forces responsibilities.

  Breach Organization                                          Responsibilities
Support force
Breach force           • Suppress enemies that can target the reduction area with direct fire. This
                       will protect the breach force as they work to clear the obstacle and the
Assault force          assault force as they move through the created passage.
                       • Fix enemy forces to isolate the reduction area.
                       • Control obscuration.

                       • Reduce, proof, and mark the necessary lanes through the obstacle.
                       • Report the status and location of the created lanes.
                       • Ensure that local security is on the near and far sides of the obstacle.
                       • Offer additional suppression of enemies overwatching the obstacle.
                       • Provide further obscuration in the reduction area.
                       • Assist the assault force in passing through the created lanes.

                       • Secure the objective on the far side.
                       • Eliminate enemy protective obstacles.
                       • Create clear routes from the reduction area to the battle handover line for
                       follow-on forces.
                       • Prevent the enemy from directing fire at follow-on forces as they move
                       through the created lanes.
                       • Conduct the battle handover with the follow-on forces.
                       • Provide reinforcing fire for the support forces.
                       • Neutralize the enemy on the far side of the obstacle to minimize threats of
                       direct fire in the reduction area.

3-13. The most critical military police support is to provide traffic management for the breach area, where
military police units provide the commander a means to control traffic flow to appropriate cleared lanes.
When multiple lanes diverge from a single far-recognition marker, military police can provide guides or

25 March 2026          ATP 3-39.30  47


---

Chapter 3

     establish TCPs to direct formations to the correct lanes. They also play a role in adjusting traffic flow when
     lanes are closed for maintenance or upgrades. Additionally, military police units allow commanders to
     modify traffic patterns in response to the tactical situation, including managing disabled vehicles or artillery
     fires.

     3-14. Figure 3-1 provides an example of breaching operations. In this example, the first BCT conducts a
     breaching operation to seize OBJECTIVE DOG and pass the second BCT along ROUTES AUSTIN and
     DALLAS. The breach force (1-52 Combined Arms Battalion) creates breach lanes 2 and 3 to allow the assault
     force (1-50 Combined Arms Battalion) to seize OBJECTIVE DOG. After the far side objective is seized, the
     breach force creates an additional lane (Lane 1) to allow the second BCT to pass through the breach area
     along two passage routes to continue to the attack. Typically, as shown in this example, military police are
     assigned to the security element within the breach force to provide local security for the reduction element
     and guide the assault force to the far-recognition markers. They are poised to reroute traffic based on changes
     in the tactical situation. Military police efforts on the nearside of the breach area are focused on directing the
     second BCT and other follow-on forces toward the routes leading up to the reduction area and optimizing
     traffic flow up to and through the breach lanes to maintain momentum. Engineers in the breach force create
     and mark the lanes through the obstacles to get the assault force through. While not necessarily being
     collocated with the breach force, military police provide traffic control through the reduction area. The
     military police force may remain under BCT control while doing this. Military police may also be organized
     within the assault force and be able to rapidly establish traffic control on the far side of the obstacle. This
     example depicts the establishment of two lanes by the breach force—providing sufficient passage for the
     assault force of the first BCT. Follow-on engineers would likely improve ROUTES AUSTIN and DALLAS,
     and military police would potentially adjust the number of TCPs necessary to support this. Follow-on
     engineers may also open more lanes to facilitate the passage of follow-on forces with a corresponding
     requirement for military police support for movement control. The first BCT is responsible for route
     maintenance and traffic control throughout the breach area until they are handed off to follow-on forces.
