                                                                                                                           Support to Mobility

CLEARING

     3-15. Clearing is a mobility (focused on movement) task performed by follow-on engineers and EOD that
     involves the total elimination or neutralization of an obstacle not normally done under fire (ATP 3-90.4).
     Commanders may order clearing tasks to enhance mobility within an AO based on an analysis of mission
     variables. They may also authorize a critical route or area clearance to eliminate explosive hazards like mines
     or other obstacles. These operations can be executed as a single mission to open or reopen a route or area or
     conducted regularly to counter ongoing threats to a critical route. Clearing operations are typically carried
     out by a combined arms force built around an engineer-based force, which may include MWDs.

     3-16. Clearing in the context of this description is not directly related to the tactical task of clear. Military
     police support for clearing operations primarily focuses on route clearance missions. Route clearance
     involves detecting, investigating, marking, reporting, and neutralizing or disposing of explosive hazards and
     other obstacles along a designated route to enable assured mobility for the maneuver commander. This
     operation is a combined arms effort that relies on reconnaissance of the route to be cleared. Route clearance
     can be conducted to open a route for necessary traffic or on a recurring basis to reduce the risk along selected
     routes. Typically, route clearance teams consist of engineers, EOD, a security element (infantry, military
     police, or an aviation unit), medical personnel, and a special operations team. MWDs may be used during the
     detection phase of the route clearance operation.

     3-17. Military police support for route clearance generally focuses on two key areas: providing
     reconnaissance information about routes in the AO and ensuring security for the detection and clearing teams.
     This security involves monitoring oncoming traffic for potential threats, identifying hazards or obstacles
     along the route, containing suspect vehicles identified by other elements of the route-clearing team, and
     managing traffic control. The two methods of route clearance are contiguous and combat. Each method
     carries risks, so conducting a thorough analysis of mission variables is essential before deciding which
     clearance method, or combination of methods, to use. Understanding these dynamics is crucial for ensuring
     successful and safe operations in the AO:

          • Contiguous. In contiguous clearance, the deliberate process begins at Checkpoint 1 and is
                completed at Checkpoint 2 (see figure 3-2, page 50). This method provides the best assurance for
                route clearance. However, while effective, it may not be the most secure option in a high-threat
                environment. The support provided by military police during contiguous route clearance is primarily
                focused on offering reconnaissance information about routes within the AO and security for
                detection and clearing assets. Commanders can also integrate patrol explosive-detection dogs or
                patrol explosive-detection dogs-enhanced MWD teams to search routes and roadways of hazards.

          • Combat. While contiguous clearance focuses on a specific route, combat clearance targets specific
                points (see figure 3-3, page 50). As previously mentioned, the IPOE or running estimates can
                identify locations where the enemy is most likely to place obstacles or set up ambush positions.
                These areas become designated as named areas of interest or objectives for combat clearance
                missions. The combat clearance method divides a route into sections based on the number of
                suspected high-threat areas. Once the clearance team clears these sections, the route is considered
                secure. In this example, military police may serve as the security element. During the sweep, the
                security element positions itself to best overwatch the activities of the detection, clearance, and
                improvement teams as they carry out their tasks. Depending on the situation, the security element
                may be positioned at the front, flank, or rear of these teams. As part of the security force, military
                police are also prepared to respond to CIVs to prevent interference with the clearance mission. This
                may involve diverting CIV traffic or establishing a temporary checkpoint or roadblock. The clearing
                element may include a specialized search dog or a patrol explosive detection dog team.

                                                                                                                           Support to Mobility

GAP CROSSING

     3-18. A gap is a ravine, mountain pass, river, or other terrain feature that presents an obstacle that may be
     bridged (ATP 3-90.4). Gaps exist in the operational environment, creating significant challenges for
     movement and maneuver. A gap crossing is the projection of combat power across a linear obstacle (wet or
     dry gap) (ATP 3-90.4). (Gap crossing operations that support both maneuver and movement are discussed in
     detail in ATP 3‑90.4.) Like breaching operations, there are three types of gap crossings: deliberate, hasty,
     and covert.

     3-19. Gap crossings require units to move in column formations along specific routes that converge at the
     crossing sites. Military police units are crucial in helping the commander manage traffic at these gap-crossing
     sites and along the routes leading to and out of the crossing area. They aim to ensure that units cross the gap
     quickly and efficiently to maintain momentum. Controlling traffic is essential to keep the crossing plan
     flexible, allowing for adjustments in response to changes in the situation that may affect the routing and
     sequencing of crossing units. Effective traffic control helps prevent congestion and the pooling of vehicles,
     which can become lucrative targets for artillery and air strikes.

     3-20. The crossing operation is typically planned and executed by the headquarters overseeing the activity.
     A division gap-crossing operation is conducted by a joint force commander or corps, depending on the
     structure of the area of responsibility. A BCT deliberate crossing is controlled by a division or corps,
     depending on how the area of responsibility is structured. During a BCT crossing, the military police assets
     assigned to the BCT may also cross to ensure continuous support after being relieved by subsequent military
     police units. In these situations, there is usually a reliance on an engineer headquarters within the area of
     responsibility to assist with the crossing. Commanders conducting a deliberate gap crossing generally
     organize their forces into mobility units and assault, bridgehead, and breakout forces as follows:

          • Mobility units, including combat engineer companies, mobility augmentation companies, bridge
                companies, military police, and CBRN units, provide essential support for crossing operations. They
                ensure traffic control and obscuration and offer crossing means. These supporting units are task-
                organized to perform specific tasks and are controlled using specified procedures that are clear,
                simple, and rehearsed by all elements to ensure responsive support of the plan and unity of command
                and effort.

          • Assault forces seize the far-side objective to eliminate direct fire threats at crossing sites.
          • Bridgehead forces assault across a gap to secure the enemy side, creating a bridgehead to allow the

                buildup and passage of a breakout force during gap crossing. The bridgehead is an area on the enemy
                side of a linear obstacle large enough to accommodate most of the crossing force. It has suitable
                terrain for defending the crossing sites, protects the crossing forces from enemy direct fire, and
                serves as a base for continuing the attack. The bridgehead line marks the limit of the objective area
                during the establishment of the bridgehead.
          • Breakout forces attack to seize objectives beyond the bridgehead as part of the ongoing offensive
                operations.

     3-21. The PM section is responsible for developing the traffic control plan. The mission variables influence
     the employment of military police in gap-crossing operations. The size of the crossing force determines the
     number and placement of military police units supporting a gap-crossing operation, the direction of the
     crossing (forward or retrograde), the level of expected enemy resistance, and the number of DCs that may be
     encountered. Military police units are placed where they can best expedite and enforce movement tables
     along the routes leading into the crossing area.

     3-22. The main effort of military police during gap-crossing operations is to manage the immediate crossing
     site and the routes leading to and from it. They direct crossing units to their designated locations using holding
     and staging areas, TCPs, and temporary signage (and subsequent signage). This helps units move through the
     area according to the established crossing and traffic circulation plans. Effective management is crucial since
     there are typically a limited number of crossing sites, especially for river crossings. Military police also
     conduct mobile patrols along primary routes to control traffic, identify problems, guide and escort vehicles,
     and reroute traffic when necessary. In most gap-crossing operations, TCPs are established on both sides of
     the gap to improve communication and coordination among the units.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  51


---

Chapter 3

     3-23. Support from military police units during gap crossing significantly reduces crossing time and promotes
     the efficient movement of vehicles. This support helps minimize congestion, accelerates the crossing process,
     and allows crossing units to maintain momentum. Military police units may establish collection points or
     holding areas to temporarily secure DCs and detainees until they can be relocated to a holding area of a higher
     echelon. This ensures that these individuals do not impede the gap-crossing operation. Depending on the
     traffic volume and the number of detainees and DCs in the area, additional military police support from a
     higher military police headquarters may be necessary. In challenging terrain, military police platoons and
     squads may operate defiles (specific circulation control measures designed to maintain smooth traffic flow
     through narrow passageways [see FM 3-39]). Additionally, military police can provide area security to the
     rear and flanks of crossing forces to enhance security. See chapter 3 and ATP 3-39.4 for more information.

     3-24. Military police units that operate within the crossing areas are usually under the operational control of
     the crossing area commander for the duration of the operation. Military police units outside the crossing area
     fall under the command of its appropriate echelon commander.

     3-25. Figure 3-4 provides an example of military police support during a gap crossing to facilitate traffic
     control. This scenario corresponds to Phase I (Advance to the Gap) of a deliberate gap crossing. While
     military police can also be integrated into assault, bridgehead, or breakout forces, this example focuses on
     military police assigned to mobility units. Those military police who are either part of or attached to other
     units will carry out missions or tasks specific to those units. For detailed information on the types of activities
     military police may perform at holding areas, staging areas, and TCPs, and for an example of a deliberate
     wet gap crossing, see ATP 3-90.4.

    JAN      January                    MP           military police

    CFA      call forward area          PL           phase line

    EEP      engineer equipment park    RL           release line

    ERP      engineer regulating point  TCP          traffic control post

    3-26. Retrograde gap crossings have the potential to be more difficult than forward crossings, particularly
    when under pressure from enemy forces. ATP 3-90.4 outlines specific planning considerations and
    techniques to keep in mind when faced with a substantial number of DCs.

52                                      ATP 3-39.30                            25 March 2026

                            Support to Mobility

PASSAGE OF LINES

     3-27. While not a primary task of mobility operations, support for a passage of lines is often associated with
     military support to mobility operations. During a passage of lines, forces move either forward or rearward
     through the combat positions of another force with the intent to engage or disengage with the enemy. This
     high-risk operation requires careful coordination between the passing unit, the stationary unit, and supporting
     forces. A passage of lines serves as both a tactical enabling task and a tactical shaping task. For further details,
     see ADP 3-90.

     3-28. Military police units support the passage of lines by minimizing confusion and congestion for units
     entering and exiting the passage area. They manage and enforce traffic in the vicinity of passage points and
     passage lanes, ensuring that passing units have priority for using the routes through these areas. The
     headquarters directing the operation sets the priority of route use.

     3-29. Before the operation, military police units assigned to support the passage of lines conduct route
     reconnaissance. They become familiar with the routes to, through, and beyond the area of passage. This
     preparation allows military police units to enhance the commander’s command and control by providing
     directions at passage points and guiding units through the passage lanes. Traffic control measures may
     include TCPs, temporary route signs, checkpoints, roadblocks, defiles, and escort or guide vehicles. Military
     police must also be ready to establish vehicle holding areas at designated locations along the movement
     routes. Vehicles will be directed to a holding area if the road network is damaged until traffic can be restored
     or rerouted.

     3-30. Maintaining unit integrity and minimizing the number of stragglers is crucial for sustaining momentum
     during a forward passage of lines. Military police units perform aggressive operations to manage stragglers
     and DCs, ensuring that individuals are returned to positive control. This prevents enemy personnel from
     blending in with DCs and potentially infiltrating the sustainment area. Military police support during the
     passage of lines is essential and requires detailed coordination with both passing and stationary units, to
     include—

          • Communication requirements.
          • Recognition signals.
          • Starting points for routes.
          • Times of passage.
          • Designated passing lanes.
          • Control measures, which include TCPs, escort and guide vehicles, and temporary route signs.

                (Temporary route signs can reduce the number of TCPs needed; however, if routes are not clearly
                defined or if they intersect congested areas, expect to provide TCPs or escort vehicles.)
          • Procedures for detainee and DC evacuations.

MAIN AND ALTERNATE SUPPLY ROUTE REGULATION AND
ENFORCEMENT

     3-31. MSR and ASR regulation and enforcement (see ATP 3-90.4) involves military police support for the
     mobility operations task of traffic management and control. This is integrated with the logistics focused
     portion of movement control (see ATP 4-16) and includes the military police activities of traffic management
     and enforcement (see ATP 3-39.10 and ATP 3-39.12-1). Traffic management and control is conducted to
     facilitate the unobstructed movement of personnel and resources along road networks as efficiently as
     possible. While primarily focused on supporting movement, traffic management can also be selectively
     applied to enable maneuver. Additionally, traffic management and control contribute to the commander’s
     protection efforts. Reconnaissance is a crucial part of effective traffic management and control. This process
     holistically incorporates transportation, military police, engineer, and other technical capabilities. Four key
     activities that support main and alternate route regulation and enforcement are route reconnaissance, traffic
     control plan development, DC control measures (see ATP 3-57.10), and straggler control. These activities
     often overlap and are interconnected, even as each fulfills its specific responsibilities.

     3-32. Traffic management and enforcement and MSR/ASR regulation and enforcement encompass all active
     and passive measures used to control traffic circulation, enforce traffic regulations, investigate traffic
     collisions, and enable the safe movement of both vehicles and pedestrians. Traffic management and

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  53


---

Chapter 3

     enforcement prioritize reducing disruptions caused by various threats, DCs, and congestion arising from
     breakdowns, adverse weather conditions, and the deterioration of road surfaces. Military police enforce route
     regulations consistent with the traffic control plan.

     3-33. Traffic management and enforcement activities are an essential part of police operations. These
     activities include controlling traffic flow, enforcing traffic regulations, and investigating traffic incidents.
     The technical skills in these areas enhance the ability of military police to effectively carry out their tactical
     missions during MSR and ASR regulation and enforcement.

     3-34. Military police units provide MSR and ASR regulation and enforcement to enhance movement and
     maneuver. Their goal is to keep these routes clear for priority tactical and sustainment operations within
     controlled areas. Additionally, military police units support the command MSR regulation measures outlined
     in the traffic regulation plan. This plan includes specific strategies to ensure the smooth and efficient use of
     the road network. It designates military route numbers or names, indicates the direction of travel, establishes
     highway regulation points, and identifies preplanned military police TCPs.

     3-35. To improve traffic flow on MSRs, military police units apply special circulation control measures, such
     as—

          • Temporary route signing.
          • Static posts such as TCPs, roadblocks, checkpoints, holding areas, or defiles at critical points.
          • Mobile patrols between static posts.
          • Traffic and road conditions monitoring.

     3-36. Military police units collect information on military and police activities concerning friendly and
     enemy forces. They assist stranded vehicles and crews and set up temporary route signs to warn of hazards
     or guide drivers unfamiliar with the routes. Through these actions, military police units maintain control over
     the road network in the AO and coordinate with the HN, when possible, to expedite movement on MSRs.

     3-37. Military police units are employed with engineer, logistics, EOD, aviation, and other forces to secure
     military traffic movement through vulnerable areas. Commanders may gradually implement additional
     protection measures to safeguard movement along a particular area or route in response to increasing threats.
     According to published movement tables, supporting protection forces will establish and maintain a safe
     passage route through uncontrolled terrain. Opening this route requires a coordinated effort, with each branch
     contributing unique movement and mobility expertise. The process typically involves route clearance and
     maintenance activities integrated with area security measures along the corridor.

     3-38. Engineer and military police forces conduct route reconnaissance missions to identify issues along the
     route. Based on the tactical situation, sustainment forces may establish temporary holding, maintenance, or
     rest areas along the corridor. Once military police establish TCPs and provide convoy escorts for critical
     supplies, and with aviation convoy security in place, the convoys can safely travel along the protected route
     to their final destination. The protected route may be opened or closed for specific periods to meet movement
     table requirements.

MARKING ROUTES

     3-39. Route signs support the traffic regulation plan by accurately identifying specific routes. Well-placed
     signs along the roadside help drivers reach their destinations. Military police patrols regularly monitor these
     signs, checking their condition and creating specific signs before critical moves. When military police patrols
     find signs damaged, destroyed, or displaced due to weather, sabotage, or combat activities, they must quickly
     create temporary replacements. (For additional information on route signs, see appendix D.) This protocol
     also applies when patrols encounter immediate and temporary obstructions, such as blown bridges or CBRN
     contamination along MSRs. Military police Soldiers quickly construct and erect signs to—

          • Identify routes.
          • Guide traffic around problem areas.
          • Provide directions, distances, and general information.

ESTABLISHING AND OPERATING A TRAFFIC CONTROL POST

     3-40. Military police conduct security and mobility support to expedite military traffic flow by operating
     TCPs at critical locations. A traffic control post is a manned post that is used to preclude the interruption of

54  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                           Support to Mobility

     traffic flow or movement along a designated route (FM 3-39). The location of each TCP is given in the traffic
     control plan.

     3-41. Military police enforce the circulation control of traffic movement and MSR regulations—their focus
     is not to apprehend violators. Their tasks may include directing military personnel to their units or guiding
     them to a collection point for stragglers if the location of their unit is unknown. They also maintain
     surveillance of friendly movements; provide reports as needed; manage DCs; and monitor for insurgents,
     conventional enemy forces, and enemy aircraft activities. Additionally, they screen suspicious individuals
     according to standard operating procedures (SOP), area ROE, and commander's guidance. When operating a
     TCP, military police also monitor local inhabitants. They ensure that local CIV traffic and DCs do not delay
     military movement. CIV traffic and DCs should be rerouted or temporarily stopped until the MSR is clear
     for military traffic. When establishing and operating a TCP, the military police—

          • Establish overwatch for the TCP.
          • Maintain reliable communication.
          • Select positions for squad members and assigned crew-served weapons by—

                 Positioning one team to direct traffic flow and control movement on the MSR.
                 Positioning another team to provide overwatch security for the military police conducting

                     movement control.
                 Rotating primary overwatch security duties between the teams in the squad.
                 Rotating responsibilities with the military police conducting movement control on the MSR.
          • Direct and prepare concealed defensive positions, ensuring cover and concealment, good fields of
                fire, and effective communications based on the METT-TC(I) framework. See FM 3-39 for military
                police perspectives on each mission variable.
          • Maintain situational awareness.
          • Reconnoiter the surrounding area for enemy activity.
          • Record key information about incidents and notify the chain of command according to the unit SOP.

     3-42. Military police units are responsible for ensuring that authorized traffic only uses classified routes
     within their timetable schedule. They set up TCPs to prevent vehicles from traveling on roads that are either
     too narrow for their passage or unable to support their weight. This helps to avoid obstruction on the route.
     Many of the skills employed by military police in managing traffic also apply to supporting operations such
     as breaching, clearing, and gap-crossing movements, including activities such as establishing TCPs,
     managing defiles, and setting up temporary route signage. While their primary focus is facilitating movement,
     these skills also support maneuver.

ESTABLISHING AND OPERATING A HOLDING AREA

     3-43. Forces utilize holding areas during traffic interruptions or when deploying from an aerial port or seaport
     of embarkation. These holding areas can serve as independent measures or be combined with other measures,
     such as defiles or checkpoints, to facilitate various operations like gap crossings or the passage of lines. For
     example, a holding area might be set up at each end of a defile to reduce congestion, or it may be implemented
     alongside a roadblock or checkpoint for vehicle inspections. It is essential that holding areas provide
     concealment and the ability to disperse the vehicles stationed within them.

     3-44. The number of military police teams required to operate a holding area is determined by METT-TC(I)
     and the size of the area. Typically, a military police squad operates a holding area. The squad leader assigns
     teams for specific tasks: one team controls the entrance, another team controls the exit, and a third team
     provides security. A fourth team provides additional overwatch, assists with traffic direction and parking,
     and carries out supplementary tasks assigned by the squad leader. The team leader also assigns each squad
     member a specific fighting position. For larger holding areas, the military police leader may need to deploy
     additional personnel to manage traffic and parking, ensuring all units adhere to the designated flow plan.

ESTABLISHING AND OPERATING A DEFILE

     3-45. Military police operate defiles when natural or man-made obstacles impede traffic flow on an MSR. A
     defile is a specific traffic control measure implemented to ensure the smooth movement of vehicles through
     a narrow passage.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  55


---

Chapter 3

     3-46. Defile operations prevent traffic jams by allowing vehicles to move in only one direction at a time.
     When setting up or operating a defile, it is important to remain vigilant for potential enemy attacks, as the
     nature of a defile can make it vulnerable. Tasks that are associated with establishing and operating a defile
     include—

          • Preparing support-by-fire positions and, if necessary, fighting positions.
          • Controlling traffic flow entering and leaving the defile at both ends.
          • Maintaining security throughout the defile.
          • Informing road users about traffic and tactical situations.

     3-47. Before establishing a defile, conduct a reconnaissance of the area and the proposed location. Use the
     simplest control method available and plan for an alternative method if a backup is needed. Control measures
     can include the following:

          • Flags. A flag is given to the last driver or attached to the last vehicle entering the defile. Another
                military police Soldier removes the flag when the vehicle reaches the end of the defile, signaling for
                traffic to begin moving in the opposite direction.

          • Riders. The rider method is similar to the flag method; however, the control person rides in the last
                vehicle of each group. This approach ensures that all vehicles have cleared the area.

          • Lead vehicles. The lead vehicle guides the convoy through the defile. This method is beneficial
                when navigating through a long or confusing defile.

          • Trail vehicles. The trail vehicle method follows the same principles as the flag and rider methods;
                however, a trail vehicle is placed directly behind the last vehicle passing through the defile.

          • Visuals. Control personnel are stationed where they can easily see and signal each other at both
                ends of the defile. Hand-and-arm signals or signaling devices, such as flashlights, are used for
                communication. This technique is typically employed in smaller defile operations, where holding
                areas are generally unnecessary.

          • Communication. Radios should be used as a last resort for communication.

     3-48. Additional tasks include—
          • Establishing a temporary or static defense based on how long the defile will be in use.
          • Utilizing temporary signs to mark alternate or bypass routes. These signs should be placed well in
                advance to allow vehicles to avoid the defile where possible, thus minimizing the need for additional
                personnel.
          • Controlling access to the defile to prevent large numbers of vehicles and personnel from entering at
                the same time.
          • Developing procedures for clearing the road in the event of breakdowns. This may require
                coordinating wrecker support; if wrecker support is unavailable, field methods should be employed.

     3-49. Large defile operations require a holding area at both ends of the defile. These holding areas should
     provide cover and concealment for vehicles and troops and must be spacious enough to accommodate the
     expected number of vehicles using the route. In scenarios where a nuclear airburst is possible, these holding
     areas should be 2 to 3 kilometers (1.24 to 1.86 miles) away from the defile. See figure 3-5 for a depiction of
     a defile operation.

    ALT      alternate supply route  MSR          main supply route

    JAN

                                                                                                                           Support to Mobility

ROUTE RECONNAISSANCE

     3-50. Military police units conduct hasty and deliberate route reconnaissance to gather information about
     routes and surrounding terrain where enemy activity could impact troop movement. Route reconnaissance
     involves the ongoing monitoring of MSRs and ASRs and crucial terrain features along these routes. The
     information collected is reported to the tactical commander. Military police patrols look for obstacles such
     as difficult terrain, weather effects on the routes, route damage, CBRN contamination, and the presence or
     absence of enemy forces.

     3-51. When enemy activity is spotted, military police patrols report the observations, maintain surveillance,
     and respond in alignment with the commander’s plan and intent. To gather information for proposed traffic
     plans, military police units assess the type and number of available routes, load classifications, route widths,
     obstructions, and restrictions. This information is crucial for the commander’s situational awareness and for
     developing a common operational picture. Route reconnaissance may be conducted as part of a
     multifunctional team that includes engineer, CBRN, and other specialties. For more details on route, area,
     and zone reconnaissance, see ATP 3-34.81 and FM 3-90.

TRAFFIC CONTROL PLAN DEVELOPMENT

     3-52. Movement control measures are supported by a traffic control plan that outlines military police support
     in managing the use of MSRs and ASRs. This plan includes details on checkpoints, rest and refueling areas,
     TCPs, highway regulation points, and mobile patrols. It identifies the major routes that will handle most of
     the traffic and specifies restrictions such as direction of travel, size and weight limits, and critical points along
     the routes.

     3-53. Military police create a traffic control plan to complement the movement control and highway
     regulation plan. This plan includes traffic enforcement measures that uphold movement control and highway
     traffic regulations. It focuses on speed control, sets up safety inspection checkpoints to protect personnel, and
     ensures that only authorized vehicles use designated routes. The traffic control plan provides specific
     measures to ensure the efficient use of the road network, including route designations, restrictions, priority
     of movement, direction of travel, highway regulation points, and preplanned military police TCPs. The traffic
     control plan supports the task of providing movement control. For further details, see ATP 4-16, which
     discusses this and other control measures. See GTA 90-01-005 for insights on TCP operations.

     3-54. Critical points in the transportation network may include facilities, terminals, ports, railheads, and cargo
     transfer points. If these areas become congested, they can significantly limit the efficiency and effectiveness
     of the entire network. The traffic control plan aims to address high-volume traffic conditions and mitigate or
     prevent excessive congestion on highways, major routes, and urban streets, including secondary routes and
     residential streets.

DISLOCATED CIVILIAN CONTROL

     3-55. DC operations are a specific category of PRC (see chapter 5 and ATP 3-57.10). The primary objectives
     of DC operations are to minimize CIV interference with military operations while ensuring their protection
     from the impacts of combat and natural or man-made disasters. Military police units responsible for traffic
     regulation and enforcement on designated routes may encounter DCs, potentially hindering military traffic
     flow. These units prioritize military movement by redirecting DCs away from MSRs and other critical areas,
     directing them instead to designated refugee routes or DC camps. They deny the movement of CIVs whose
     location, direction, or actions threaten their safety, hinder tactical operations, or compromise sustainment
     operations. When the HN government is operational, it is responsible for identifying safe routes for the
     movement of DCs out of an AO. Military police units may assist CA and HN assets in redirecting DCs to
     alternative routes as needed. The control of DCs may focus on tactical routes to support maneuver operations
     or on MSRs and ASRs to facilitate movement during mobility operations that are typically focused on the
     support area.

     3-56. Effective planning for managing DC populations must incorporate bottom-up and top-down
     information to ensure that the plan is viable and efficient. Strategies may involve blocking, clearing, or
     collecting to minimize disruptions caused by DCs, as discussed in chapter 5.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  57


---

Chapter 3

     3-57. Military police and other U.S. forces do not take control of DCs unless requested by the HN or when
     operating in an environment where the government is hostile or nonfunctional. When the joint force
     commander or geographic CCDR assumes responsibility, military police units coordinate with CA elements
     to establish TCPs at critical points along the route. These points help direct DCs to secondary roadways and
     areas not occupied by military forces. When necessary, DCs may be housed in camps managed by U.S. forces
     and supported by military police. Ideally, HN authorities handle mass DC operations by executing planned
     and rehearsed evacuation strategies. If a military force takes responsibility for planning DC operations, the
     planners should consider incorporating HN resources into the planning and implementation processes.
     Figure 3-6 provides an example of an overlay for a DC collection plan supporting an offensive operation.
     The plans encompass collection points, designated DC routes (and time windows), assembly areas or holding
     areas, and the anticipated location of DC camps. For more information on DC camps, see appendix B; for
     additional details on DC planning and techniques, see ATP 3-57.10.

STRAGGLER CONTROL

    3-58. Straggler control refers to the operations to manage friendly forces that have become separated from
    their commands due to events in the AO. Military police units carry out straggler control by using mobile
    patrols, TCPs, and checkpoint teams to reunite stragglers with their parent units. Most stragglers are simply
    Soldiers who have been separated from their command due to a tactical operation. Forced marches and
    movements may lead to disabled vehicles and emerging stragglers. Military police units direct Soldiers back
    to their parent unit or a replacement unit according to command policies. They also provide basic first aid
    and initiate medical evacuation for wounded stragglers.

    3-59. Military police units can establish special posts and collection points for managing stragglers following
    CBRN hazards or major enemy actions that lead to many lost or confused military personnel. Mobile military
    police patrols operate between these posts to direct and collect stragglers. If many stragglers are present in
    the AO, designated straggler collection points may be necessary. When multinational forces are present in
    the theater, each nation will set up its own collection point for its personnel. Military police units must be
    aware of where allied straggler collection points are located and assist allied Soldiers in returning to their
    respective commands. They utilize available theater transportation assets to move stragglers from TCPs and
    checkpoints to the main straggler collection points. Stragglers are screened and sorted at these collection

58                 ATP 3-39.30                                          25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                     Support to Mobility

points for further action, including transport to a medical treatment facility, return to their units, or movement
as directed by the controlling headquarters. The establishment of a hasty collection point is highlighted in
figure 3-7. This method supports straggler control by using a quadrant system to facilitate the organized
separation of various activities that may occur at a collection point. Each quadrant of a crossroads can be
designated for a specific group or purpose. Control points should be located 55 to 110 yards (50 to 100
meters) away from the roads to keep different groups adequately separated. Common requirements (food and
water, medical treatment, and protection) can be consolidated by establishing various collection points within
the same vicinity, maximizing the efficient use of available resources.

3-60. Military police units are responsible for reporting information about stragglers they encounter. This
information is compiled by the headquarters of the military police unit and then forwarded through the
appropriate channels to higher command. Information from stragglers with immediate tactical significance
is reported promptly through command channels. Stragglers identified as deserters are placed in detention
channels.

                                                 Chapter 4

                            Support to Security

    Military police support security operations primarily by carrying out area and local security
    tasks. These tasks are the last two of the five subordinate tasks outlined in ADP 3-90 and further
    detailed in FM 3-90. Area and local security tasks focus on protecting friendly forces, assets,
    and operations in an AO, particularly in support areas. In area security, military police play a
    vital role in enhancing the security of the sustainment base and support units by executing
    several key tasks and activities. They are also important contributors to local area security
    efforts, encompassing aspects of AT and physical security. For a comprehensive discussion of
    area and local security, see ADP 3-37.

SECURITY OPERATIONS

     4-1. Security operations are those operations performed by commanders to provide early and accurate
     warning of enemy operations, to provide the forces being protected with time and maneuver space within
     which to react to the enemy, and to develop the situation to allow commanders to effectively use their
     protected forces (ADP 3-90). Security operations consist of five primary tasks—screen, guard, cover, area
     security, and local security. The first three tasks are associated with the movement and maneuver warfighting
     function, while the last two are related to the protection warfighting function. Although the latter tasks
     primarily focus on providing protection, they can also facilitate the movement and maneuver of the force.
     Military police conducting security operations can concentrate on a force, such as during convoy operations,
     or a facility, which may include a base camp, a DC camp, a detention site, or other locations where they are
     responsible for providing security.

     4-2. Military police may also conduct counterreconnaissance in the performance of area and local security as
     a supporting tactical mission task. Counterreconnaissance is a tactical mission task that encompasses all
     measures taken by a unit to counter enemy reconnaissance and surveillance efforts (FM 3-90)
     Counterreconnaissance is not a distinct mission; rather, it is an integral part of all forms of security operations.

     4-3. Military police use their technical skills and engage in specialized activities to support security
     operations. This encompasses a range of tasks, including reconnaissance and surveillance, the use of UAS,
     and the deployment of MWDs.

AREA SECURITY

     4-4. Area security is a type of security operation conducted to protect friendly forces, lines of
     communications, and activities within a specific area (ADP 3-90). It preserves the commander’s freedom to
     move reserves, position fire support means, exercise command and control, and conduct sustaining
     operations.

     4-5. Forces involved in area security are responsible for protecting the force, installations, routes, areas, and
     local security assets and efforts. While area security is crucial for the success of military operations, it is
     typically considered an economy-of-force mission. Its primary aim is to ensure the continuation of
     sustainment operations and to support the essential tasks, purposes, and objectives of an operation by
     generating and maintaining combat power.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  61


---

Chapter 4

     4-6. Area security is a key method for protecting support areas essential for positioning, employing, and
     safeguarding the resources needed to sustain, enable, and control forces. Area security is an effective means
     of providing civil security and maintaining order during stability activities. When engaged in area security,
     forces can saturate a specific area or position on important terrain to offer protection. This includes
     capabilities such as early warning, reconnaissance, surveillance, preemptive attacks, and defense against
     surprise enemy assaults. Early warning, reconnaissance, and surveillance data can be gathered from a variety
     of sources depending on the operational environment, including—

          • Space-based sensors. Satellites offer a broad view of areas and can detect large-scale movements
                or activities.

          • Ground-based sensors. Ground-based sensors include radar systems and acoustic sensors that
                provide localized information.

          • UAS. UASs deliver flexible and continuous surveillance capabilities, supplying real-time imagery
                and data.

          • Aviation assets. Manned aircraft, both fixed-wing and rotary, offer various reconnaissance options,
                from high-altitude imagery to close air support.

          • Maritime assets. Ships increase maritime domain awareness by tracking vessels and monitoring
                coastal regions.

             Note. Military police units have a specific function and typically do not possess the independent
             resources needed to fully understand the complexities of their operating area. They must
             collaborate and share information with other friendly forces to gain a comprehensive
             understanding of the area.

     4-7. Area security primarily focuses on named areas of interest to answer CCIR, confirm or deny threat
     intentions, and aid in decision making. The maneuver enhancement brigade, along with certain military police
     units, is specially equipped and trained to conduct area security operations, which may be the only force
     available during certain phases of an operation. Additionally, area security benefits from the local security
     measures performed by all units, regardless of their location in the AO. Military police forces carry out area
     security missions emphasizing mobility, lethality, and communication capabilities.

     4-8. During offense, defense, and stability operations, various military organizations may conduct area
     security in an economy-of-force role to counter enemy reconnaissance and surveillance efforts. This approach
     also establishes standoff distances from enemy direct and indirect fire systems; enhances movement and
     maneuver; and protects convoys, collection points, supply points, and other critical fixed sites. Additionally,
     it protects LOCs without significantly diverting combat power. In DSCA, many of the same tasks may be
     performed, but they typically do not occur in an economy-of-force role or against the same type of enemy.
     Bases and base camps implement local security measures, including EOD, assessments and
     recommendations, random AT measures, and increased force protection levels. However, they may still be
     vulnerable to remnants of enemy forces, necessitating a response that exceeds the capabilities of the base
     camp. Area security supports offensive operations by providing a response capability to base clusters and
     sustainment areas and to designated geographical locations such as routes, bridge sites, or lodgments.
     Commanders perform area damage control (ADC) in support areas to prevent and respond to adverse effects
     from enemy actions that could diminish combat power.

     4-9. For a discussion on critical asset security and the methodology involved in vulnerability assessments for
     units, installations, facilities, and base camps, see ADP 3-37. This reference includes the staff planning
     required to develop a critical asset list, followed by the identification of supporting assets for those designated
     on the defended asset list. The general sequence of a vulnerability assessment is—

          • Step 1. List assets and capabilities along with the threats against them.
          • Step 2. Determine the common criteria for assessing vulnerabilities.
          • Step 3. Evaluate the vulnerability of assets and capabilities.

     4-10. All commanders apportion combat power and assign resources for protection tasks and systems by
     analyzing the operational environment, evaluating the likelihood of threats, and determining the relative
     value of friendly resources and populations. Based on their assessments, joint force commanders may
     designate the Army to appoint a joint security coordinator responsible for specific joint security areas. While

62  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                           Support to Security

     all resources hold value, an analysis of mission variables may reveal that certain resources, assets, or locations
     are more critical to the success of the mission from both enemy/adversary and friendly perspectives.
     Commanders rely on risk management, which includes threat assessments; critical asset evaluations; and
     vulnerability analyses of units, installations, facilities, and bases or base camps. This information helps
     facilitate decision making, provide guidance, and allocate resources. Key considerations in determining
     protection priorities include criticality, vulnerability, and recoverability, which shape commander guidance
     and focus area security efforts. Area security often focuses on the following activities:

          • Route and convoy security.
          • Checkpoints and combat outposts.
          • DC control.
          • ADC.
          • Base and base camp security and defense.
          • Node protection.
          • Protective services.
          • Air, sea, and rail ports and terminals security.
          • Response force operations.

ROUTE AND CONVOY SECURITY

     4-11. The protection of LOCs and the friendly forces along them is crucial for military operations. Plans for
     route security may involve increasing security measures for all or part of a route, designating specific units
     for convoy security, providing guidance to units for self-protection during convoys, or establishing security
     requirements for convoys transporting critical assets. Although convoy security is a distinct task, it is always
     connected to the overall security of LOCs and relies on broader area security to support local activities related
     to convoy protection. Ensuring the security of LOCs and supply routes (railroads, pipelines, highways, and
     waterways) poses one of the greatest challenges in an AO. Typically, route and convoy security operations
     are perceived as defensive and focus on the terrain. For further information, see ADP 3-37.

     4-12. Movement control is a critical component to the control of LOCs, routes, and area security. However,
     its primary focus is on control rather than security of the movement. Movement control is the dual process of
     committing allocated transportation assets and regulating movements according to command priorities to
     synchronize distribution flow over lines of communications to sustain land forces (ADP 4-0). Security
     considerations must always be integrated into movement control. Additionally, mobility considerations are
     essential for ensuring security. See chapter 2 and ATP 3-90.4 for a detailed discussion on the role of mobility
     operations in LOCs and route and convoy security.

     4-13. Military police units are crucial in providing security for LOCs and routes identified as critical to
     military operations. They also provide convoy security for high-priority designated units transporting critical
     supplies to combat forces, as designated by the joint force commander or geographic CCDR. However, it is
     generally less effective to have military police teams embedded directly within a convoy for security
     purposes. Instead, deploying military police units for aggressive patrolling and conducting route, area, and
     zone reconnaissance is often the most efficient way to create a safe and secure environment for all unit
     movements. The effectiveness of military police security depends on a well-trained convoy employing
     appropriate self-protection techniques, as seen in ATP 4-01.45.

Route Security

     4-14. Route security operations protect LOCs such as highways, pipelines, railways, waterways, and friendly
     forces traveling along these routes. Units conduct route security missions to prevent enemy ground forces
     from moving into direct-fire range of the protected route. These operations also aim to deter or reduce other
     threats that could disrupt traffic along the route. Generally, route security operations are terrain-oriented and
     defensive in nature.

     4-15. Threat forces attempt to sever supply routes and LOCs by various methods. Roads, waterways, and
     railways may be obstructed by explosive hazards such as mines, IEDs, or other obstacles; ambush sites may
     be located adjacent to the route being secured; or bridges and tunnels may be destroyed by demolitions
     causing diversions, delays, or further actions against traffic using the route. Because of the nature of this

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  63


---

Chapter 4

     mission, long routes may be challenging to secure; however, measures can be enforced to reduce the effect
     of threat forces on the routes.

     4-16. A route security force operates along and on the flanks of a designated route. Route security is typically
     used in an economy-of-force role to secure critical MSRs or other routes. To accomplish the route security
     mission, the force performs the following functions:

          • Conduct continuous mounted and dismounted reconnaissance of the route and key locations along
                it to ensure that the route is trafficable.

          • Perform route clearance at irregular intervals to identify explosive hazards and other obstacles along
                the route.

          • Identify specific sections of the route to be searched and locations that are likely to support threat
                activities.

          • Establish roadblocks and checkpoints along the route and lateral routes to stop and search vehicles
                and individuals entering or traveling along the route.

          • Occupy key locations and terrain near the route. Establish a screen to prevent direct-fire threats and
                observation from impacting the route.

          • Conduct aggressive ground and aerial patrols and surveillance to maintain route security.
          • Establish OPs, either covertly or overtly, or set up ambushes at critical points to monitor for threat

                activity.

     4-17. Security forces conduct route reconnaissance at irregular intervals to prevent the threat from
     recognizing a predictable pattern. If sufficient resources are available, they should reconnoiter the route and
     perform zone reconnaissance to a designated distance on both flanks. Supporting aviation or intelligence
     assets may conduct reconnaissance ahead of ground elements or help screen the flanks. In addition to
     reconnaissance, security elements may escort engineers performing route clearance, improvements, or
     maintenance; clearing terrain at choke points or other potential ambush sites; or repairing damage caused by
     threat actions.

     4-18. Military police may assist with route reconnaissance, route clearance, and route maintenance as part of
     route security missions. Generally, military police will be part of the forces performing route security. For a
     detailed discussion on reconnaissance and mobility support activities related to route security, see chapter 2.

     4-19. Military police may require engineer support to assist with the hasty construction of checkpoints and
     potentially for building barriers that direct traffic to designated search areas (see ATP 3-37.34). The primary
     focus of military police is security, which enhances mobility along the route. Engineer geospatial support can
     assist with the planning, preparation, execution, and assessment of the mission (see ATP 3-34.80). This
     support includes analyzing potential ambush sites or locations where the enemy could hinder friendly forces
     or local CIV traffic by deploying obstacles or explosive ordnance. For further details, see ATP 4-32.

Convoy Security

     4-20. A convoy security operation is a specialized type of area security operation conducted to protect
     convoys. Units carry out convoy security operations when there are insufficient friendly forces to
     continuously secure routes and other LOCs in an AO, especially when there is a significant risk of enemy or
     adversary ground action targeting the convoy. Commanders should conduct convoy security in conjunction
     with route security and coordinate with other supporting activities in the area. Typically, local or theater
     policies dictate when or which convoys receive support for security and protection.

     4-21. Planning involves designating units for convoy security, providing or confirming TTP for units to
     ensure their safety during convoys, and establishing protection and security requirements for convoys
     carrying critical assets. Convoy security operations are primarily defensive, focusing on the protected force;
     however, this does not negate the importance of aggressive reconnaissance as part of the mission. For more
     detailed information on convoy security training requirements and TTP, see ATP 4-01.45.

     4-22. Convoy security missions have specific requirements that the commander must consider when
     developing a plan. The convoy security commander and their subordinates receive briefings on the latest
     information regarding the threat situation and the area where the convoy will travel. The commander then
     formulates a comprehensive plan and issues orders that include the commander’s intent, assignments for
     security force elements (reconnaissance, screening, escort, and response), the movement formation, the

64         ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                     Support to Security

intervals between echelons and vehicles, the rate of travel, and detailed plans for actions upon contact. (See
appendix C for guidance on developing security and mobility support attachments that support the base
OPORD.) Immediate action drills, such as responses to enemy ambushes, obstacles, and indirect fire, must
be identified, rehearsed before movement, and executed if contact occurs.

4-23. A convoy security mission involves essential tasks that guide both planning and execution. To protect
a convoy, the security force must carry out the following actions:

     • Reconnoiter the convoy route.
     • Clear the route of obstacles and positions where a threat could affect movement along the route.
     • Provide early warning to prevent the threat from impeding, harassing, containing, seizing, or

          destroying the convoy.

4-24. The convoy security force typically consists of four key elements:
     • Reconnaissance. The reconnaissance element is responsible for conducting route reconnaissance
          ahead of the convoy. It may also carry out tasks usually assigned to the screen element.
     • Screen. The screen element provides early warning and security for the flanks and rear of the
          convoy. It can also perform functions typically associated with the reconnaissance element.
     • Escort. The escort element offers local protection to the convoy. Additionally, it may serve as a
          response force to help repel or neutralize threats.
     • Response. The response force is equipped to provide firepower and support, assisting other elements
          in managing the situation or conducting a hasty attack. It may also take on duties typically handled
          by the escort element.

4-25. Commanders plan and execute all troop and supply movements as part of tactical operations. Due to
the inherent dangers associated with convoy operations, there is a strong emphasis on implementing extensive
security measures. These measures include—

     • Maintaining secrecy when planning and disseminating orders, adhering to strict noise and light
          discipline during movement, and varying routes and schedules to avoid predictability.

     • Coordinating with fire support and aerial support units to ensure that they understand how their
          assistance can enhance movement security and provide close, continuous support for combat
          operations.

     • Maneuvering to prepare for counterambush actions, formulating contingency plans for immediate
          responses to ambushes, and utilizing formations that allow part of the convoy to maneuver against
          an ambush force.

     • Establishing clear communication and coordination with supporting units, units positioned along
          the route, adjacent HN forces, and higher headquarters, including airborne radio relay assets.

     • Collaborating with the supported unit that is moving in formation by implementing control
          measures, identifying leader locations, ensuring communications, and providing medical support
          and weapon systems.

     • Gathering information from local CIVs along the movement route to identify potential enemy
          ambush sites.

4-26. Military police are often selected to conduct convoy security operations, primarily in the support area
and along LOCs. They are generally best suited for either the reconnaissance or escort element. While limited
additional training may be necessary to fulfill various tasks associated with convoy security, this can be
effectively managed when properly equipped. Commanders may have access to geospatial support to help
identify choke points, potential ambush locations, or concerns related to road conditions during the IPOE.
Many tasks associated with route security may also be relevant to or connected with the execution of this
mission.

       Note. The demand for military police resources is significant during large-scale combat operations
       making convoy security a top priority. To optimize the use of limited military police assets,
       protection working groups should rank convoy protection based on the importance of the assets
       or missions involved. This approach enables military police to allocate resources effectively and
       ensure the security of the most vital movements.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  65


---

Chapter 4

    4-27. Military police units can provide convoy security for high-priority designated units that are transporting
    critical supplies, as designated by the joint force commander or the geographic CCDR, to combat forces.
    However, military police teams traveling with a convoy are generally not the most effective means of
    securing it. A more effective approach is to use military police units for aggressive patrolling and conducting
    reconnaissance missions—whether route, area, or even zone reconnaissance—to create a safe and secure
    environment for all types of unit movement. This may include acting as the forward security element, as seen
    in ATP 4-01.45.

    4-28. During the execution of convoy security missions, military police adhere to four key principles of
    mounted movement for tactical convoys:

         • 360-degree security. Combine maximum all-around visibility for situational awareness with
              interlocking sectors of fire and mutual support. Convoy members must be aware of their
              surroundings, including what is above and below their position. Situational awareness involves
              being vigilant about approaching vehicles, explosive ordnance, suspicious wires and antennas, and
              terrain features such as bridges, overpasses, and questionable individuals or groups.

         • Deterrence. Project an aggressive, professional demeanor that conveys a readiness and willingness
              to engage. Such a posture can deter potential adversaries from approaching or attacking the convoy.

         • Agility. Adapt to the conditions presented by the environment or the actions of the enemy.
         • Unpredictability. Minimize the ability of the enemy to anticipate the timing, route, composition,

              or purpose of the convoy.

    4-29. Military police leaders assigned to a convoy security mission are responsible for developing the security
    plan, providing guidance on TTP for units to provide their own security during convoys, and establishing
    protection and security requirements for convoys transporting critical assets. The mission leader organizes
    the security of the convoy by creating a strategy that considers all essential factors for the specific mission.
    This includes coordinating with relevant units for necessary support, acquiring the required equipment and
    supplies, and preparing mission briefings for security personnel. When planning convoy security operations,
    the mission leader completes the following tasks:

         • Coordinate with HN security personnel (when necessary).
         • Consult with all sources (intelligence, engineers, highway traffic division, and other units) to obtain

              as much information as possible about current and past activity and other information affecting the
              convoy route (and alternate routes) and projected timeframes.
         • Apply mobility considerations (see ATP 3-90.4).
         • Reconnoiter the convoy route to—
               Identify likely trouble spots and ambush sites.
               Determine possible locations for TCPs or checkpoints.
               Identify route conditions.
               Determine the location of friendly units in the area.
         • Coordinate with the convoy commander.
         • Determine actions to take if—
               Attacked by an explosive ordnance.
               Attacked by a sniper.
               Ambushed with the road blocked.
               Ambushed with the road not blocked.
               Attacked from the air.
               Attacked by indirect fires.
               Encountering CIVs on the route.

           Note. The response to an enemy attack during convoy security operations depends on the specific
           mission and the type of cargo being transported. When dealing with special weapons or
           ammunition, it is crucial to outline the appropriate procedures in the special orders, detailing
           exactly how to react to enemy contact.

         • Determine convoy organization, to include locations of critical cargo vehicles, command vehicles,
              armored vehicles, maintenance and recovery vehicles, and those equipped with crew-served
              weapons.

66         ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                           Support to Security

          • Determine the location at which military police vehicles and Soldiers will be positioned within or
                in proximity to the convoy.

          • Determine primary and backup radio frequencies and emergency communications procedures.
          • Determine the timetable for movements.
          • Determine start points and release points.
          • Determine security measures to be used at halts and rest stops.
          • Determine the time and place that military police support begins and ends.
          • Determine road conditions.
          • Determine available support from indirect fires, mobility assets, aviation assets (to include UASs),

                HN police support, or other friendly assets that may affect the convoy routes.
          • Identify primary and alternate routes.
          • Determine the method of escort for the convoy.
          • Brief the personnel on the mission, enemy situation, and specific individual duties to be performed.
          • Coordinate with friendly units in the area where the convoy will pass and identify the support the

                friendly units can provide.
          • Coordinate with aviation assets, if available.
          • Ensure that leaders conduct thorough precombat checks or precombat inspections and rehearsals.

                See ATP 3-39.4 for a description and examples of precombat checks or precombat inspections.

Watercraft Security

     4-30. Military police may be requested to enhance the security of Army watercraft and provide security
     during transit, at ports, or while conducting joint logistics over-the-shore. Watercraft can encounter threats
     while navigating restricted waterways such as straits and harbors or when anchored off hostile coastlines,
     making them high-risk targets.

     4-31. When conducting watercraft security, many of the same principles used for convoy security apply;
     however, there are unique factors that require careful consideration. Leaders must first understand the type
     of vessel being used for the operation. The characteristics of the supported vessel greatly impacts mission
     planning, security posture, the size of the security force, and equipment required.

     4-32. During the mission analysis and planning phase of the operation, military police must consider several
     factors to determine the security plan of the vessel, such as:

          • What type of vessel is being secured? This will dictate the size of the security element needed.
          • What type of weapons and equipment are already on board the vessel?
          • What are the expected threats? (Request a threat analysis from S-2.)
          • How long will the vessel be in motion? This will influence the size of rations and the work/rest

                cycle.
          • Is lodging available on the vessel?
          • Are rations provided on the vessel?
          • Are there CIV crew members on the vessel, and have they been properly screened?
          • Who is the vessel captain, and/or who is the point of contact for this mission at higher headquarters?
          • Are other security elements, such as HN or joint-service waterborne security, contracted security,

                or HN port police operating during this mission?

     4-33. When supporting vessel security while in port or during joint logistics over-the-shore, leaders should
     consider—

          • The location of access control points at the port or causeway and who is responsible for security and
                operations at these points.

          • The traffic control plan, including the priority for embarkation.
          • The control measures for access to restricted and limited areas for both vehicles and pedestrians.
          • The barrier plan, which should include appropriate stand-off distances for vehicles and small craft.
          • The force protection condition posture during cargo transfer and as the cargo departs from the port

                area.
          • Security boundaries for adjacent security elements and the plan for early warning and response

                forces.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  67


---

Chapter 4

          • Whether the military police have the required personal protective equipment to effectively conduct
                watercraft security.

     4-34. Before departing for watercraft security, ensure that at least the following preparations are completed:
          • Communicate with the port authority.
          • Coordinate with joint or HN security forces providing waterborne or land-based security (if
                available).
          • Request additional security and protection assets (biometrics, sensors, and C-UAS).
          • Ensure that effective communication is established between the vessel, headquarters, and the port.

Roll-off Plan

     4-35. When transitioning from watercraft security to port security, it is crucial to have a completed roll-off
     plan that is thoroughly understood by the security team and all entities at the port. The nature of the port
     (contested or noncontested) will dictate the security posture of the support security element. Figure 4-1,
     provides an example of a roll-off concept at a port. Below are key considerations for developing an effective
     roll-off plan:

          • Is the port contested?
          • How long does it take for the vessel ramp to lower into position?
          • Is the off-load area at the port clear of CIVs or obstacles that could impede ground movement?
          • How long will it take to unload equipment from the vessel?
          • Is the vessel docking at a CIV port or an unimproved landing site (joint logistics over-the-shore)?

MP       traffic control post
TCP
