Littoral Defense

     4-36. The littoral zone is the coastal area of the sea situated between the high tide and low tide marks,
     typically ranging in depth from 0 to 200 feet. For military operations, vessels in this zone must be light
     enough to traverse over the sandbar and reach land or be close enough to establish a floating bridge, especially
     in the case of joint logistics over-the-shore. Within this zone, units are vulnerable to direct and indirect fire
     from small arms, including attacks from UASs. The conditions in the littoral zone compel vessels to slow
     their movement, often leading to docking, which increases the risk of disruption from enemy attacks.
     Providing effective security for units operating in the littoral zone is crucial.

     4-37. As the vessel approaches the littoral zone, the security team should man all weapon stations and activate
     available electronic warfare or C-UAS equipment. The team should then conduct a reconnaissance of the
     dock using UAS. The UAS can help identify potential enemy forces, suspicious activities, or obstacles that
     could impede the docking of the vessel. The necessary clearance to operate in an airspace must be obtained
     before a UAS is launched. The security element will implement their roll-off plan once the vessel docks at
     the port.

CHECKPOINTS AND COMBAT OUTPOSTS

     4-38. Controlling movement within an AO may be necessary for specific periods or as part of long-term
     operations. This can be achieved by establishing checkpoints and combat outposts along designated roads
     and routes, and on key terrain identified through mission variables.

     4-39. Checkpoints control, regulate, and verify movement, while combat outposts provide sanctuary, support,
     information collection, or area denial. Typically, most checkpoints will not evolve into combat outposts.
     However, a checkpoint may develop into a combat outpost if it experiences a high density of other activities.
     These activities could include several collocated collection points, the necessity to maintain the checkpoint
     for an extended period, or significant enemy activity in the area.

Checkpoints

     4-40. Commanders often need to control the freedom of movement within an AO. Military police achieve
     this by establishing short- or long-term checkpoints along designated routes and roadways and on key terrain
     identified by analyzing the mission variables. Checkpoints monitor and control the movement of personnel
     and vehicles, inspect cargo, enforce laws and regulations, and provide information. Additionally, checkpoints
     can be used to coordinate movement and surveillance activities.

     4-41. Establishing checkpoints is a critical measure of a commander’s overall protection efforts. The
     commander designates these checkpoints along a movement route to help marching units comply with the
     timetable. The movement overlay highlights key points along the route where potential disruptions could
     occur. To prevent congestion and confusion, the commander positions TCPs along the route, which may be
     staffed by military police or personnel from other units. These Soldiers report to the appropriate area
     movement control organization when a convoy, march column, or march serial arrives at and completes
     passage of their location. Checkpoints can also indicate critical terrain features, assisting in coordinating air-
     ground integration and facilitating effective civil control. For more information regarding civil disturbance,
     see ATP 3-39.4 and ATP 3-39.33.

     4-42. Military police offer their expertise to commanders regarding the construction and procedures of
     checkpoint operations. They are responsible for operating essential checkpoints that manage traffic flow,
     enforce laws, and control movement at important locations, such as border crossings and access points to
     critical facilities. (See chapter 5 for more information on border operations.) Military police establish and
     manage two types of checkpoints: deliberate and hasty.

Deliberate Checkpoints

     4-43. A deliberate checkpoint is a fixed location established to protect an operating base camp, a well-
     established MSR, or a main road in rural or urban areas. Unlike a hasty checkpoint, a deliberate checkpoint
     is typically preplanned and intended to remain in place for longer periods of time. Additionally, rehearsals
     may be required to ensure proper operation.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  69


---

Chapter 4

     4-44. Survivability considerations may be significant when compared to a hasty checkpoint. For detailed
     information on creating and utilizing obstacles, including access and entry control points, see ATP 3-37.34.
     Additionally, ATP 3-39.4 provides specifics related to all access control points. For an example of a
     deliberate checkpoint, see figure 4-2.

    Legend:  United States
    U.S.

             Figure 4-2. Example of a deliberate check point

    4-45. Deliberate checkpoints are typically used to—
         • Control all vehicles and pedestrian traffic so crowds cannot assemble.
         • Identify and detain known offenders or suspected enemy or insurgent personnel.
         • Provide straggler control collection.
         • Enforce curfews.
         • Deter illegal movement.
         • Prevent the movement of supplies to the enemy.
         • Deny the enemy contact with or prevent insurgents from hiding with the local inhabitants.
         • Dominate the area around the checkpoint.
         • Collect information.

    4-46. Checkpoints are typically categorized based on the expected volume of traffic that will pass through
    them. A heavy-traffic checkpoint usually requires a platoon to operate it. While not ideal, a military police
    squad (or other types of squads) can manage a light-traffic checkpoint for a limited duration, typically 12
    hours or less. Checkpoints may also be classified by the number of other activities collocated or occurring in
    the same vicinity.

70                          ATP 3-39.30                       25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                           Support to Security

Hasty Checkpoints
     4-47. Units establish a hasty checkpoint as part of a larger tactical plan or in response to hostile activities,
     such as a bomb, mine incident, or sniper attack. The checkpoint can be closed on the command of the
     controlling headquarters. A hasty checkpoint always has a specific task and purpose. Additionally, hasty
     checkpoints can evolve into deliberate checkpoints. Figure 4-3 provides an example of a hasty checkpoint.

                                   Figure 4-3. Example hasty checkpoint

4-48. Units establish hasty checkpoints to achieve surprise. Leaders avoid predictable patterns by randomly
changing the checkpoint locations and methods of operation. Hasty checkpoints are set up in locations where
they cannot be seen by approaching traffic until it is too late for vehicles to withdraw unobtrusively. Ideal
locations for hasty checkpoints include bridges, narrow passages (defiles), highway intersections, the reverse
slope of a hill, and just beyond sharp curves. Key characteristics of a hasty checkpoint include—

     • Using vehicles reinforced with concertina wire as obstacles. Soldiers may also use tire deflation
          devices or road spike strips, which can be more effective and less intrusive in stability operations.

     • Positioning vehicles at each end of the checkpoint to partially block the route.
     • Conducting searches in the area between the vehicles.
     • Concealing a reaction force, consisting of at least one team, nearby to respond if the site is attacked.

4-49. The basic organization of a checkpoint includes a headquarters element, a security element, a search
element, and an assault element. While checkpoint forces may vary in size, typical sizes are shown below.
To operate a checkpoint, task-organize the unit as follows:

     • Headquarters element. This element is responsible for—
           Exercising command and control.
           Maintaining a log of all activities.
           Maintaining communication with its higher headquarters.
           Coordinating local patrols.
           Coordinating the role of civil authorities, as required.
           Coordinating linkups, as required.
           Coordinating relief in place, as required.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  71


---

Chapter 4

                 Integrating a reserve force or a response force.
          • Security element. This element consists of a military police squad responsible for—

                 Monitoring traffic flow up to and through the checkpoint.
                 Watching for and reporting suspicious activity.
                 Providing early warning to the search and assault elements.
                 Preventing enemy ambush.
          • Search element. This element consists of a military police squad and is responsible for—
                 Halting vehicles at the checkpoint.
                 Guiding vehicles to the designated search point.
                 Conducting personnel and vehicle searches.
                 Directing cleared vehicles to continue through the checkpoint.
                 Detaining personnel as directed.
          • Assault element. This element is responsible for—
                 Preparing and occupying fortified fighting positions.
                 Eliminating hostile elements that force their way past the search team, according to the ROE.

             Note. Checkpoints are a part of police operations. All Soldiers involved in the operation must
             clearly understand the ROE and the use of deadly force.

     4-50. The layout, construction, and operation of checkpoints are based on an analysis of the mission variables.
     The following procedures and considerations typically apply:

          • Position the checkpoint in a location where traffic cannot turn back, leave the road, or bypass it
                without being observed.

          • Place vehicles and crew-served weapons off the road but within sight of the checkpoint. This
                positioning helps deter resistance against Soldiers operating the checkpoint. Vehicles should be
                positioned in a hull-down configuration, allowing Soldiers to engage vehicles attempting to break
                through or bypass the checkpoint.

          • Install obstacles to slow or channel traffic into the search area. Traffic should be funneled through
                the checkpoint in a single file.

          • Display signs written in the HN language that explain the purpose of the checkpoint and provide
                instructions for passing through it.

72  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                           Support to Security

          • Establish communications according to the unit SOP.
          • Designate specific search areas for vehicles and personnel. If possible, surround the search area with

                barriers to protect against threats such as booby-trapped vehicles or suicide bombers. Women should
                only be checked with a metal detector or searched by female personnel, depending on the ROE and
                mission variables. ATP 3-39.4 contains additional information regarding searches of vehicles and
                personnel.

             Note. If applicable, checkpoint personnel should include linguists.

Combat Outposts

     4-51. A combat outpost is a reinforced observation post capable of conducting limited combat operations
     (FM 3-90). Using combat outposts is an effective strategy for deploying security forces in challenging terrain
     where mounted units cannot adequately cover the area. These outposts are also useful when smaller OPs or
     checkpoints and TCPs are at risk of being overrun by enemy forces infiltrating the security zone. The
     commander establishes a combat outpost to extend the depth of the security perimeter, maintain friendly
     forward OPs until they can observe the main body of the enemy, or protect friendly forward OPs that may
     become encircled by enemy forces. Both mounted and dismounted units can utilize combat outposts
     effectively. See figure 4-4 for an example of combat outposts supporting a guard mission.

                                          Figure 4-4. Combat outposts

4-52. An analysis of the mission variables determines the appropriate size, location, and number of combat
outposts a unit should establish. Typically, a reinforced platoon occupies a combat outpost. A combat outpost
must have enough resources to accomplish its designated missions effectively but not so many that it
significantly weakens the strength of the main unit. The outpost is generally positioned far enough ahead of
the protected force to prevent enemy ground reconnaissance elements from observing the actions of that
force.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  73


---

Chapter 4

     4-53. The commander organizes a combat outpost to provide an all-around defense to withstand a superior
     enemy force. When the enemy possesses a significant amount of armored capability, the commander may
     allocate more antitank weapons to the combat outpost than the standard amount. The forces stationed at these
     outposts can conduct aggressive patrolling, engage and eliminate enemy reconnaissance units, and confront
     the main body of the enemy before their extraction. The commander also plans to extract friendly forces from
     the outpost before the enemy overruns their position.

     4-54. The most likely situation is when multiple activities are collocated at a specific site, and the likelihood
     of enemy activity against that site increases to where the security level of a combat outpost may be needed.

BASE AND BASE CAMP SECURITY AND DEFENSE

     4-55. Base defense is the local military measures, both normal and emergency, required to nullify or reduce
     the effectiveness of enemy attacks on, or sabotage of, a base to ensure the maximum capacity of its facilities
     is available to United States forces (JP 3-10). Base security and defense primarily fall under the protection
     warfighting function; however, they also incorporate tasks from both the protection and movement and
     maneuver warfighting functions as described in ADP 3-37 and ADP 3-90, respectively. Additional important
     references for base security and defense operations include ATP 3-37.2, FM 3-90, GTA 90-01-011, JP 3-10,
     and JP 3-26.

     4-56. A division or corps may be required to protect multiple bases or base camps. They may choose to
     establish one or more base cluster. In base defense operations, a base cluster is a collection of bases,
     geographically grouped for mutual protection and ease of command and control (JP 3-10). Base commanders
     are responsible for establishing defense procedures for their AO. Depending on the mission variables, units
     may be assigned to conduct base defense operations on a permanent or rotating basis. For a detailed
     discussion on the security and defense of bases and base camps, see ATP 3-37.10.

     4-57. The base and base camp security and defense framework is organized into three primary areas (see
     figure 4-5, page 75). This structuring facilitates the organization of protection and defense information, aligns
     requirements, and directs efforts. The three areas include the—

          • Outer security area. The outer security area extends beyond the base camp perimeter to the limits
                of the base camp commander’s AO. Commanders establish an outer security area to ensure early
                warning, provide reaction time, and deny enemy reconnaissance efforts and vantage points for
                conducting standoff attacks. Mobile security elements typically patrol this outer area and establish
                checkpoints or other security measures along LOCs.

          • Perimeter zone. The perimeter zone encompasses the base camp perimeter and the area
                immediately in front of or behind it, where operations, fighting positions, and entry control points
                are located. Selected base camps may have designated inner and outer perimeters. A single perimeter
                is more common in larger base camps, supplemented by inner barriers and access control measures
                around critical facilities. Creating a double perimeter is resource-intensive and seldom used.

          • Inner security area. The inner security area is situated inside the base camp perimeter. Plans for
                interior barriers can be implemented around individual unit locations and critical assets, serving as
                traffic control measures that enhance the depth of the base camp security plan. These measures aim
                to halt or impede threats that penetrate the perimeter zone.

             Note. The framework for base and base camp security and defense can be applied for planning the
             security and defense of critical assets/sites.

74  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                            Support to Security

                    Figure 4-5. Framework for base camp security and defense

4-58. Collectively, the outer perimeter area, perimeter zone, and inner security area form the base camp AO.
Commanders assigned to an AO must fulfill specific responsibilities as outlined in FM 3-90. However, not
all commanders designated as base camp commanders will possess the necessary organic capabilities within
their units to fulfill every requirement. In such cases, the higher commander must specify in the order which
AO responsibilities will not be carried out by the base camp commander and identify who will perform those
tasks. Alternatively, they should provide the necessary additional capabilities to meet these responsibilities.
For further details, see appendix E regarding the development of security and mobility support attachments
for the base OPORD.

4-59. Base camp commanders and their staffs apply the framework for base security and defense to guide
their planning activities, ensuring that all critical elements of base security and defense are considered. This
framework is not meant to be a comprehensive solution for base security and defense; instead, it serves as a
general template for planning.

4-60. Military police are responsible for conducting security operations to protect friendly forces, bases, base
camps, and actions in the support area. As part of their area security duties, military police units provide
protection for bases and base camps, including airbases, within the AO. In this capacity, they are equipped
to detect enemy forces, provide early warnings, and engage threats in the outer security areas. Utilizing their
skills, military police may interact with the local population and apply intelligence techniques to enhance the
security of the base camp. They deliver thorough security by operating beyond the perimeter of the base
camp, where they are out of range of threats to the base itself and within the perimeter and inside the base
camp. This approach allows for the early detection and engagement of enemy forces from a safe distance,
enabling military police to disrupt or eliminate threats before they can effectively target the base camp.
Additionally, military police units may operate within the perimeter and inner security zones, acting as the
response force when needed.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  75


---

Chapter 4

     4-61. Airbase protection and defense are key components of military police area security. When a threat
     exceeds the capabilities of the airbase, the commanding officer can request assistance from military police.
     Effective airbase defense requires special coordination between military police, the United States Air Force
     security forces, and other security personnel responsible for base defense. Air Force security forces units are
     tasked with internal security and defense of the airbase, while military police units typically handle external
     defense. Air Force and Army forces must coordinate their defensive efforts, which include establishing
     boundaries, fire control measures, and contact points. By combining the resources of Air Force security forces
     with military police units, a comprehensive defense can be implemented for critical assets such as weapon
     systems, aircraft, command centers, personnel, and other priority resources defined by the airbase
     commander. For more information on Air Force security operations, see DAFI 31-101.

     4-62. In developing a base defense strategy, creating a simple and flexible defensive plan is essential. This
     plan should maximize the use of SOPs and battle drills at the user level. Additionally, the base defense plan
     should incorporate the following fundamentals:

          • Understand the enemy. Defenders must be familiar with the capabilities and limitations of enemy
                forces, including their weapons, equipment, and tactics.

          • Create situational awareness. Intelligence reporting is crucial for providing an accurate overview
                of the operational environment.

          • Use the advantages of the defenders. Defenders may be able to overcome a numerically superior
                force by leveraging their advantages, which include—
                 The ability to fight from cover.
                 Detailed knowledge of the local terrain and environment.
                 The ability to prepare positions, routes between them, obstacles, and fields of fire in advance.
                 The ability to plan for communications, control measures, indirect fires, and logistics support
                     for contingency operations.
                 The ability to mislead enemy forces regarding friendly defensive capabilities, troop positions,
                     and execution of operations.

          • Concentrate at critical times and places. The defense of a base or base camp should leverage
                interior lines, enabling the timely and secure movement of forces to engage the most significant
                threats. Commanders must concentrate combat power at points of decision by economizing
                resources in some areas, maintaining a reserve, and maneuvering to achieve local superiority where
                it is most needed.

          • Conduct counterreconnaissance and counterattacks. Fixed bases typically have well-established
                perimeters with limited depth. Implementing counterreconnaissance and counterattacks can provide
                added depth to the battle outside the perimeter, allowing the base or base camp to fulfill its primary
                mission with minimal interruption. Counterreconnaissance patrols should extend 2 to 3 miles
                (approximately 3 to 5 kilometers) beyond the perimeter, depending on the terrain, to reduce the risk
                of fratricide, especially at night.

          • Coordinate critical defense assets. Synchronizing indirect fire, air defense resources, tactical
                aircraft, engineers, dismounted troops, armored vehicles, naval surface fire support, and helicopters
                can create a combined-arms effect.

          • Balance base security with political and legal constraints. This fundamental is particularly
                critical in a low-intensity conflict environment.

          • Understand the law of war and ROE. Base and base camp commanders and their subordinates
                must adhere to ROE. In joint-service or multinational operations, resolving inconsistencies related
                to ROE is crucial.

     4-63. Emphasis on specific base defense and security measures varies with the anticipated threat level. Any
     or all levels presented in table 4-1, page 77, may exist simultaneously.

4-64. The mission of the base security forces is to conduct operations that react to, deter, deny, resist, or
destroy an enemy Level I force (see table 4-1) that is attacking the base or base camp. The base defense force
comprises a combination of security assets provided by the units located at the base camp. The base camp
commander evaluates all available Army, joint, and multinational resources and forces to determine the
appropriate organization of the base defense force. In the event of an attack on the base camp, the commander
will respond based on the capabilities of the base defense force.

25 March 2026                                   ATP 3-39.30                                                    77


---

Chapter 4

     4-65. A base defense force should have significant direct-fire lethality, achieved through small arms,
     automatic weapons, and antitank systems. It should also have access to supporting indirect fires, a high degree
     of tactical mobility, and a reasonable span of control. The force should be capable of—

          • Conducting reconnaissance patrols to detect and report the location, strength, and capabilities of
                enemy forces near the base camp.

          • Developing positions both within and outside the base camp to effectively stop or destroy enemy
                advances.

          • Utilizing reserve elements to engage and attack smaller enemy units that threaten to breach the base
                camp perimeter.

          • Providing internal security for critical assets and capabilities within the base camp.

     4-66. To streamline the command and control of the base defense force during an attack, the base commander
     may appoint a base defense force commander and set up a base defense operations center. This center will
     facilitate the integration of defense plans and maximize the overall effectiveness of the defense efforts of the
     base. A base defense operations center is a command-and-control facility established by the base commander
     to serve as the focal point for base security and defense (JP 3-10). The structure of the base defense operations
     center will depend on the types of forces involved, which may include joint, interagency, or multinational
     components.

     4-67. Military police leaders anticipate the integration of their capabilities into the base defense plan. They
     understand their roles, abilities, and dependencies and can effectively communicate this information to
     nonmilitary police leaders or battle staff. PIO can provide crucial early warnings of enemy activity, enabling
     early predictions and potentially preventing threats from impacting base operations. Additional tasks for base
     defense may include conducting patrols, managing access control, securing critical base facilities, setting up
     LPs or OPs, and deploying quick reaction forces or initial response forces.

FIXED SITE UNMANNED AIRCRAFT SYSTEM DEFENSE

     4-68. When building a defense in depth for a fixed-site location, military police must consider that an
     adversary may use UASs to observe or attack the location. If C-UAS capabilities are available, military police
     should incorporate them into their defense plan. To develop an effective C-UAS defense plan, leaders must
     first determine the optimal placement of radar, electronic warfare, optic, or kinetic systems. The locations of
     these systems should be indicated on a map, sector sketch, or base defense plan, detailing their maximum
     detection and engagement ranges. Generally, radar and optics can detect threats at greater distances than the
     engagement ranges, providing an early warning zone. This allows the operations center to issue alerts and
     warnings about incoming threats (see figure 4-6). Furthermore, military police must coordinate with the air
     defense and air management cell in the area to maintain an updated air picture, enabling them to identify and
     differentiate between friendly and hostile UASs.

    Figure 4-6. Detection and engagement ranges

78  ATP 3-39.30                                  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                     Support to Security

4-69. If handheld or man-packed C-UASs are available, they can be deployed to OPs to cover blind spots
or critical approaches. Leaders should consider placing these systems in elevated positions to avoid
obstructions caused by terrain and structures. Commanders and leaders should position some portable
systems at static posts to protect critical assets on site. Others should be assigned to mobile teams to ensure
a quick response to any location on the site.

4-70. For bases located in the United States or overseas, effective coordination between military police, local
law enforcement, and HN security forces is crucial when responding to UAVs, especially if the UAV is
monitoring a fixed site from a location beyond U.S. control. In contingency environments involving base
clusters, base camps, or lodgments, collaboration between the operations center on the base and the mobile
security force in the area is essential for effectively responding to UAS.

4-71. Security forces should take the following steps in response to a UAV detected at a fixed site:
     • Maintain visual contact. Upon detecting a UAV, focus your attention outward and upward,
          ensuring that you maintain visual contact with the UAV.
     • Collect information. Use the SALUTE report format to collect information about the UAV. This
          includes the—
           Size. The dimensions or type of the UAV.
           Activity. The current actions or behaviors of the UAV.
           Location. The position of the UAV.
           Unit. A description of the UAV.
           Time. The time of detection.
           Equipment. Noticeable payload or cameras on the UAV.
     • Report the incident. Immediately report the incident to the appropriate authorities, such as the
          operations center, command post, or police operations center.
     • Identify the operator. Attempt to locate individuals holding a controller or device that appears to
          be operating the UAV. If the UAV seems to be coming from outside the fixed site, or if an operator
          can be seen offsite, contact local law enforcement, HN security forces, or a mobile security team if
          applicable.
     • Check for injuries or damage. Look for damages or injured individuals. If the UAV is suspected
          of carrying a chemical agent or a device that could detonate, search the area for signs of injury or
          items that may have been dropped from the UAV.
     • Execute appropriate actions. If the operator is found, instruct them to land the UAV and interview
          them regarding its operation. If the approval authority has determined that the UAV should be
          disabled, follow the active defense measures listed in ATP 3-01.81.

4-72. Downed UAVs should be treated as evidence or captured enemy material, and the area surrounding
them should be considered a crime scene or sensitive site. When security forces respond to the downing of a
UAV, they must take the following actions:

     • Establish security and secure the scene. Initial responding security forces must secure the area
          and establish a cordon around the UAV. If there are injured individuals or damaged infrastructure,
          notify additional emergency services. Additional responding security forces should search nearby
          for a possible operator or potential dropped payload (technical, explosive, or chemical). If it appears
          that the UAV has a payload attached to it or if a dropped payload has been discovered, a larger
          cordon should be established, the surrounding area should be evacuated, and security forces must
          take extra precautions.

     • Contact EOD. UAVs may have been used as weapon delivery platforms or could be booby-trapped.
          They may contain explosives or a chemical payload. Once the UAV has been rendered safe by EOD,
          processing of the scene may begin.

     • Process the UAV. Treat the UAV like any other piece of electronic or digital evidence. This
          includes photographing it, handling it with care, and packaging it appropriately (see ATP 3-39.12-
          1 for evidence collection and handling procedures). After collecting the UAV, adhere to installation-
          specific SOPs regarding electronic evidence and suspected espionage or sabotage, and submit the
          UAV to the appropriate law enforcement or intelligence authorities.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  79

Chapter 4

NODE PROTECTION

     4-73. Command posts and operations centers typically use area security techniques to safeguard operations.
     This involves deploying protection and security assets in a layered, integrated, and redundant manner. Such
     an approach can help keep potential threats at a distance by maximizing the standoff distance from explosive
     effects while ensuring that the protected asset remains beyond the range of direct-fire weapons and
     observation from enemies or adversaries.

     4-74. Military police understand the elements of the movement order, including the potential activation of
     alternate command post nodes. Providing security for key nodes is potentially a very fluid requirement.
     Therefore, military police must take a proactive approach to this mission. This responsibility is often linked
     to the security of HRP. Nodes may be mobile rather than static sites. Part of the military police requirement
     is reconnaissance of new sites before that site is established as a node. In these situations, military police may
     participate as part of the reconnaissance team or as a quartering party to facilitate the transition and ongoing
     security of the node and the associated HRP.

PROTECTIVE SERVICES

     4-75. High-risk personnel are personnel who, by their grade, assignment, symbolic value, or relative
     isolation, are likely to be attractive or accessible terrorist targets (JP 3-26). Special precautions are taken to
     ensure the safety and security of these individuals and their family members from terrorist or criminal threats.
     When units identify a significant risk to selected personnel, the local commander typically organizes security
     details from organic resources. However, in certain circumstances, designated personnel may require
     protective services from specially trained units or teams. For more information on protective services, see
     ATP 3-39.35.

     4-76. There are four levels of HRP:
          • Level 1 and 2 HRP require significant protection, which includes dedicated personal security details
                and other security measures as outlined in DODI O-2000.22.
          • Level 2.5 allows appointing a personal security officer who coordinates travel security for the
                assigned HRP. This officer may only be armed when functioning as part of a larger, temporary
                protective detail.
          • Level 3 personnel do not qualify for protective services; however, they should receive additional
                training in AT and personal protective measures.

     4-77. Military police are responsible for conducting HRP security in high-risk/combat environments when
     designated by a CCDR or identified through a personal security vulnerability assessment. CID is tasked with
     providing continuous executive-level protective services for key officials, including the SecDef, Deputy
     SecDef, Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, Vice-Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, Secretary of the
     Army, Chief of Staff of the U.S. Army, Chief of the National Guard Bureau, and the commanders of U.S.
     Africa Command, U.S. European Command, and U.S. Southern Command. MWD teams may also enhance
     the detection and protection capabilities of military police and CID.

     4-78. A protective services detail mission aims to safeguard the HRP from all potential hazards, whether they
     arise from intentional actions, accidents, or negligence. While absolute protection is never achievable,
     adhering to established principles of protective services can significantly enhance the likelihood of a
     successful mission. Five essential fundamentals contribute to the effectiveness of protective services detail
     missions. They include—

          • Planning and coordinating. Effective planning is crucial for a successful mission. Every aspect of
                the protection detail should be meticulously organized, with close attention paid to each action and
                movement of the principal and the protective services personnel to ensure maximum security.
                Mission leaders must provide thorough briefings and conduct rehearsals of emergency procedures,
                enabling team members to react instinctively and appropriately in critical situations.

          • Ensuring the availability of resources. The number of personnel assigned to a protection detail
                and the equipment used are influenced by several factors, with the availability of resources being
                the most pivotal. It is important to consider the position of the principal, the threat level at the
                destination, and the complexity of the mission when requesting and allocating resources for a
                successful operation.

80  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                           Support to Security

          • Assigning responsibilities. Clearly defining individual responsibilities is essential for completing
                all aspects of the mission.

          • Controlling information. It is crucial to closely manage the release of personal information related
                to the principal and other members of the party, details of the itinerary, and the security procedures
                in place. Ensure that personnel assigned to protective services understand that they are prohibited
                from discussing or sharing this information with anyone except authorized personnel. Unauthorized
                release of information can compromise the integrity of the security mission.

          • Being flexible. Unexpected changes to the itinerary require a focus on flexibility when planning
                protective services missions. It is important to prepare alternate and contingency plans to address
                circumstances such as inclement weather, potential threats, or other factors that may affect the
                security of the HRP. Implementing effective preventive measures and having comprehensive
                backup plans are critical for ensuring safety.

AREA DAMAGE CONTROL

     4-79. Area damage control are the measures taken before, during, and/or after a hostile action or natural or
     manmade disasters to reduce the probability of damage and minimize its effects (JP 3-10). Commanders
     conduct ADC when the extent of damage and the impact of an attack are limited, allowing them to use local
     assets and resources to respond and recover. Commanders engage in ADC in support areas to prevent and
     mitigate the adverse effects of enemy or adversary actions that may diminish combat power. While engineers
     carry out many tasks related to ADC, military police also play a crucial role. For more information on ADC,
     see ADP 3-37, FM 3-39, and FM 3-81.

     4-80. Military police facilitate the reestablishment of order and control during disasters, helping to mitigate
     their often-devastating effects. There are probably few, if any, new activities or capabilities required for
     military police to perform support to area damage, and many of them may not be specific to the security and
     mobility discipline.

     4-81. When performed as part of DSCA, ADC is commonly referred to as incident management. Some
     incidents may escalate to a level of national significance, necessitating additional resources for mitigation,
     recovery, and investigation. Incident management and ADC follow established battle drills and SOPs. These
     drills enable effective action against the fear, panic, and confusion that can arise following an attack. They
     may be carried out as part of a maneuver enhancement brigade. For more information on incident
     management, see ADP 3-28, ADP 3-37, FM 3-81, and JP 3-10.

RESPONSE FORCE OPERATIONS

     4-82. Response force operations involve planning to counter Level I and II threats and managing Level III
     threats until the designated TCF arrives. These operations use a mobile force with the necessary fire support
     to address Level II threats in the AO. A crucial aspect of these activities is the seamless transition of
     responsibility from a base camp or base cluster and its security forces to a TCF. A tactical combat force is a
     rapidly deployable, air-ground, mobile combat unit with appropriate combat support and combat service
     support assets assigned to, and capable of, defeating Level III threats, including combined arms (JP 3-10).

     4-83. Response force operations expediently reinforce unit organic protection capabilities or complement
     that protection with maneuver capabilities based on the threat. These operations involve planning and training
     to defeat Level I and II threats while managing Level III threats until a designated TCF arrives. A response
     force, typically supported by firepower designated by the area commander, handles Level II threats in the
     AO. For further details on response force operations, see ATP 3-37.10, FM 3-39, and JP 3-10.

     4-84. A response force is a mobile unit, equipped with appropriate fire support that is designated by the area
     commander to address Level II threats within the operational area. Military police units may serve as the
     response force for base camps or critical facilities, particularly against Level I and Level II threat attacks.
     These units gather intelligence about enemy activities while conducting security and mobility support
     missions throughout the AO. This intelligence helps update the commander's common operational picture
     regarding enemy and criminal activity near base camps and throughout the AO. When necessary, military
     police units can deploy wheeled armored response forces to respond to attacks on base camps and critical
     facilities, with the objective of neutralizing the enemy. The defense plan of a base camp commander is
     essential for protecting units and ensuring the success of sustainment operations.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  81


---

Chapter 4

     4-85. Military police may serve as the designated response force for a base camp or other specific location.
     Being designated as a response force is similar to creating a reserve in that the response force may only be
     used to perform activities associated with being the response force—it is a dedicated force. The response
     force operates with adequate tactical mobility and fire support, prepared to address Level I and Level II
     threats and to manage Level III threats until they can be defeated by a TCF or other available response units
     (see ATP 3-37.10). The response force provides the base camp or base cluster commander enhanced security
     and defense capabilities. Once the response force is committed, the commander should be ready to reorganize
     and reconstitute it. The base camp commander can assign various tasks to the response force, both within the
     security area and the perimeter of the base camp. These tasks may include—

          • Reinforcing a threatened area or responding to perimeter breaches.
          • Establishing contact with potential threats and engaging them as needed within the base camp

                security area, defeating Level II threats and delaying Level III threats until a TCF can defeat them.
          • Reinforcing engaged units located outside the perimeter.
          • Conducting reconnaissance and surveillance activities.
          • Responding to threats against critical assets, infrastructure, or HRP.
          • Performing security checks and conducting random patrols within the base camp perimeter.

     4-86. The size and composition of the response force depend on a threat assessment and the levels of
     uncertainty and risk. These factors may be adjusted based on changes in the situation. Additionally, the
     responsiveness level (readiness condition) of the response force is modified according to the current threat
     conditions. The response force should be effectively deployed to ensure adequate protection and tactical
     mobility.

     4-87. When a threat surpasses the capabilities of a base camp or critical facility, the commander will request
     support from a response force. If military police are designated as the response force, units that are nearby,
     on patrol, or conducting area security will consolidate their forces, respond as quickly as possible, and engage
     in combat operations to destroy the enemy. If the threat exceeds the capabilities of the military police, they
     may conduct a battle handover to a TCF. Military police forces performing or tasked as a response may
     conduct a—

          • Movement to contact.
          • Hasty ambush.
          • Hasty attack.
          • Delay.
          • Call for fire (indirect fire or close air support).
          • Critical site defense.

     4-88. If military police are designated as the response force, they must—
          • Review the base defense plans.
          • Coordinate with the supported base commanders to synchronize response strategies.
          • Exchange communication frequencies to ensure effective communication between security
                elements.
          • Identify contingency plans to counter likely enemy activities.
          • Integrate air defense artillery, engineer, CBRN, indirect fire, and close air support into their plans,
                if available.

     4-89. Although the current intelligence and the base or critical facility commander’s risk assessment and
     stated needs are the primary factors in decision making, the military police commander should also assist in
     determining the size and composition of the response force. When designated as the response force
     commander, the military police commander should consider the following when developing the response
     plan:

          • The existing base defense plans and the commander’s guidance.
          • The priority of ongoing operations.
          • The criticality of the base under attack.
          • The amount of time required for different elements to consolidate.
          • The ability to effectively communicate between security elements.
          • The identification of contingency plans to counter likely enemy activities.

82  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                           Support to Security

          • The availability of additional forces to supplement the response element, if necessary.
          • The integration of engineer, CBRN, fires, and close air support.

     4-90. Military police consolidate into squads or platoons to delay, defeat, or defend against Level I and Level
     II threats. They also work to shape and delay Level III threats until a designated combined arms TCF arrives.
     Military police forces acting as a response unit must be capable of performing the following tasks:

          • Movement to contact.
          • Hasty ambush.
          • Hasty attack.
          • Delay.
          • Call for fire.
          • Defense of critical sites and assets.
          • Establishment of a fighting or survivability position (see ATP 3-37.34 and ATP 3-39.4).

CONTESTED LOGISTICS, SUSTAINMENT MODE, AND NODE SECURITY

     4-91. Peer adversaries have the ability and resources to observe, disrupt, delay, and attack U.S. forces,
     infrastructure, and operations across all domains, both within the United States and abroad, and during transit.
     Military police are crucial in detecting, preventing, and mitigating risks to sustainment and force generation.
     They protect logistics and Army forces from their points of origin to the endpoint.

     4-92. Leaders must recognize that they are constantly under observation from various domains and ensure
     that they do not become attractive targets for adversaries. During competition, military police deter enemies
     from gathering intelligence and sabotaging Army forces, infrastructure, and operations. Peer adversaries have
     capabilities in air, space, and cyberspace, which increases the chances that they can maintain continuous
     visual and electromagnetic contact with Army forces. During crisis and armed conflict, enemies focus on
     logistics operations across all domains, employing methods to detect and quickly target sustainment forces
     and resources. Concentrated and stationary forces and resources are easier for adversaries to detect and
     destroy. Therefore, sustainment forces plan to operate in a dispersed manner, quickly massing when
     necessary and then rapidly dispersing again to enhance their survivability. Military police forces must remain
     agile to effectively support these operations.

     4-93. Compared to sustainment forces, military police have significantly fewer resources and capabilities.
     This scarcity necessitates prioritizing the protection of vital sustainment nodes, capabilities, and capacities.
     When planning logistics protection, military police leaders must ensure that sustainers focus on essential
     logistics and allocate security forces appropriately. By effectively managing movement tables, regulating
     routes, and securing areas and routes, military police enable effective distributed sustainment operations.

Air, Sea, Rail Ports, and Terminals

     4-94. Army forces deploy by air and sea from designated strategic support areas. Greater emphasis is placed
     on the protection of air and sea routes for embarkation and debarkation due to the enhanced capabilities of
     the adversary to attack them. Military police are often called upon to provide security support for ports and
     terminals involved in terminal operations. Although there are similarities among different environments, each
     port or terminal is unique and requires specialized knowledge to effectively apply security fundamentals and
     basic military police skills and capabilities. While other military police disciplines may have connections to
     ports and terminals, the focus remains on mobility and security support, ensuring the uninterrupted movement
     of U.S. forces and sustainment assets at aerial ports of embarkation, aerial ports of debarkation, seaports of
     embarkation, and seaports of debarkation.

     4-95. Aerial ports and terminals often have military police supporting the Air Force or CIV organizations,
     focusing on the transitions from air to land or land to air shipment and transport. Seaports and terminals
     typically require support from the Navy and Marine Corps or CIV organizations, concentrating on the
     transitions from water to land or land to water. Railheads can link to aerial ports, seaports, and terminals but
     also have unique requirements. Military police may need to secure the length of the railway and fixed sites
     within the rail system. LOGSEC offers a range of specialized capabilities that enhance security for ports and
     terminals (see chapter 5).

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  83


---

Chapter 4

     4-96. Army expeditionary intermodal operations encompass a range of activities related to transportation.
     The fundamentals of these operations and general terminal techniques integrate various transportation skills
     to improve the deployment, redeployment, and distribution processes for moving personnel, equipment, or
     forces from start to finish. To better understand how military police may support intermodal operations, see
     ATP 4-13 or the specific manuals related to intermodal components and transportation competencies.

Aerial Port and Terminal Security

     4-97. Military police may be needed to support the security of airfields or LZ. When these locations are
     connected to a base, base camp, or base camp cluster, the respective commanders outline specific security
     requirements. If the base primarily serves as an air base, the Air Force will likely hold the command.
     Regardless, the senior airfield authority plays a crucial role in this type of mission.

     4-98. Most base camps require at least a LZ to support resupply operations and casualty evacuations. When
     these activities are conducted near a base camp with active airfields and LZ, assessing vulnerabilities related
     to aircraft approaching and departing is important. Protective measures should be implemented to counter
     threats, including shoulder-launched and surface-to-air weapons and heavy machine guns. For more
     information, see JP 3-10.

     4-99. Military police may need to coordinate their security efforts with Air Force security forces when an
     airbase is situated within or connected to a base camp. The Air Force approach to airbase security closely
     resembles the doctrine used for securing a base camp, although there are differences in terminology and
     specific practices. For more information on integrated defense operations, see DAFI 31-101.

     4-100. Military police may be needed to help secure airports and airfields in the United States and its
     territories at the request of other federal agencies. When this occurs, it is part of DSCA tasks, and ADP 3‑28,
     JP 3‑27, and JP 3‑28 should be reviewed for more information. The only significant change when operating
     in the domestic environment is the legal restrictions that may apply.

Seaport and Terminal Security

     4-101. Military police are responsible for providing security in port and pier areas. The joint force
     commander and subordinate joint force commanders must clearly define and assign port security plans and
     responsibilities. The Military Surface Deployment and Distribution Command serves as the single port
     manager for all ports, as designated by the United States Transportation Command. Area commanders, along
     with their subordinate military police assigned to a port area, must develop and organize plans in
     collaboration with the appropriate Military Surface Deployment and Distribution Command units (either
     brigade or battalion). This ensures that Soldiers are properly trained and equipped to secure port areas and
     cargo as needed. Dedicated port security units typically patrol harbors and anchorages, including waterfront
     security operations. For more information on port security units, see JP 3-10; for details on Army water
     transportation operations, see ATP 4-15.

     4-102. In a wartime environment, military police and CID personnel implement LOGSEC measures (see
     chapter 5) at terminals within the continental United States and overseas locations. Teams stationed at ports
     of debarkation are aware of sensitive cargo shipments arriving at their facilities. This awareness is crucial for
     minimizing or eliminating the risk of criminal activity targeting military equipment as it moves into the
     theater of operations. The same procedures must be followed during redeployment to protect military
     equipment returning to its home station.

     4-103. Base camps can be located alongside military ports and terminals. The CCDR or the joint force
     commander defines the responsibilities of the base camp commander, the military port commander, and the
     senior airfield authority (if one is included in the port area) to ensure a unified effort.

Railway and Railhead Security

     4-104. Military police and CID support the security of railheads, terminals, and railways with LOGSEC. This
     collaboration provides specialized capabilities to enhance security. Similar to air and sea ports, railheads and
     terminals may be collocated with a base camp.

84  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                            Support to Security

     4-105. Military police support Army rail operations by providing local and area security and the specialized
     capability of LOGSEC to facilitate these operations. To effectively plan for these intermodal operations,
     military police must have a solid understanding of the basics involved. (For more information on Army rail
     operations, see ATP 4-14.) It is important to be aware of the key assets associated with rail operations, such
     as tracks, locomotives, rolling stock, switching modes, tunnels, bridges, and marshalling yards, in addition
     to the materiel and personnel being transported. Route security considerations apply to the tracks, while local
     security principles focus on protecting specific sites and points within the rail system. Military police may
     also provide security as part of the security force on the rolling stock, which includes armored trains and cars.
     Many of the basic concepts of convoy security are also applicable in these situations.

DISLOCATED CIVILIANS

     4-106. Controlling DCs has a security component to it, but the primary focus is facilitating movement and
     maneuverability for other forces. While supporting security operations, the primary consideration is to ensure
     that enemy forces and threats are not concealed among the DC population.

     4-107. Chapter 5 discusses the importance of DC control in PRC operations. Additional information is
     provided in appendix A, which details camps related to DC operations. Military police support for populace
     control primarily focuses on DC control.

ANTITERRORISM

     4-108. Antiterrorism consists of defensive measures used to reduce the vulnerability of individuals and
     property to terrorist acts, to include rapid containment by local military and civilian forces (JP 3-26). It is an
     element of protection and part of the broader mission of combating terrorism. All military forces must
     consider AT during their operations. Military police receive extensive training in AT measures, methods for
     assessing threats, implementing preventative actions, and responding to terrorist incidents in both law
     enforcement and broader contexts. They can also advise leaders and staffs from other units on AT
     requirements and response strategies. Additionally, military police are expected to be adept at applying
     various AT measures and activities. For more information, see ATP 3-37.2.

     4-109. Military police identify potential terrorist threats and other threat activities to support the freedom of
     action by U.S. forces. Recognizing these threats is essential for establishing protective measures against
     surprise attacks, observation, detection, interference, espionage, terrorism, and sabotage. By identifying
     threats, U.S. forces can take proactive steps and implement procedures to minimize vulnerabilities to terrorist
     acts or attacks. These efforts aim to reduce personnel vulnerability to terrorism through education that
     enhances understanding of its nature, maintaining a heightened awareness of current threats, and mitigating
     risks by implementing appropriate protective measures.

     4-110. Military police are trained and prepared to quickly respond to terrorist attacks when prevention efforts
     fail. Their emergency response actions include treating casualties, apprehending perpetrators, preserving
     evidence, minimizing property damage, restoring operations, and speeding up the criminal investigation
     process. They also focus on collecting lessons learned from the incident. For more information on this topic,
     see AR 525-13 and ATP 3-37.2 regarding AT.

     4-111. Counterterrorism consists of activities and operations taken to neutralize terrorists and their
     organizations and networks in order to render them incapable of using violence to instill fear and coerce
     governments or societies to achieve their goals (JP 3-26). Counterterrorism actions involve conducting strikes
     and raids against terrorist organizations and their facilities outside the United States and its territories. While
     counterterrorism is a designated mission for certain special operations forces, conventional Army units can
     also play a role. When commanders use conventional forces to target terrorists, they are carrying out offensive
     operations rather than counterterrorism operations. Although counterterrorism is related to AT efforts, it
     typically does not fall under protective measures, as it primarily focuses on offensive actions.

PHYSICAL SECURITY

     4-112. Physical security is that part of security concerned with physical measures designed to safeguard
     personnel; to prevent unauthorized access to equipment, installations, material, and documents; and to
     safeguard them against espionage, sabotage, damage, and theft (JP 3-0). The Army employs comprehensive
     physical security measures to protect personnel, information, and critical resources in various locations and

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  85


---

Chapter 4

    situations against multiple threats. This is achieved by developing and implementing effective security
    policies and procedures. The approach is systematic and relies on continuous analysis and the deployment of
    protective measures, which include physical barriers, clear zones, lighting, biometrics-capable access and
    key control systems, intrusion detection devices, defensive positions, and nonlethal capabilities. For more
    information regarding physical security requirements and procedures, see AR 190-13 and ATP 3-39.32.
    GTA 19‑08‑004 may be a useful reference for nonlethal munitions.

    4-113. Physical security measures are applied in-depth to implement security and AT strategies at static
    locations. These measures are essential for preventing unauthorized access to restricted, controlled, or
    vulnerable areas. It is important to prioritize physical security measures based on available resources and
    vulnerability and threat assessments to effectively protect critical sites, personnel, and equipment. Physical
    security should be integrated with other security measures—such as mobile patrols, operational strategies,
    and information security—as part of a comprehensive security program. Areas that require extensive physical
    security measures may include—

         • Bases, installations, and base camps.
         • Troop housing areas, particularly those with high concentrations of personnel.
         • Storage areas for arms, ammunition, and explosives.
         • Key command posts.
         • Aerial ports of debarkation and embarkation and seaports of debarkation and embarkation.
         • Critical sustainment hubs.
         • Access points and entry control points.

    4-114. Physical security policies, programs, and objectives are approved by the Provost Marshal General,
    under the authority of the Army Deputy Chief of Staff for Operations and Plans. The United States Army
    Military Police School offers a physical security course designed to train physical security inspectors and
    subject matter experts. This course provides military police Soldiers an H3 (Physical Security) identifier.
    Completion of the course is a requirement for physical security inspectors stationed at Army installations,
    posts, camps, and stations. Installation PMs are typically responsible for delivering physical security
    expertise, including conducting periodic inspections to ensure compliance with established physical security
    directives.

    4-115. Military police are experts in physical security procedures and applications. They can offer valuable
    subject matter expertise to commanders and their staffs regarding the safety of personnel and assets. In
    operational environments where strict adherence to physical security standards may not be feasible, military
    police trained in this area can help commanders and staffs develop strategies to address gaps in physical
    security requirements. While military police implement various physical security measures during their
    operations, it is important to note that all Army units are required to implement these measures. Physical
    security measures employed to protect personnel and equipment may include—

         • Establishing checkpoints. Checkpoints are set up to monitor and control the movement of
              personnel and vehicles, inspect cargo, enforce laws and regulations, and provide information. They
              play a critical role in a commander's overall protection strategy and can facilitate effective civil
              control operations. Military police can offer valuable expertise to commanders regarding the
              construction and procedures associated with checkpoint operations. These checkpoints may regulate
              traffic flow, enforce laws, and control movement at significant locations such as border crossings
              or access points to critical facilities. For further details on access control and checkpoint operations,
              see ATP 3-39.32 and TC 19-210.

         • Controlling access to equipment, installations, materials, and documents. Access control
              involves creating a system of complementary and overlapping security measures to regulate access
              to vital resources and information. These measures can include physical barriers, secure zones,
              adequate lighting, access and key control, the issuance of security badges, intrusion detection
              devices, defensive positions, and nonlethal capabilities. (For more information on access control
              and checkpoint operations, see ATP 3-39.32 and TC 19-210.) Additionally, AR 190-11 outlines
              physical security requirements related to access control for arms, ammunition, and explosives, while
              AR 190-13 covers physical security requirements for Army assets other than arms, ammunition, and
              explosives.

         • Employing electronic security systems. The employment of electronic security systems includes
              conducting site surveys and installing and operating these systems to protect Army installations,

86         ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                            Support to Security

                personnel, operations, and critical resources in tactical and nontactical situations. For more
                information on the use of electronic security systems, see ATP 3-39.32.

     4-116. Coordinating the employment of countermobility and survivability measures is essential for
     enhancing the physical security of facilities and sites. Countermobility (see ATP 3-90.8) and survivability
     (see ATP 3-37.34) measures are often linked to specialized entry control strategies and checkpoint operations.
     These measures encompass a variety of barrier types. Additionally, for considerations regarding camouflage
     and concealment that may impact physical security, see ATP 3-37.34.

LOCAL SECURITY

     4-117. Local security is the low-level security activities conducted near a unit to prevent surprise by the
     enemy (ADP 3-90). Local security offers immediate protection to friendly forces. Typically, this is conducted
     by a unit for its defense, but another unit can also provide it if the security needs exceed what a unit or facility
     can offer by itself or requires specialized capabilities that it lacks. Local security may involve countermobility
     and survivability activities. All area security operations benefit from the local security measures implemented
     by various units, regardless of their location within the AO. Furthermore, all local security efforts should be
     connected to the broader area security initiatives.

     4-118. Local security involves measures taken by military units to counter enemy actions. This includes
     avoiding detection by the enemy and misleading them about friendly positions and intentions. Additionally,
     local security focuses on identifying nearby enemy forces and gathering as much information as possible
     about their positions and plans. By maintaining effective local security, a unit can prevent surprises and
     maintain the initiative in operations. This requirement is vital across all military actions, including tactical
     enabling operations. Military police play a crucial role in AT and physical security activities due to their
     specialized training and capabilities. LOGSEC is one specialized capability that military police provide for
     both tasks.

     4-119. Units use both active and passive measures to provide local security. Active measures include—
          • OPs and patrols.
          • The establishment of specific levels of alert in the unit. The commander adjusts those levels based
                on the mission variables.
          • The establishment of stand-to times. The SOPs of a unit detail its activities during the conduct of a
                stand-to.
          • Using UASs.

     4-120. Passive local security measures include—
          • Camouflage and concealment.
          • Movement control.
          • Noise and light discipline.
          • Proper communications procedures.
          • The employment of available sensors, night-vision devices, and daylight sights to maintain
                surveillance over the area immediately around the unit.
          • The incorporation of emission control to prevent the enemy from detecting, identifying, and locating
                friendly forces.

     4-121. When military police provide protection around critical sites or assets, they typically use wheeled
     armored vehicles equipped with crew-served weapons and communication systems to conduct mobile
     patrols. These patrols are staffed by three military police Soldiers who are part of a military police team and
     are organized into squad, platoon, or company size elements. This standoff protection can detect and
     neutralize Level I and Level II enemy threats as they attempt to approach within direct-fire range of facilities
     or assets. Responsibilities may include establishing checkpoints and LPs or OPs. They may also involve
     controlling internal access points to facilities or sites and acting as an initial response force. Additionally,
     military police may provide in-transit security for critical assets moving between locations.

     4-122. Military police can assist HN forces by establishing security measures and identifying vulnerabilities
     in the existing security of critical facilities. Facilities with national, cultural, religious, or military significance
     may require dedicated security forces. Critical assets include power generation plants, water treatment
     facilities, hospitals, police stations, religious structures, and armories.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  87


---

Chapter 4

    4-123.  A military police unit tasked with securing a critical facility or site typically undertakes the following
    tasks:
              Receive and review the mission directive. Begin by understanding the security mission directive,
         •    and initiate troop leading procedures.
         •    Gather information. Collect data from previous reconnaissance reports, other units, or personnel
         •    relieved at a static post.
              Brief squad members. Ensure that all squad members are informed about the following details
         •    before executing the mission:
               ROE, including actions to take upon contact.
         •     The tactical situation and the security defense plan.
         •     General orders.
               Special orders that outline specific duties, responsibilities, and procedures.
              Determine the security method. Decide on the security approach—whether to use static posts or
              mobile patrols—and assign teams to specific mission tasks. If no specific orders are given, the squad
              leader should choose the best method to employ or utilize a combination of both (see appendix C
              for guidance on developing security and mobility support attachments that complement the base
              OPORD).
              Enforce situational awareness. Stress the importance of situational awareness, and ensure that
              certain squad members know the procedures for reporting unusual activity. Soldiers must be familiar
              with their surroundings, including the local CIV and military populace.
              Act as a response force. Respond as quickly as possible to engage in combat operations to destroy
              the enemy.

COUNTER-UNMANNED AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS

    4-124. Military police face a growing threat from small UASs as adversaries take advantage of these
    relatively inexpensive, flexible, and expendable technologies. This proliferation poses challenges,
    particularly regarding attribution and its implications for deterrence. Commanders at every level must find
    ways to counter this air threat and thwart adversarial reconnaissance within their operational environments.
    Small UASs come in various sizes and capabilities, and military police will likely encounter them in all Army
    operations. These systems are difficult to detect as they move across the battlefield, making them a preferred
    method for executing tactical-level strikes. While not every hostile air threat necessitates engagement by air
    defense measures from air and missile defense units, there remains a critical need to detect, identify, and
    prepare to defeat all classes of UASs.

    4-125. Passive air defense, combined arms for air defense, and training for counterreconnaissance tasks
    should be essential components of C-UAS practices of units. Military police should develop and refine
    C-UAS planning tailored to the expected threat environment. They must plan and execute operations as an
    integrated combined arms team, utilizing all available passive air defense techniques when active air defense
    is limited or unavailable. IPOE provides commanders and their staffs specific information about known
    enemy locations, tactics, and capabilities. Military police should operate under the assumption that they are
    being observed by the enemy and should not assume they are protected by air and missile defense units.

    4-126. For any mission, adversary, and environment, military police must reduce its signature. While it is
    possible to reduce their signature, it will never completely mask it. Battle positions, patrol bases, and
    operating bases should be small, dispersed, well-camouflaged, and temporary. For more information on
    threats and C-UAS operations, see ATP 3-01.81.

TECHNIQUES FOR CONDUCTING COUNTER-UNMANNED AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS

    4-127. The use of air guard techniques enhances security operations. An air guard helps mitigate the threat
    posed by UASs, which can conduct reconnaissance, surveillance, and intelligence operations and carry out
    attacks on friendly forces. Air guards must remain vigilant and watch the horizon while employing search
    and scan techniques to detect approaching threat UAVs. They are responsible for monitoring their assigned
    sectors to identify potential launch points and patterns of UAV activity. Early warning is crucial for air
    guards, as they are responsible for alerting their formation of potential air threats. When reporting UAS
    activity, air guards should provide an estimated location of the threat based on their position. They need to
    convey details such as the approximate distance, time, duration, size, estimated elevation, and direction the

UAV was headed at the time of detection. A standard reporting format. For an example of this reporting
format should be used to report a threat UAS, see table 4-2.
