Passive Defense

     4-128. Companies and platoons should be prepared to employ passive defense measures to protect
     themselves from detection, observation, and attack. These measures reduce the effectiveness of enemy
     attacks using UAS. Damage-limiting and attack avoidance strategies are types of passive defense that help
     avoid detection by aerial threats and minimize damage if an attack occurs. Military police must exercise
     caution when using C-UAS passive measures. Commanders should choose advantageous positions that
     provide concealment for Soldiers, equipment, and unit activities. When planning damage-limiting and attack
     avoidance measures, commanders should consider the following passive defense tasks for their forces:

          • Operate at night or during limited visibility. It is important to conduct operations at night or under
                limited visibility conditions. During these times, light restrictions should be practiced, and discipline
                must be maintained to enhance operational effectiveness.

          • Disseminate early warnings of air threats. Providing early warnings of air threats down to the lowest
                echelons is crucial for countering UAS threats.

          • Practice good operational security (OPSEC). OPSEC is a vital component of the planning process.
                Commanders and leaders must consistently enforce OPSEC measures within their units.

          • Use emission control. Limit your electromagnetic and acoustic footprint by selectively and
                strategically using emitters. This optimizes command and control systems while protecting OPSEC.

          • Utilize camouflage and concealment. Camouflage involves using natural or artificial materials to
                disguise personnel and equipment. Concealment focuses on reducing recognition factors.
                Techniques such as hiding, blending in, and disguising help achieve concealment.

          • Employ decoys and deception. Use decoys to create false locations, use smoke to divert attention
                from operations, or employ emitters and emulators to confuse enemy reconnaissance. These tactics
                help conceal unit activities from enemy detection.

          • Hardening. Use protective construction measures and overhead cover to limit potential damage to
                friendly forces and equipment. Hardening and fortifying cover reduces the ability of UASs to
                visually detect targets and limits the impact of aerial attacks.

          • Use obscurants. Use optical and noise-reducing techniques to limit equipment glare and noise.
                Placing mud on headlights and using camouflage nets to obscure the glare of windshields prevents
                drawing attention to their position.

          • Unit dispersion. Disperse assets strategically to minimize detection and damage in the event of an
                attack. The proper dispersion of units and equipment reduces target density, diminishing the
                potential impact of enemy ordnance.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30                                  89


---

Chapter 4

          • Find a concealed site. Conform to terrain by finding low dead ground and micro-terrain behind hills,
                tucked against the shadows of buildings, or under trees. In urban environments, move inside a
                building.

          • Maintain vigilance. The company team must assume they are always vulnerable to enemy targeting
                attempts. This is especially true during troop movements, supply actions, traversing open areas, or
                concentrating at choke points.

          • Establish an early warning network. Establish an air defense early warning network using radios
                and digital networks.

Active Defense

     4-129. Commanders are responsible for taking all necessary actions to protect their forces and equipment
     from attacks while ensuring that their Soldiers operate according to established ROE. When Soldiers
     encounter groups 1 and 2 UAS, they should consider these as threats eligible for engagement unless they can
     be positively identified as nonthreatening. Active measures should include basic rules that aid in identifying
     and defeating threat UASs. For example, commanders should establish SOPs for disseminating weapon
     control status and hostile criteria. Additionally, commanders should consider training their forces on the
     following active measures:

          • Define characteristics for threat UASs. Factors defining the characteristics of threat UASs are speed,
                altitude, location, and heading.

          • Develop and transmit weapon control status. Weapon control status is a control measure designed
                to establish procedures for forces using surface-to-air weapons (including small arms weapons) to
                engage threats. Weapon control statuses can apply to weapon systems, volumes of airspace, or types
                of air platforms. This includes established restricted and engagement zones. Categories of weapons
                control status are:
                 Weapons-free. This indicates that weapons systems may fire at targets not positively identified
                     as friendly. This is the least restrictive weapon control status.
                 Weapons-tight. This indicates that weapons systems may only fire at targets identified as hostile
                     according to the current ROE.
                 Weapons-hold. This indicates that weapons systems may only fire in self-defense or in response
                     to a formal order. This is the most restrictive weapon control status.

          • Use air guard. Designate air guards for every vehicle and position to establish 360-degree security.
                 React to threat UAVs by determining distance and bearing to the threat and take pictures if
                     possible.
                 Immediately report sightings of threat UASs using the SALUTE report as prescribed by
                     ATP 3-01.81, table 3-1.
                 If the position and personnel of the air guards become threatened, respond according to the
                     established unit SOP, which could include moving to alternate positions, engaging the threat
                     UAV with small arms using combined arms for air defense firing techniques, and requesting
                     engagement support with air and missile defense weapon systems and aviation assets.

LOGISTICS SECURITY SUPPORT

     4-130. LOGSEC supports the integrity of the logistics system by preventing, identifying, and investigating
     criminal acts carried out by terrorists, criminal elements, or insider threats. These threats can range from
     actions involving the logistics provider of the United States Army to the military forces on the ground.
     LOGSEC addresses criminal or terrorist activities aimed at diverting, stealing, destroying, substituting
     inferior products, or sabotaging supplies meant for the U.S. military. Additionally, it focuses on protecting
     the transportation infrastructure and conveyances under the control of the United States Army from damage
     or destruction. The LOGSEC mission is the responsibility of all CID field elements, and each level of CID
     should be actively involved as needed to provide cohesive and effective support to the Army. This support
     enhances combat readiness, safety, and security, serving as a combat force multiplier. When CID is absent,
     military police may initiate LOGSEC support for commanders at echelon until CID personnel arrive in the
     theater and establish command and support relationships.

90  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                           Support to Security

     4-131. LOGSEC encompasses the security of the entire logistics system, from the acquisition of materials
     (procurement fraud operations are under the investigative purview of CID through all modes of the
     transportation network and storage sites; retrograde cargo through the transportation system; and
     reutilization, disposal, or demilitarization operations. Supply diversion is normally encountered during in-
     transit movement of supplies or cargo and would fall to the investigative purview of the mainstream CID
     forces.

MILITARY POLICE SUPPORT TO LOGISTICS SECURITY

     4-132. Criminal investigators, as required and assigned, provide investigative support and criminal
     intelligence on the battlefield to each echelon of command, from battalion to echelons above corps. CID will
     assess the threats, capabilities, and intentions of criminal and terrorist elements. Additionally, CID assist the
     supported command in taking actions to eliminate these threats or minimize their impact.

     4-133. The CID LOGSEC mission is critical for accomplishing the combat mission of the Army. LOGSEC
     ensures the integrity of the logistics pipeline, which runs from the manufacturer to the individual combat
     Soldier. It involves preventing, detecting, and investigating criminal and terrorist activities related to HN or
     allied personnel and U.S. personnel. Operations that support the CID LOGSEC mission prevent, detect, and
     investigate criminal interference in the logistics pipeline that could negatively impact the combat readiness
     and effectiveness of the Army. This is achieved by—

          • Enhancing the readiness of the force.
          • Increasing the effectiveness of CID LOGSEC-related activities.
          • Raising awareness of LOGSEC and the role of CID in improving the combat readiness of the Army.

     4-134. Regardless of the port type, the principles of LOGSEC remain consistent, though the specifics change.
     A successful LOGSEC program at a port relies on a three-pronged approach aimed at enhancing the security
     of military property during its transit from the port of embarkation to the port of debarkation and to its final
     destination. This approach includes—

          • Implementation of proactive crime prevention measures designed to eliminate crime-conducive
                conditions within the staging areas of the port of embarkation and the port of debarkation.

          • Surveillance and visual inspection of the cargo and loading and unloading procedures.
          • LOGSEC awareness briefings for supercargo personnel accompanying cargo shipments.

     4-135. Shipping major end items such as tanks, trucks, and weapon systems poses a significant LOGSEC
     challenge to CID. Unlike containers, these items must remain fully accessible to port personnel during the
     loading and discharging processes.

     4-136. Major end items are identified, accounted for, and processed by military terminals in the same way as
     containers. This cargo is controlled by the logistics application of automated marking and reading symbols
     (LOGMARS). With the logistics application of automated marking and reading symbols label, the cargo is
     scanned when it arrives at staging areas, scanned again when it leaves, and then scanned again for manifesting
     onto its designated vessel. During discharge, this process is reversed. However, frequent problems arise in
     accountability due to inaccurate scanning or the loss or absence of logistics application of automated marking
     and reading symbols labels. Major end items are often reported missing when they are not clearly indicated
     on the manifest of the final ship. In many cases, it becomes frustrated cargo.

     4-137. Secondary, or nested cargo, is not controlled by logistics application of automated marking and
     reading symbols and is not listed on the ship manifest. In many cases, this cargo is readily accessible to
     criminal elements. Determining the exact circumstances surrounding the loss of such cargo is nearly
     impossible. Historically, in their eagerness to move supplies and equipment, deploying units have often
     overlooked established security procedures. To prevent losses, it is essential to implement strong security
     measures and conduct proactive threat assessments at the embarkation and debarkation seaports.

     4-138. Previously referred to as frustrated cargo, astray cargo can become a source of frustration.
     Unidentified cargo will eventually be placed in the astray cargo area. Frequently, reports of lost, missing, or
     stolen cargo can be resolved by checking this area. Careful examination should be made of the frustrated
     cargo procedures. Lack of security and inexperienced personnel can result in cargo being stolen, diverted, or
     reissued without accountability.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  91


---

Chapter 4

     4-139. Immediately following the departure of a loaded vessel, a criminal information report will be prepared
     in message format and forwarded to the CID activities responsible for the seaport of debarkation and the final
     destination of the cargo. A criminal information report should contain the following information:

          • Date of departure.
          • Cargo identification and inventory.
          • Intermediate stops.
          • Identity of supercargo personnel.
          • Identify of LOGSEC issues.
          • Estimated time of arrival at the seaport of debarkation.

PHASES OF LOGISTICS SECURITY

     4-140. In the acquisition management phase, the investigative efforts focus on supporting user activities,
     whether from the end users or the acquisition program manager. This investigative support spans from
     concept development and exploration to research and development, engineering, manufacturing, and the
     delivery and acceptance process by the United States government (USG). Major procurement fraud units
     normally engage this segment of LOGSEC.

     4-141. The materiel distribution phase is the most fluid aspect of the logistics pipeline and the most
     vulnerable to criminal activity. This phase involves the movement of materiel through various modes of
     transportation, including land, rail, air, and sea; and material storage and warehousing activities at depots.
     (For more detailed information on intermodal and rail center operations, see ATP 4-13 and ATP 4-14.)
     Depots are responsible for releasing materiel to supply activities or deploying units. Vulnerabilities in
     materiel are most apparent at locations where there are changes in transportation modes, commonly known
     as transportation nodes. During the distribution phase of LOGSEC operations, CID field elements assist in
     investigating supply diversion. Field CID elements routinely conduct these investigations as assets progress
     through the logistics pipeline.

     4-142. Proactive investigative efforts conducted during the materiel distribution phase of LOGSEC include
     LOGSEC awareness briefings and threat assessment support for commanders and their unit movement
     control officers during deployment or redeployment. These efforts help identify and update estimates of
     criminal activity threats, assess risks along transportation routes, and evaluate the potential for economic
     crimes at transportation facilities, storage locations, and military installations. Additionally, LOGSEC threat
     assessments support operations at major transportation nodes and conduct vulnerability assessments at ports
     of debarkation and embarkation.

     4-143. The materiel management phase of LOGSEC should concentrate on ensuring the security of materiel
     after it is delivered from depots or ports to intermediate supply activities, installations, or forward-deployed
     operating bases. At an installation, investigative support is provided to accountable officers at supply support
     activities. This investigative support must be provided to forward-deployed operating bases where materiel
     is warehoused before it is issued to the user.

     4-144. LOGSEC performed during deployment is typically joint in nature, as CCDRs face a variety of
     missions. A forward-deployed LOGSEC mission should begin by establishing effective liaison with all
     interested organizations. This collaboration allows for a more thorough analysis of the mission. The analysis
     should consider the investigative resources of other military criminal investigative organizations, and the
     responsibilities, requirements, and capabilities of each agency and branch of Service. Because of their
     specific focus areas, some Services may have LOGSEC capabilities that are better suited for a specific
     mission than another Service.

THREAT ASSESSMENT PROCESS

     4-145. CID and PM staffs provide police information and conduct intelligence analysis for commanders to
     identify potential crime indicators and criminal threats against Army property, facilities, or personnel. CID
     elements collect, consolidate, analyze, and disseminate criminal intelligence and terrorist counteraction
     operations aimed at U.S. and allied interests and activities on the battlefield.

92  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                     Support to Security

4-146. The CID LOGSEC effort focuses on proactively preventing, detecting, and investigating criminal
activities that lead to logistics and sustainment materiel loss or malfunction. The threat assessment process
consists of the following four steps:

     • Step 1. Identify the threat.
     • Step 2. Identify targets vulnerable to the threat.
     • Step 3. Prioritize identified targets.
     • Step 4. Develop proactive plans of action.

4-147. To implement an effective LOGSEC program, CID field activities should be added to the distribution
list for installation activities that receive reports related to the supported commander's logistics posture. These
reports may be internal or external to the installation and can be prepared by one or more of the following:

     • Army Audit Agency.
     • Defense Contract Audit Agency.
     • Office of Workers' Compensation Programs.
     • Defense Finance and Accounting Services.
     • Government, DOD, and contracting organizations for military Services.
     • Government Accounting Office.
     • Internal Review and Compliance Office.
     • Directorate of Logistics.
     • Movement control team.
     • Theater Movement Control Agency.

4-148. Reviewing and analyzing audit reports and coordinating with the relevant preparation activities can
provide a clear overview of specific logistics operations at the installation. Reports generated by other
agencies can highlight particular issues that may negatively impact the combat capabilities of the Army.
These reports cover a broad range of topics, including logistics systems and their use in support of combat
operations. Other agencies preparing such reports may include the—

     • Department of the Army Inspector General.
     • Department of the Defense Inspector General.
     • Installation inspector general.
     • Logistics Control Activity. Upon request, this activity can provide the ordering history of a specific

          class of supply or item, along with details about the required activity, through the Logistics
          Information File. This file can be accessed by computer modem.
     • Defense Logistics Studies Information Exchange. This information is also accessible by computer
          modem.

4-149. Reports on logistics systems are initiated based on either a cyclic requirement or as needed. These
reports can be obtained from either the Department of Logistics Assistant Chief of Staff for Logistics or the
accountable supply office and include the following:

     • Unit status reports. These reports provide the status of Soldiers and their materials by unit.
     • Material release denials.
     • Report of discrepancies.
     • Discrepancy in shipment reports.
     • Quality deficiency reports.
     • Inventory adjustment reports.
     • Reports of survey.
     • Sample data collection reports.

4-150. Unit commanders generate reports such as AR 15-6 investigations and statements of charges. These
reports are typically initiated when property is destroyed or lost or when accountability for an item has been
lost. This can occur at the installation level, within the unit, or for an individual Soldier.

4-151. Additional sources of information regarding the local criminal threat include local police, HN police,
MI, and counterintelligence. Informants and sources used by the CID drug suppression team can also be
leveraged to gather intelligence on economic crimes and LOGSEC issues. It is common for both drug users
and dealers to trade or steal government property to support their activities.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  93

Chapter 4

DEVELOPMENT OF CRIMINAL INTELLIGENCE

     4-152. The development of criminal intelligence at the operational level in support of LOGSEC must be
     directed at assessing the criminal threat of the adversary, whether it is an enemy military unit, terrorist
     elements, the HN, allied personnel, or U.S. personnel. The focus on the development of operational-level
     intelligence is broad and includes the following factors:

          • Political.
          • Economic.
          • Sociological.
          • Technological.
          • Opportunity.

     4-153. These factors can significantly influence the nature of threats at the operational level. Developing this
     type of intelligence requires access to information not typically developed or obtained by CID. To assist in
     the development of operational-level intelligence, whether in a peacetime or combat environment, the
     military police and special agent should—

          • Assess the situation and identify the threats.
          • Identify targets that may be vulnerable to these threats.
          • Analyze and convert information into usable criminal intelligence.
          • Target activities based on the gathered criminal intelligence.
          • Identify, investigate, or deter and prevent criminal activities.
          • Recognize conditions or procedures conducive to criminal activity.
          • Minimize or eliminate opportunities for criminal offenses or criminal activity.
          • Understand the criminal environment.

     4-154. The engagement of police and the subsequent collection of information remains a primary focus for
     military police. PIO is vital for proper situational understanding, given the linkage to criminal, terrorist, and
     insurgent threats. However, some police intelligence may not be shared with the intelligence community due
     to intelligence oversight regulations and procedures regarding collecting information on U.S. citizens, U.S.
     corporations, and non-U.S. citizen residents. For more information, see ATP 3-39.20 and DODM 5240.01.

     4-155. Initial collected information may indicate potential problems, such as product substitution, supply
     diversion, or other criminal activities. This information can arise from a commander's situation or after-action
     report, which may highlight issues such as shortages of fuel, spare parts, ammunition, or other critical items
     that delayed or prevented mission completion. This information should be disseminated by the most
     expeditious means available to the widest audience possible to assist in the further development of the
     information, even if it may still be fragmented, incomplete, or cryptic. Over-classification can limit the use
     of this information and should be avoided.

     4-156. Agents assigned to support combat operations are responsible for identifying conditions and indicators
     that may suggest criminal activity. Follow-up investigative work involving fragmentary information and
     occurring outside the area of combat operations will be conducted by CID in the continental United States or
     at the location where the crime occurred. Threat assessment instruments used within this arena include the
     following:

          • A LOGSEC threat assessment. This assessment—
                 Is a crime prevention survey of a logistics activity.
                 Is performed in the same manner as a crime prevention survey.
                 Reports findings and observations.
                 Provides recommendations.
                 Is initiated by CID or can be requested by commanders.

          • A criminal activity threat assessment. This assessment—
                 Is performed separately from all other reports.
                 May be incorporated into a LOGSEC threat assessment or a port vulnerability assessment.
                 Reports on specific criminal threats to a specific installation or activity.
                 Identifies crime trends and threats.
                 Requires collection and correlation of information collected from federal, state, and local law
                     enforcement agencies.

94  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                           Support to Security

          • A port vulnerability assessment. This assessment—
                 Is a crime prevention survey of a sea or aerial port of debarkation or port of embarkation.
                 Is performed the same as a LOGSEC threat assessment.
                 Is formatted as prescribed by headquarters.
                 Incorporates information from the criminal activity threat assessment into the body of the
                     report.

     4-157. These assessments help identify areas of potential criminal vulnerability associated with the logistical
     flow of supplies leaving wholesale activity and being delivered to the most forward-deployed units. All
     traveled routes and stops should be evaluated, with an emphasis on the security and safety of the following:

          • Depots and supply nodes.
          • Airports, railheads and terminals, and seaports.
          • Air, land, and water transportation nodes.

COORDINATION WITH OTHER AGENCIES

     4-158. A multitude of federal, state, and local government agencies and activities oversee the ports,
     waterways, railroad infrastructure, highways, and other transportation facilities used by the military.
     Additionally, several DOD and Army commands conduct assessments of transportation infrastructure
     vulnerability and physical security. This includes port-integrated vulnerability assessments done by the U.S.
     Navy and the Interagency Commission on Crime and Security in U.S. Seaports. Other agencies, such as the
     Defense Threat Reduction Agency, perform balanced survivability assessments, while high-quality joint staff
     integrated vulnerability assessments are conducted by the Joint Staff (J-34/Combating Terrorism) of the
     DOD. The U.S. Coast Guard has an Office of Investigations and Analysis that is a valuable resource for
     conducting LOGSEC assessments. The Area Maritime Security lead, done by the U.S. Coast Guard captain
     of the port, can be another good resource when conducting LOGSEC assessments. Additional resources for
     conducting LOGSEC assessments include open sources such as the American Association of Port
     Authorities, the National Waterways Conference, the American Waterways Operators, the Center for Ports
     and Waterways of the Texas Transportation Institute, and international organizations like the International
     Maritime Organization. Other relevant U.S. agencies include the Department of Transportation, the
     Department of Commerce, the Department of the Interior, the Maritime Administration, the U.S. Army Corps
     of Engineers, the National Geospatial Intelligence Agency, the Transportation Security Administration, and
     the Environmental Protection Agency.

     4-159. Past assessments provide valuable guidance on the expertise needed to assemble a team of specialists
     for conducting a LOGSEC assessment. Previous assessment teams have included experts in counterterrorism,
     structural engineering, emergency operations, information operations, security operations, utility operations,
     communications, and mission operations. Since the onset of the Global War on Terrorism, the Federal Bureau
     of Investigation, leading a joint terrorism TF, has collected intelligence on a wide range of potential terrorist
     activities and targets. When CID receives a request for LOGSEC support from a senior Army commander,
     and the scope of the mission is defined, CID field elements may discover that much of the necessary
     groundwork has already been completed by one or more of the aforementioned agencies. Therefore,
     coordination with other agencies involved in transportation security is imperative.

     4-160. LOGSEC support to military commanders includes logistics-related crime prevention efforts,
     LOGSEC awareness briefings, and criminal activity threat assessments. These operations aim to enhance the
     accountability, security, and visibility of the Army concerning materiel, personnel, and data within the
     logistics pipeline. This term refers to the entire process of acquiring, producing, transporting, and maintaining
     military assets. It encompasses acquisition management, materiel distribution, materiel management, and
     property disposal.

                                                 Chapter 5

          Support to Populace and Resources Control

    PRC is conducted in conjunction with and as an integral part of all military operations. PRC
    consists of two distinct yet linked components: populace control and resources control.
    Typically, these components are managed by local civil governments. The appropriate
    authorities define, implement, and enforce PRC measures in civil or military emergencies. For
    practical and security reasons, military forces apply PRC measures throughout military
    operations. Military police play a key role in supporting PRC, primarily focusing on reducing
    CIV interference with military activities. However, ensuring the safety of DCs is also a crucial
    part of the military police mission. Many activities associated with mobility operations (chapter
    3) and security operations (chapter 4) are also relevant to providing military police support to
    PRC. Whether dealing with uncontrolled CIV movements within the operational environment
    or illegal activities by certain sectors of the population (which can disrupt resource control),
    military police must understand and integrate PRC measures in both the planning and execution
    of operations.

POPULACE CONTROL

     5-1. Populace control provides security for the indigenous populace, mobilizes human resources, denies
     enemy access to the population, and identifies and reduces the effectiveness of enemy agents. This involves
     establishing public order and safety, securing borders, protecting population centers and individuals, holding
     people accountable for criminal activities, managing the actions of individuals or groups that may pose
     security risks, reestablishing essential civil services, and creating conditions in the operational area that
     support stability through unity of effort. Populace control may become necessary due to military operations,
     man-made disasters, or natural disasters.

     5-2. International law requires that military forces focus on essential tasks that create a safe and secure
     environment while addressing the immediate humanitarian needs of the local population. Measures for
     controlling the populace can include curfews, movement restrictions, travel permits, registration cards, and
     CIV resettlements. The decision on which populace control measures to implement requires a framework
     that can be applied across the spectrum of military operations, ranging from stable peace to general warfare.
     Two special categories of populace control—DC operations and NEO—require extensive planning and
     coordination among various military and nonmilitary organizations.

ROLES AND RESPONSIBILITIES FOR POPULACE CONTROL

     5-3. Populace control is typically the responsibility of HN CIV governments. U.S. forces may take on
     populace control when HN CIV authorities or agencies are unable or unwilling to control the CIV population.
     Populace control and resources control are escalated during CIV or military emergencies. In a permissive
     environment, U.S. forces carry out populace control measures with the consent of the local government. In a
     hostile environment, these measures are implemented according to international law and the laws of war.

     5-4. Population control typically requires integrated and synchronized CMO as an inherent command
     responsibility. These operations involve the activities undertaken by commanders to establish and maintain
     relationships with civil authorities, the general population, and other organizations. The assistant chief of
     staff, CA operations (G-9), or the battalion or brigade CA operations staff officer (S-9) serves as the primary
     staff integrator for CMO. The G-9 or S-9 is responsible for developing plans that use U.S. and multinational
     forces, along with all available NGOs and resources, to enhance CMO efforts. Given the potential impact on
     the CIV population and the movement of HN assets and personnel, CA forces are responsible for planning
     support for PRC.

     5-5. The G-9 or S-9 enhances the relationship between military forces, CIV authorities, and personnel in the
     AO to ensure mission success. The responsibilities and functions of the G-9 or S-9 can vary depending on
     the operational echelon. Military police units may be deployed to support CMO globally, and those involved

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  97


---

Chapter 5

     in CMO must be briefed and understand the intent of these operations. The function of PIO (see ATP 3‑39.20)
     is a significant enabler during CMO, as is the proper treatment of all categories of detainees and DCs.
     Maintaining an appropriate mindset and good situational awareness is critical. U.S. Armed Forces may be
     tasked with alleviating human suffering, such as following a natural disaster. Appropriate disciplinary
     measures and controls are enacted to meet each situation in these situations.

SUPPORTING ORGANIZATIONS

     5-6. PRC involves coordinating and synchronizing activities among multiple CIV agencies and military
     organizations, with significant participation from military police. The organizations supporting PRC include
     a wide range of military and nonmilitary participants with divergent missions. The agencies involved in PRC
     typically come from the joint community, interagency organizations, NGOs, international organizations, and
     HN or multinational entities.

     5-7. Because there are typically many different organizations involved, achieving a unified effort is critical
     to avoiding duplication of effort. Effective unified action relies on close coordination, liaison, and a common
     purpose for mission success. For more information on supporting organizations, see ATP 3-57.10.

GENERAL PLANNING CONSIDERATIONS

     5-8. The specific planning focus of PRC may vary at different levels of command. It will depend on the type
     and nature of the operation and other relevant aspects of the operational environment. All commands and
     national and international agencies involved must have clearly defined responsibilities. When planning and
     executing PRC, consider the following actions:

          • Coordinate with the Department of State (DOS), the United Nations Office for the Coordination of
                Humanitarian Affairs, and HN civil and military authorities to identify the appropriate levels and
                types of aid required and available.

          • Minimize outside contributions by providing only basic needs items until DCs become self-
                sufficient and encourage them to achieve as much independence as possible.

          • Review the effectiveness of humanitarian responses regularly and adjust relief activities as
                necessary.

          • Coordinate with CA units to ensure collaboration with the United States, HN, and international
                organizations such as the United Nations Children's Fund and the Cooperative for Assistance and
                Relief Everywhere. Receiving assistance from these organizations leverages their experience and
                reduces the demands on U.S. Armed Forces.

          • Implement security restrictions as necessary for DCs. Although DCs have the right to freedom of
                movement under international law, security considerations may require restrictions in the event of
                a mass influx of DCs.

     5-9. The planning scope for PRC often differs from the actual task execution, depending on the command
     level. This variation also depends on the type and nature of the operation and other relevant aspects in the
     operational environment. Military police must have a basic understanding of the planning CA units conduct
     for PRC.

     5-10. The theater commander issues directives regarding the care, control, and management of DCs based on
     national policy directives and other political efforts. These directives include general guidance on mitigating
     the challenges DCs pose. Potential ways for mitigating the DC situation include preventing dislocations (for
     example, implementing a stay-put policy), avoiding situations that cause dislocations, controlling the
     movement of DCs, or employing a combination of these approaches. The PRC operations plan—

          • Includes procedures for migration and evacuation.
          • Establishes minimum standards for care.
          • Provides definitions regarding the status and management of DCs.
          • Establishes designated routes and movement control measures.
          • Considers cultural and dietary needs.
          • Includes information on the plans, routes, and areas of concentration related to DCs that all

                concerned staff elements need.
          • Provides measures to alleviate suffering.

98  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                       Support to Populace and Resources Control

          • Establishes protocols for enforcing order and discipline within facilities to ensure the security and
                safety of both DCs and military personnel.

          • Provides an aggressive information program utilizing support agencies and leadership from the DC
                community.

     5-11. PRC measures may require large groups of CIVs to be temporarily quartered for less than six months
     or semipermanently for more than six months. Military police may be assigned to establish, manage, and
     operate DC camps in close coordination with CA forces, HN agencies, USG agencies, psychological
     operations units, NGOs, international humanitarian organizations, and other interested entities. A military
     police unit commander may be designated as the commander of the DC camp. For more information, see
     appendix B.

RULE OF LAW

     5-12. Planning for the follow-on phase after major combat operations should begin during the early stages of
     war planning. Efforts to transition to the rule of law must commence as soon as stability tasks are underway,
     often occurring simultaneously with major combat operations. First, it is essential to establish security
     conditions and initiate political conditions that support policing and judicial and corrections systems,
     ensuring fair and equitable treatment of citizens by the HN government. Achieving this can be challenging,
     as many nations lack the foundational elements necessary for the rule of law and may need coaching,
     mentoring, training, and ongoing support to move toward a peaceful existence. Additionally, if combat
     operations do not focus on shaping the area for stability, organized crime groups, insurgents, and violent
     extremist organizations may attempt to control the population and the emerging government, making efforts
     to establish the rule of law significantly more difficult. Citizens must have the right to ownership, fair
     treatment by policing and judicial systems, and corrections systems that enforce compliance with the law
     equitably and uniformly. Military police forces are particularly well-suited to provide assistance and training
     to the HN, focusing primarily on two of the three legs of the rule of law—the policing and corrections aspects.

     5-13. Military police units should establish relationships with HN police and local officials in the operational
     area as early as possible in the operation. This proactive approach will enhance efficiency during the
     transition period. Building these relationships will also provide valuable police intelligence, which will help
     the unit plan and recommend COA to establish the rule of law.

     5-14. Whether in a transitional military authority role or supporting a legitimate HN government, military
     police support the PRC plan to mitigate the impact of criminal activity through resources control measures.
     These actions may include—

          • Securing critical commodity stocks from theft or pillage.
          • Supporting border security and customs enforcement.
          • Identifying and mitigating black market activities dealing in critical commodities.
          • Protecting stockpiles of natural resources.

     5-15. Military police support for transitioning to the rule of law may include—
          • Assisting in establishing and training local police officers in basic and advanced law enforcement
                skills and in the operation of police stations.
          • Aiding in developing requests for necessary law enforcement equipment and sustainment items for
                local law enforcement agencies.
          • Conducting joint patrols.
          • Operating police stations.
          • Mentoring HN personnel until they are capable of independent operations.

     5-16. Military police detention units can aid in establishing correctional operations in a reopened or
     temporary facility. They can also train the guard forces and prison operations staff to run a facility effectively.

     5-17. All these activities build confidence in the local population that law-abiding citizens will be treated
     fairly and equitably and that laws will be applied equally and uniformly to everyone. Citizens need assurance
     that they will not be unlawfully detained or imprisoned. They must also be confident that unlawful activities
     others commit will be policed and punished.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  99


---

Chapter 5

     5-18. The rule of law cannot flourish or be maintained in crime-ridden environments where citizens fear for
     their safety due to criminal activity. Resources control measures specifically targeted to deter criminal
     activity contribute toward building the trust and confidence of the local populace while strengthening the
     legitimacy of the HN government or provisional authority. For more information on the rule of law, see
     ATP 3-39.10.

RULES FOR THE USE OF FORCE

     5-19. Rules for the use of force (RUF) used in PRC vary depending on the operation. The CCDR establishes
     RUF in coordination with the staff judge advocate and with approval from the joint staff. They also approve
     special RUF created for use in resettlement facilities. The RUF is designed to adapt to changing
     circumstances, ensuring the protection and safety of the DC population and U.S. military personnel. RUF
     should remain simple and understandable so that Soldiers are not confused and do not have to memorize
     extensive checklists. Standing RUF applies to 10 USC military police conducting operations in the United
     States and its territories unless there is specific additional guidance from the SecDef. After consulting with
     their servicing judge advocate, commanders may also submit requests for supplemental RUF for approval by
     the SecDef.

     5-20. The RUF can authorize nonlethal measures at any time during an operation to protect Soldiers and DCs
     from injury. Nonlethal weapons may include items such as riot batons, pepper spray, stun guns, and shotguns
     loaded with nonlethal munitions. For any operation that employs nonlethal weapons to succeed, extensive
     preparation is essential, with individual and unit training vital. Training should be designed to give
     individuals an understanding of the entire subject area and enable them to function efficiently as members of
     a unit or independently. To achieve this, the training must be intensive and realistic. Familiarity with
     nonlethal weapon capabilities is critical to ensure that personnel can deploy these tools with confidence and
     proficiency. The RUF may allow less-than-lethal force to protect mission-essential equipment from damage
     or destruction. Mission-essential equipment includes tactical and nontactical vehicles, communications
     equipment, weapons, computers, and office and personal items.

RULES OF INTERACTION

     5-21. The rules of interaction serve as a guideline for Soldiers when interacting with CIV populations. Rules
     of interaction include—

          • Treating all evacuees humanely and with respect.
          • Avoiding discussions of politics and policies with DCs.
          • Avoiding promises. If pressed, respond with, "I will see what I can do."
          • Refraining from making obscene gestures, as DCs may understand their meaning.
          • Avoiding derogatory remarks, as DCs may comprehend English, and local linguists certainly do.
          • Treating all DCs equally, as some may feel offended if they do not receive the same treatment or

                resources as others.
          • Respecting religious articles and materials.
          • Treating medical issues seriously and maintaining a professional relationship.
          • Greeting DCs in their native language.

DISLOCATED CIVILIAN OPERATIONS

     5-22. Dislocated civilian, or DC, is a broad term primarily used by the DOD that includes a displaced person,
     an evacuee, an internally displaced person, a migrant, a refugee, or a stateless person (JP 3-29). Legal and
     political factors define the categories of DCs. These individuals leave or are forced to leave their homes or
     usual places of residence due to several reasons, including fear of persecution, armed conflict, widespread
     violence, violations of human rights, natural disasters, man-made disasters, or economic hardship. The
     categories of DCs include:

          • Displaced person. Displaced person is a broad term used to refer to internally and externally
                displaced persons collectively (JP 3-29).

          • Refugee. A refugee is a person who, owing to a well-founded fear of being persecuted for reasons
                of race, religion, nationality, membership of a particular social group, or political opinion, is outside
                the country of their nationality and is unable or, owing to such fear, is unwilling to avail themselves
                of the protection of that country (see JP 3-29).

100  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                       Support to Populace and Resources Control

          • Evacuee. An evacuee is a civilian removed from a place of residence by military direction for
                reasons of personal security or the requirements of the military situation (JP 3-57).

          • Stateless person. A stateless person is a person who is not considered as a national by any state
                under the operation of its law (JP 3-29).

          • War victim. This is a classification created during the Vietnam era to describe CIVs suffering
                injuries, loss of a Family member, or damage to or destruction of their homes because of war. War
                victims may be eligible for a claim against the United States under the Foreign Claims Act.

          • Migrants. A migrant is a person who belongs to a normally migratory culture who may cross
                national boundaries or has fled their native country for economic reasons rather than fear of political
                or ethnic persecution (JP 3-29).

          • Internally displaced persons. An internally displaced person is any person who has been forced
                or obliged to flee or to leave their home or places of habitual residence, in particular as a result of
                or to avoid the effects of armed conflict, situations of generalized violence, violations of human
                rights or natural or human-made disasters and who has not crossed an internationally recognized
                state border (JP 3-29).

          • Returnee. A returnee is a displaced person who has returned voluntarily to his or her former place
                of residence (JP 3-29).

          • Resettled person. A resettled person is a refugee or an internally displaced person wishing to return
                somewhere other than his or her previous home or land within the country or area of original
                displacement (JP 3-29).

     5-23. The goals of DC operations are to protect CIVs from the effects of violence or disasters and to reduce
     CIV interference with military operations. These activities typically include controlling the movement of
     CIVs (see chapter 3). DC activities are carried out throughout Army operations but are most commonly
     conducted in support of stability operations and DSCA. The authority to approve these operations within
     U.S. territories lies with the SecDef and may require a special exception to the Posse Comitatus Act (18 USC
     1385). The Posse Comitatus Act prohibits 10 USC (federal status) U.S. military from enforcing CIV laws
     within the United States or its territories without specific authorization.

     5-24. The U.S. Constitution, along with federal, state, and local laws, can significantly influence operations
     within the United States and its territories if enforcement of CIV law is required. Using 10 USC U.S. military
     forces for law enforcement tasks in these situations requires authorization from Congress, such as under the
     Insurrection Statutes (10 USC 331-334), or a Constitutional authorization, like the President invoking his
     Executive Authority under Article 2 of the Constitution. The Posse Comitatus Act does not restrict National
     Guard Soldiers operating in a nonfederal status (32 USC or state active-duty status). For further information,
     see ADP 3-28 and JP 3-28.

     5-25. Planning and conducting DC activities is a collective effort involving CA forces and military police,
     who coordinate with the supported command and other CIV organizations within the operational area. The
     United Nations, other international organizations, and NGOs provide basic assistance and services for the
     affected population. However, the U.S. military may be requested to offer support when necessary. This
     support can include establishing and operating DC camps; providing essential care such as food, medical
     treatment, and protection; and assisting with the movement or relocation of DCs. Military police units are
     particularly well-suited for this mission because many skills required for effective DC camps overlap with
     those needed in policing and detention operations. These skills include enforcing curfews, implementing
     movement restrictions, managing travel permits and registration cards, establishing checkpoints, overseeing
     amnesty programs, and conducting inspections. Additional details about DC camps are discussed in
     appendix B.

MILITARY POLICE SUPPORT TO DISLOCATED CIVILIAN
OPERATIONS

     5-26. The first objective of military police support for DC operations is to control the movement of DCs.
     This ensures that their presence does not obstruct military forces and protects them from avoidable hazards.
     Effective movement control is critical when managing large groups of DCs. Military police incorporate the
     movement of these CIVs into a comprehensive traffic control plan, which is developed in close coordination
     with movement control, CA planners, and other staff members. Additional tasks related to DC activities

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  101


---

Chapter 5

     (conducted within the framework of police operations) include enforcing curfews, restricting movements,
     issuing travel permits and registration cards, establishing checkpoints, implementing amnesty programs, and
     conducting inspections. While military police may provide limited support during the initial contact with
     DCs, efforts are focused on quickly moving them to designated HN-controlled sites where they can receive
     assistance.

     5-27. When performing DC activities, the level of control is typically drastically different from that of the
     majority of those interned during detainee operations. In detainee operations, there is a high level of control
     and supervision due to significant and obvious security risks. However, in activities involving DCs,
     individuals are granted freedom of movement as long as it does not interfere with ongoing operations. While
     security risks are always present, measures must be implemented to manage these risks to an acceptable level.
     Counterinsurgency operations can influence DC activities and may blur the distinctions between DCs and
     detainee operations. For more detailed information on detainee operations, see FM 3-63.

     5-28. The mitigation of casualties among CIVs is always a priority. Military police may use various
     techniques to achieve this goal while conducting activities supporting DCs or NEOs. A discussion on the
     rules of interaction, RUF, and the rule of law is included in the following discussion of NEOs. For more
     information on DC operations, please see ATP 3-57.10.

DISLOCATED CIVILIAN SITUATION TEMPLATE

     5-29. Military police planners consider several factors when creating a situation template for DC movements.
     Key considerations include the time and distance involved (such as the length of a DC column), the gaps
     between individual DCs and groups of DCs, traffic density (usually measured in DCs per kilometer), and the
     rate of march. Once these factors are established, they can be illustrated in a movement graph (a time-space
     diagram that visually represents DC movements within a specific area). This situation template is a
     foundation for planning and helps coordinate route usage within the movement control plan for operational
     forces. For more information, see ATP 3‑57.10.

Dislocated Civilian Route Planning

     5-30. Military police and those involved in planning consider several factors when developing movement
     routes (figure 5-1, page 103) for DCs:

          • Route selection. When selecting routes for CIV movement, military police consider the types of
                transportation commonly used in the area. The PM typically coordinates the proposed traffic control
                plan with the movement control officer and the G-9 or S-9. All DC movements occur on designated
                CIV evacuation routes, which HN requirements may influence.

          • Route identification. After designating movement routes, plans are made to ensure that these routes
                are marked in languages and symbols that CIVs and U.S. and multinational forces can understand.
                Psychological operations units; military police units; and other joint, multinational, and HN military
                forces can assist in marking the routes according to agreed-upon standards.

          • Control and assembly points. Once the movement routes are selected and marked, control and
                assembly points are established at key intersections. These points are coordinated among military
                police, CA, transportation, and logistics planners and captured in the traffic control plan.

          • Emergency rest areas. Emergency rest areas are set up at congested points to address immediate
                needs such as water, food, fuel, maintenance, and medical support.

          • Local and national agencies. Collaborating with local and national agencies conserves military
                resources and minimizes the need for interpreters and translators. CIV authorities usually have the
                legal status necessary to effectively manage their people.

          • Routing fundamentals. Planners use the following key principles when developing routes for DCs:
                 Balance. The characteristics of each route must align with the needs of the traffic it supports.
                     This includes identifying the route maintenance and improvement requirements and
                     implementing protective measures.
                 Separation. Adequate road space must be allocated to prevent conflicts and minimize the
                     potential for congestion.
                 Distribution. The number of routes provided should be sufficient to meet demand and avoid
                     congestion. Distributing traffic across multiple routes also enhances passive security.

102  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                           Support to Populace and Resources Control

• Routing principles. When creating routes for DCs, planners take the following principles into
     account:
      Assign the highest-priority traffic to routes that minimize time and distance.
      Consider the sustainability of the route network when assigning movements.
      Separate motor vehicle movements from pedestrian movements.
      Ensure that CIV traffic (both vehicular and pedestrian) is kept separate from military
          movements.

DC
                   Figure 5-1. Example of dislocated civilian route planning

Dislocated Civilian Movement Planning Factors

     5-31. When creating a situation template for DC movements, planners must consider several variables or
     factors. These factors assume a controlled movement and apply to all types of DC movements, regardless of
     size. Planners evaluate the values of these variables based on logical assumptions until they can be confirmed
     through observation. For DCs moving in denied areas, it may be beneficial for planners to request support
     from UAS to gather accurate information. DC movement planning factors include—

          • Distance factors:
                 Dislocated civilian road space (DCRS). Used in determining time length of the column.

         Note. DCRS consists of two parts: (1) the space occupied by one DC alone and distance between
         another DC, and (2) the sum of the distance between elements of a number of DC foot columns.
         (Total DCRS = road space [individual DC] + DCRS column distances.)

          DC column gap. The space between two organized DC elements following each other on the
              same route.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30                                                    103


---

Chapter 5

           DC traffic density. The average number of DCs that occupy 1 kilometer—expressed in DCs
               per kilometer (DC/km).

           Length of DC column. The length of roadway occupied by a column, including gaps, measured
               from front to rear inclusive.

           Road gap. The distance between two DC march elements.
     • Rate factors:

           Speed. The actual rate of speed at a given moment.
           Pace. The regulated speed of a DC column or element set by the column.
           Rate of march. The average number of kilometers traveled in any given period of time,

               including short delays or periodic halts—expressed in kilometers per hour (km/h).

     • Time factors (must be adjusted for demographic of column, health, and weather conditions):
           Arrival time. The time when the head of the DC column arrives at a designated point.
           Clearance time. The time when the last of a DC column passes a designated point.
           Completion time. The time when the last element of a DC column passes a designated point.
           Extra time allowance. Time added, based on assessment of situation, to the pass time.
           Pass time. Actual time required for a DC column, from the first to the last element, to pass a
               given point.
           Road clearance time. The total time a DC column requires to travel over and clear a section
               of road.
           Time distance. The time required to move from one point to another at a given rate of march.
           Time gap. Time measured between rear and front of successive DC columns as they move past
               any given point.

     • Formulas:
           Distance = Rate x Time.
           Distance/Time = Rate.

           Distance/Rate = Time.
           EXAMPLE: Determine Time distance of a DC column moving on foot traveling 20 kilometers

               at a rate of 4 km/h. Answer: Time distance = 20 km ÷ 4 km/h = 5 hours.

       Note. An additional time allowance of 3 hours has been added based on the assessment of the
       demographic composition of the DC column, which includes women, children, and the elderly,
       and current weather conditions. The head of the DC column is expected to reach the completion
       point in approximately 8 hours.

           Completion Time = Start Point + Time Length (TL) + Scheduled Halts + Extra time allowance.
     • Time Length, Foot Column (Rate Formula) (table 5-1), shows an example of individual space

          factors for day and night DC movement):
           0 km/h TL (min) = road space (meters) × .0150.
           3.2 km/h TL (min) = road space (meters) × .0187.
           2.4 km/h TL (min) = road space (meters) × .0250.
           1.6 km/h TL (min) = road space (meters) × .0375.

       Note. The DC movement rate of 4 km/h during the day slows to 3.2 km/h at night. The cross-
       country DC movement rate of 2.4 km/h during the day slows to 1.6 km/h at night.

Dislocated Civilian Movement Graph

     5-32. A DC movement graph (see figure 5-2) is a time-space diagram that visually represents the movement
     of DCs from the starting point to the completion point. This graph is used during the movement-planning
     phase to integrate and coordinate efforts, prevent congestion along the route, and deconflict route usage with
     the military highway regulation and traffic circulation plan. It is also used to prepare or check the DC road
     movement table. The graph illustrates the relative time and location of both the lead and trailing elements of
     each march column at any given point along the route and the arrival and clearance times of DC columns at
     critical points and restrictions or congestion in the network.

Legend:        dislocated civilian
DC

               Figure 5-2. Dislocated civilian movement graph

5-33. Planners directly transfer information from march formulas or DC march tables onto the graph. To
complete the movement graph, they must determine the time-distance, arrival time, and pass time for each
identified column based on data collected from organized DC columns.

CONTROLLING MOVEMENT

5-34. Controlling movement is a common mission for military police. Military police may use techniques
such as blocking, clearing, and collecting to control DC movement. These techniques depend on detailed
planning and assessment.

Blocking

     5-35. Roadblocks may be accompanied by checkpoints to prevent DCs from accessing roads or areas
     essential for military operations. The purpose of blocking is to stop these CIVs from entering these sensitive

25 March 2026                       ATP 3-39.30                105


---

Chapter 5

     locations and to redirect them to alternative areas, such as returning to their homes or along a designated DC
     route.

     5-36. Depending on the security situation and other factors, CIVs and their means of transport may or may
     not be searched at the blocking position. The following questions must be considered when planning blocking
     operations:

          • What is the expected timing, direction, route, rate, and flow of DCs? (This information is required
                to position forces where and when they are most needed.)

          • Where is the terrain that canalizes DCs?
          • Does the ability exist to reinforce a roadblock under pressure?
          • Does the flexibility exist to disengage on order?

Clearing

     5-37. Clearing involves directing DCs away from MSRs, ASRs, and other areas of military significance to
     prevent interference with operations. This process is carried out at the small-unit level by assigned Soldiers
     or specialized teams specifically tasked with confronting DCs. Their goal is to safely remove these
     individuals from their current location and guide them to the designated area determined by the commander,
     which may sometimes be the shoulder of the road.

     5-38. Clearing is designed for fast-paced, unit-level operations and is ineffective for managing large-scale
     DC missions. It must be deliberately planned and integrated with other control techniques. The primary
     purpose of clearing is to channel or direct DCs away from military operations, installations, or encampments
     until better-organized DC activities, such as collecting, can manage them. Some of the challenges of clearing
     operations include the following:

          • The clearing technique is temporary; units must continuously guide or redirect new or returning
                DCs.

          • External support is often necessary to communicate the intended message in a manner that DCs can
                understand.

          • DCs pose an ongoing security concern for friendly forces, including the threat of terrorist acts like
                car or suicide bombings.

          • The resources of a unit can quickly become overwhelmed if the number of DCs is large or if they
                require emergency assistance.

Collecting

     5-39. Collecting provides positive control over DCs in designated holding areas. This helps prevent
     interference with operations and facilitates their care and processing. The resource-intensive collection plan
     must be carefully coordinated and synchronized with operations, logistics, and security plans. Identifying
     DCs during collection may not always be necessary; this decision depends on guidance from higher
     headquarters, CA units, the HN, and other agencies. The need for identification can vary with each operation.
     Identification of DCs might be necessary for the following reasons:

          • To verify rosters against the actual population.
          • To provide timely reunification of Family members.
          • To match DCs with their medical records in case of a medical emergency or evacuation.
          • To check the identities of DCs against the transfer roster.
          • To identify individuals sought by HN, multinational, or U.S. forces.

     5-40. Existing HN facilities should be considered as collection points. Collecting must be planned and
     executed in collaboration with HN authorities, NGOs, and international governmental organizations
     specializing in public health, public safety, public communications, transportation, public works and utilities,
     and mass care and feeding. Its main features are:

          • Collection points. These are temporary holding areas for gathering small numbers of DCs before
                they are moved to assembly areas or DC camps. Units establishing DC collection points should
                provide minimal emergency relief supplies to address immediate needs for a short duration (one day
                to three or four days). This may include items such as water and trauma first aid.

106  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                 Support to Populace and Resources Control

• DC routes. These routes protect DCs by diverting them from the main activities of military,
     logistics, or humanitarian assistance operations.

• Emergency rest areas. These designated locations along routes provide DCs immediate essentials
     such as water, food, and medical services.

• Assembly areas. These are larger and more organized than collection points. They offer DCs
     emergency relief, including food, medical support, and temporary shelter. Designated personnel,
     including military members and CIVs from interagency, HN, NGO, and international governmental
     partners, begin screening and registering DCs. This process helps identify Family groups, determine
     points of origin and intended destinations, and gather other pertinent information. Additionally, they
     start to separate enemy prisoners of war, hostile CIVs, and deserters. Assembly areas are typically
     in division security zones and may accommodate DCs for a week or longer. Authorities may decide
     to transfer DCs from assembly areas to camps so they can continue toward their intended
     destinations or return home. If necessary, assembly areas may evolve into DC camps.

• DC camps. These facilities, which can be temporary or semipermanent, support the effective and
     efficient provision of shelter and aid to DCs over an extended period. DC camps are discussed in
     appendix B.

TASK FORCE DISLOCATED CIVILIAN CONTROL

     5-41. DC operations require significant manpower and a combined arms approach to achieve success. One
     effective technique for managing DCs in a tactical AO is to organize organic forces into a TF specifically
     designed for this mission, referred to as TF DC control. This TF has four imperatives:

          • Implement an integrated system of control.
          • Provide lifesaving and life-sustaining care, including access to water.
          • Process CIVs to determine their identity and status and to collect military and civil-military

                information.
          • Transition control operations in an orderly manner.

5-42. Generic TF DC consist of a combined arms team that integrates infantry with military police,
psychological operations teams, and CA specialists or personnel trained in CA. These teams manage CIV
populations using fundamental techniques such as blocking, clearing, and collecting. Table 5-2 illustrates the
essential equipment these various teams require to perform their tasks effectively.
