                 host nation

     5-43. The basic action element for clearing operations is the combined arms clearing team, which consists of
     one military police team equipped with their own vehicle and one military information support operations
     (MISO) team. TF DC control may adjust one or more combined arms teams based on the civil-military
     situation, specific tasks, terrain, and available assets. For example, this could involve using a military police
     squad rather than a military police team as the core component of all combined arms teams or augmenting
     the teams with combat engineers.

     5-44. DC control may be enhanced by deploying five special-purpose ad hoc teams, each tailored to specific
     mission requirements, forming a combined arms team. Each team is designed to successfully carry out
     missions requiring specialized training or equipment. The five special-purpose teams are as follows:

          • Negotiation team. The primary purpose of the negotiation team is to assist in high-stakes
                negotiations that have the potential to create or escalate unrest or may lead to highly negative public
                perceptions beyond the battlefield. The negotiations involve discussions with civil leaders but do
                not include hostage situations; hostage rescue and similar operations fall outside the scope of generic
                TF DC control. Instead, the objectives of the negotiation team are to manage the incident or issue
                in a way that prevents undue adverse influence on the populace, to resolve it peacefully whenever
                possible to protect CIV lives, and to ensure that the incident does not become a focal point for local
                or international news media.

          • Special reaction team (overwatch). The primary purpose of the special reaction team (overwatch)
                is to effectively and safely neutralize specific threats that may arise during blocking, clearing, and
                collecting operations. This team also supports the apprehension of troublemakers and ringleaders
                by assisting in their removal from a crowd. However, apprehending suspects in other contexts is
                beyond the scope of generic TF DC control.

          • Special reaction team (armored vehicles). The primary purpose of the special reaction team
                (armored vehicles) is to conduct show-of-force operations, particularly at roadblocks. They are
                responsible for protecting TF elements and CIVs in their care. Additionally, they assist the TF as
                needed, including executing apprehensions in crowd situations.

          • Medical care team. The primary purpose of the medical care team is to respond swiftly to CIV
                mass casualty incidents. They begin the triage process and coordinate further medical responses
                with the surgeon and medical operations center of the parent unit or the equivalent. They provide
                medical care beyond basic emergency first aid, as needed by the TF.

          • Civil-military team. The primary purpose of the civil-military team is to exploit the potential for
                military and civil-military information from CIVs encountered by the TF. This may involve
                biometric collection to assist with positive identification and vetting of DCs. Human intelligence
                collectors are trained explicitly in screening operations and are often fluent in the primary language
                of the AO, or they work with a translator. CA, human intelligence, and MISO forces create a strong
                collaborative effort within the TF and for higher headquarters.

DISLOCATED CIVILIAN COMMUNICATION

     5-45. Convincing people to follow a DC plan can be quite challenging. To assist in this process, HN public
     information programs and MISO assets can be invaluable. They can provide mass media broadcasts, use
     loudspeakers with prerecorded messages, create culturally appropriate signs, and distribute leaflets.

     5-46. The following messages, prerecorded in the dominant language of the AO, are useful for controlling
     CIVs in tactical situations:

          • Standard roadblock recording:
                 This is a roadblock.

108              ATP 3-39.30                                       25 March 2026


---

                                                                                       Support to Populace and Resources Control

                 For your safety, you will not be allowed to pass this point.
                 Return to your homes.
          • Standard clearing recording:
                 Stay off the road or leave this area.
                 If you do not comply, you will be detained or arrested.
                 Return to your homes.
          • Standard recording for a DC collection point:
                 This is a civilian collection point.
                 You will not be harmed.
                 Everyone will be searched. Vehicles will be searched and parked. Some belongings may be

                     taken from you temporarily for the safety of everyone.
                 Water and emergency medical care will be provided to you after you have been searched.
                 If we take any of your belongings, you will receive a receipt. If any of your belongings for

                     which you have a receipt are not returned to you, you will be compensated for them.

     5-47. The following words should be printed in both English and the primary language of the AO on 3 x 5
     cards. These cards can be used for point and talk by number. A well-prepared DC control site should also
     display the words in the same order on a large sign. The 10 words or phrases that every Soldier should be
     able to say in the dominant language of the AO are—

          • Go.
          • Stop.
          • Hands up.
          • Right.
          • Left.
          • Stand.
          • Sit.
          • Yes.
          • No.
          • Water.

             Note. “Put down your weapon” and other phrases are also important, but “hands up” is a simpler
             way to express surrender, control, and related concepts

TECHNIQUES FOR DISLOCATED CIVILIAN COLLECTION POINTS

     5-48. The quadrant method is a technique used to designate specific areas for managing noncombatants and
     other groups at hasty sites. In this method, each quadrant of a crossroads is assigned a specific purpose or
     group. For example, as seen in figure 5-3, West is designated as a CIV collection point; Northeast is
     designated for enemy prisoners of war or detainees; Southeast serves as a casualty collection point; and
     Southwest is designated as a multipurpose area for maintenance, supplies, and other purposes.

               Figure 5-3. Designation of hasty control sites by quadrant

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30                                                 109


---

Chapter 5

     5-49. Each control point is positioned 50 to 100 meters from the roads to ensure that the groups remain
     sufficiently separated. This distance enhances the safety and security of each group, minimizes workforce
     requirements, and reduces the potential for terrorism by keeping people at a reasonable distance from passing
     troops. Prior training and basic supplies, such as water cans or bottles and large quantities of chemical light
     sticks, facilitate the operation of a hastily set up CIV collection point day and night.

     5-50. When a hasty DC collection point is activated, designated personnel will convert the site into a
     deliberate DC collection point. Five key tasks must be completed at a deliberate DC collection point. These
     tasks are shown in figure 5-4 and are explained in detail in the following paragraphs.

     Legend:  civilian
     CIV      dislocated civilian
     DC

     Figure 5-4. Example layout of a dislocated civilian collection point

Local Security

     5-51. The collection point should be located so that DCs will not suffer any greater exposure to the effects of
     combat than would exist for them away from the collection point. Local security measures must be
     implemented to protect the occupants, personnel operating the collection point, and friendly troops nearby or
     passing through the area. Guards should be stationed at both the entrance and exit of the collection point and
     given special orders, as required.

Physical Security Within the Collection Point—Vehicle Search and Dislocated Civilian Search

     5-52. This task requires setting up special purpose areas within the collection point and following certain
     procedures—

          • Ensure that all private cars, public vehicles, livestock, and carts are parked outside or on the outskirts
                of the collection point, specifically in the vehicle search area, until they have been searched. All
                passengers must dismount.

          • Direct passengers to the DC search area.
          • Make the driver remain with the vehicle until it is searched. Designated personnel will conduct the

                search. If an undercarriage observation device is available, it should be used during the search. Once

110           ATP 3-39.30          25 March 2026


---

                                                                                       Support to Populace and Resources Control

                the search is complete, the driver and search personnel will jointly move the vehicle, livestock, or
                cart to the vehicle holding area.

     5-53. Searchers treat livestock as vehicles and pets as livestock if this does not create more problems than it
     avoids. A searcher then escorts the driver to the DC search area. Designated personnel—

          • Search DCs and their belongings for prohibited items.
          • Vary search methods. A quick pat down is used for some people. A more invasive search is done

                for others. If a handheld metal detector is available, it should be used to expedite the searches.
                Property taken under the control of the searcher should be tagged, and a copy should be given to the
                owner. A field property control card and an explanation card should be used as necessary.
          • Always use trained personnel to perform searches. If possible, female Soldiers are used to search
                females, infants, and little boys. If a female Soldier is not at the collection point but is close enough
                to get there in a reasonable time, these searches are deferred until she arrives. The unsearched people
                are held separately until then, so they do not pose a potential danger to others. If a female Soldier
                cannot be obtained, a trained male Soldier should do the search using the back of the hand technique
                if its use is not contrary to orders and if special security concerns require a search.
          • Always use a searcher (unarmed) and an overwatcher (armed). They must be trained in these skills
                and work together.

Dislocated Civilian Processing and Property Control

     5-54. The operation of a deliberate DC collection point may be postponed for some time, but it is not
     advisable to completely waive this process as a general practice. The processing of DCs consists of two
     stages. All individuals must go through stage one. Stage two can be delayed, reserved for specific individuals,
     or may not occur at all.

Stage One Processing

     5-55. This is the quick screen to identify enemy prisoners of war, CIV internees, and detainees who need to
     be immediately segregated from the rest of the group. This initial processing can be conducted without the
     assistance of a translator. Searchers should remain vigilant for irregulars and infiltrators attempting to
     disguise themselves as CIVs. Once identified, all enemy prisoners of war, CIV internees, and detainees
     should be placed in the short-term detainee holding area. Generally, anyone causing disruptions at the
     collection point is detained. While it is ideal to further separate CIV internees and detainees from enemy
     prisoners of war, there is often insufficient time or resources to do so.

     5-56. Consistent with orders, searchers are responsible for securing all items that could be dangerous to the
     team, friendly forces passing through the collection point, or the DCs. Additionally, searchers will confiscate
     and label all items that noncombatants are prohibited from possessing under U.S. or HN policy.

     5-57. If available, a DA Form 4137 (Evidence/Property Custody Document) is attached to the vehicle or
     animal. The DA Form 4137 must contain, at a minimum, the following information: the DC collection point
     number, the date, the quantity of seized items, a description of the seized items, and a signature block for the
     officer in charge or the noncommissioned officer in charge at the collection point. A copy of this card is given
     to the driver.

Stage Two Processing

     5-58. This stage is intended to help categorize DCs more effectively. It aims to determine details such as U.S.
     citizenship, reunite families at the collection point, identify influential individuals, and gather information
     that may have intelligence value from equipment, weapons, documents, and conversations. This processing
     occurs when time and resources allow and is not considered a high priority. A translator is usually required.

Controlled Property

     5-59. Units have several options for the disposition of controlled property. Depending on the property
     category, units may choose to retain control, return the property to the individuals from whom it was taken,
     hand it over to other forces or agencies (such as local law enforcement or follow-on forces taking control of

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  111


---

Chapter 5

     the DC collection point), or a combination of all three. Unit commanders must act according to their moral
     and legal obligations, weighing the advantages and disadvantages of each option.

Retaining Control

     5-60. If units are taking possession of property (they must do so if no one signs for it and returning the item
     is not an option), they should provide the owner an official receipt (such as DA Form 3161, [Request for
     Issue or Turn-In]). The units should explain the intention for the property, and the rights and procedures of
     the owner for seeking compensation should be outlined. This reiteration of rights is meant to reassure the
     owners and may be necessary to ensure a smooth handoff.

Transferring

     5-61. To transfer control of the property, units must complete a property control register that lists all items
     being transferred. An official from the follow-on forces must sign for these items and a copy of the register
     using DA Form 3161.

Limited Services (Medical, Food, Water, Shelter, and Sanitation)

     5-62. Services at a DC collection point can vary based on need and available resources, ranging from
     immediate care for life-threatening conditions to ancillary services like food distribution. However, to
     comply with international law, providing at least water and immediate medical care is essential. Medical
     services should not be offered to DCs until they have completed the quick-screen stage of processing, except
     in cases of emergency care necessary to prevent imminent death. Soldiers should—

          • Treat life-threatening emergencies, including providing first aid for traumatic injuries and
                administering oral rehydration therapy for dehydrated infants.

          • Provide water as a preventive measure if an adequate supply is available.
          • Allow occupants to relieve themselves by providing one designated area for males and another for

                females, along with basic equipment (such as shovels and latrine screening materials) to facilitate
                the creation of rudimentary sanitation facilities (slit trenches). Occupants must be supervised.
          • Distribute food only to occupants who have been at the collection point for 24 hours or more.
                Distributing food too generously can create a pull factor. Soldiers should also be mindful that certain
                ready-to-eat meals may not be appropriate due to religious or cultural restrictions or may be too rich
                for malnourished individuals, potentially causing immediate health issues. (Yellow-packaged
                international humanitarian rations are considered safe.)
          • Provide additional services that align with the commander’s legal, moral, and mission-specific
                obligations and requirements.

Resolution or Disposition of Dislocated Civilians

     5-63. When a DC collection point becomes operational, there are four potential outcomes for the collection
     point operators:

          • Retain control of the collection point. The moral obligations to the DCs at the collection point
                increase as time passes.

          • Close down the collection point. This can be done by releasing the DCs if the tactical situation and
                other factors justify this action.

          • Arrange for movement of the DCs. This involves transferring the DCs to another holding area,
                such as a CIV assembly area.

          • Hand off collection point operations. This would involve transferring responsibility for the
                collection point to other operators, such as a support unit or the HN. This is the most likely outcome
                for combat units that are on the move.

Dislocated Civilian Collection Point Handoff

     5-64. As a unit moves out of an area, it must be ready to hand off (transition) an active DC collection point
     to follow-on forces. These forces may or may not have training in managing DCs. Regardless, the outgoing

112  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                            Support to Populace and Resources Control

     unit should ensure that it provides a comprehensive briefing to the follow-on forces about the operation of
     the collection point.

             Note. Using biometric collection devices allows for collecting, sharing, matching, and storing
             identity information, such as fingerprints, iris scans, facial recognition, voice recordings, and
             contextual data. This information can be cross-referenced with DOD biometric holdings (DOD
             ABIS) and the Defense Watch List.

NONCOMBATANT EVACUATION OPERATIONS

     5-65. NEO refers to the organized and authorized departure of noncombatants from a specific area by the
     DOS, the DOD, or other appropriate authorities. While the United States typically associates NEOs with
     combat operations, these operations may also be conducted in anticipation of or response to natural disasters
     or human-made crises in foreign countries. Additionally, civil unrest in a country may prompt an evacuation
     to the United States or other safe haven.

             Note. Pursuant to Executive Order 12656, the DOS is responsible for protecting and evacuating
             U.S. citizens and nationals abroad and for safeguarding their overseas property. The DOS is the
             lead agency for planning and conducting NEOs. Executive Order 12656 also directs the SecDef
             to advise and assist the Secretary of State in preparing and implementing these plans.

     5-66. DOD defines two categories of noncombatant evacuees:
          • U.S. citizens that competent authority can order to evacuate. This includes—
                 CIV employees of all USG agencies and their dependents.
                 Military personnel of the U.S. Armed Forces who are specifically designated for evacuation as
                     noncombatants.
                 Dependents of members of the U.S. Armed Forces.
          • U.S. and non-U.S. citizens that competent authority may authorize or assist (but not necessarily
                order) to evacuate. This includes—
                 CIV employees of USG agencies and their dependents who are residents in the country and are
                     willing to evacuate.
                 Private U.S. citizens and their dependents.
                 Military personnel and their dependents who are not under an ordered evacuation.
                 Designated aliens, including dependents of CIV employees of the USG and military personnel,
                     as prescribed by the DOS.

             Note. JP 3-68 provides additional information on NEO.

MILITARY POLICE SUPPORT TO NONCOMBATANT EVACUATION
OPERATIONS

     5-67. Military police assist with NEOs at the request of the DOS. They coordinate with HN police and CA
     units to carry out these evacuations. Military police can provide security at departure locations, extraction
     sites, and for convoys transporting evacuees. Their experience in law enforcement has equipped them with
     skills in interpersonal communication and the graduated use of force, making them well-suited for direct
     interactions with evacuees, often in collaboration with a translator. These skills are essential for interacting
     with evacuees and handling potentially hostile situations. Military police are key members of marshaling
     teams, responsible for locating evacuees and guiding them to assembly areas and evacuation sites.

     5-68. When a decision is made to evacuate a community, planners must create detailed plans to ensure that
     uncontrolled groups do not interfere with the movement of military units, supplies, and first responders. Mass
     evacuation planning includes the following:

          • Transportation. Planners should maximize the use of CIV transportation.
          • Movement control. Planners must identify both natural and man-made obstacles that could hinder

                movement along designated routes and take steps to mitigate their effects. Checkpoints and signage

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  113


---

Chapter 5

                should be established to guide traffic flow, and measures should be in place to redirect vehicles to
                alternate routes as needed, depending on changes in the situation. Collection points should be set up
                for vehicles that break down.
          • Security. Military and HN security forces are responsible for ensuring the safety of CIVs following
                evacuation. These organizations also safeguard CIV property left behind, including farm animals,
                pets, and other personal belongings.
          • Documentation. In certain situations, evacuees may need identification documents to ensure proper
                registration and facilitate orderly movement.
          • Briefing. Before movement, the movement control officer conducts a briefing for evacuees. This
                can be done using leaflets, loudspeakers, posters, or other available means. The briefing covers the
                details of the move, restrictions on personal belongings, the organization of movement, and
                schedules.
          • Rations. For movements lasting no more than two days, planning should ensure that evacuees
                receive rations at departure or at designated points along the route.
          • Health care. The public health team will make the best use of CIV medical personnel, equipment,
                and supplies to care for the health and well-being of the evacuees. Military medical personnel and
                resources may be used to support HN and CIV organizations if necessary and authorized. Before
                movement, medical personnel should take appropriate measures to prevent the spread of infectious
                diseases.
          • Return. During planning, it is important to consider the eventual return of the evacuees and to
                establish criteria for determining the duration of their absence.

RESOURCES CONTROL

     5-69. Resources control involves regulating the movement and consumption of material resources,
     mobilizing these resources, and denying them to the enemy. It specifically targets sectors of the wealth and
     economy of a nation, including natural resources, food and agriculture, immovable property, finances, and
     critical infrastructure. (For more detailed information, see ATP 3-57.20.) Military police support to resources
     control may include—

          • Securing existing harvest storage facilities to prevent spoilage and protect harvested crops from
                looting.

          • Implementing rationing and distribution programs for essential commodities, such as food and fuel.
          • Establishing border security, including customs procedures, to prevent arms smuggling, human

                trafficking, and the transport of contraband like drugs and currency.
          • Regulating and securing access to valuable natural resources.
          • Stopping illicit trade in natural resources and developing governance mechanisms and incentives to

                bring this trade into the legal market.
          • Initiating processes to address and resolve resource ownership and access issues.

          • Locking international access to overseas financial accounts to prevent money laundering.
          • Protecting and securing strategically important institutions, including government buildings,

                archives, museums, religious sites, courthouses, and communications facilities.

     5-70. Military police may be tasked to secure critical convoys to ensure safe transit. Their functions in support
     of resource control operations include the following activities:

          • Protecting against enemy activities along MSRs.
          • Securing supply routes and critical convoys.
          • Conducting reconnaissance and surveillance.
          • Evaluating and recommending protective measures for high-risk facilities.
          • Implementing protective measures for HRP.
          • Applying protective measures for designated supplies.
          • Conducting area and local security operations.
          • Performing response force operations.
          • Applying AT measures.
          • Implementing physical security measures.

114  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                            Support to Populace and Resources Control

PUBLIC AND PRIVATE PROPERTY CONTROL

     5-71. Resources control, which includes managing both public and private movable and immovable property,
     is most common during the occupation of foreign territory by U.S. forces. However, control measures can
     also be implemented across various military operations based on specific authorities granted to a commander
     for a particular mission. Effective resources management requires the combined efforts of all instruments of
     national power. The Hague Conventions of 1899 and 1907 and the 1949 Geneva Convention (IV) outline
     rules regarding property in occupied territories. A territory is considered occupied when it is taken over by a
     sovereign power following military intervention. Typically, the period of occupation is temporary and lasts
     until a peace treaty is signed or a new government is established. The occupation is limited to the areas where
     such authority has been established and can be exercised. (See ATP 3-57.20 for additional information.)
     International law recognizes five basic powers that a military commander of an occupying force holds
     concerning property in enemy territory:

          • Destruction.
          • Confiscation.
          • Seizure.
          • Requisition.
          • Control.

             Note. The authority of an occupational force commander regarding public and private property
             depends on the specific situation. The treatment of real and personal property by the occupier is
             assessed based on several factors, including the type of property, the needs of the occupier, and
             whether it is state-owned or privately owned. It is strongly recommended that the servicing staff
             judge advocate conduct a legal review before exercising authority granted to an occupational
             force. See FM 6-27 and the Operational Law Handbook for more detailed information.

     5-72. Resources control measures can be implemented in collaboration with an HN government during
     military engagements, limited interventions, peace operations, irregular warfare, and major operations. The
     authority to execute these measures stems from a formal agreement negotiated by the DOS, including a status-
     of-forces agreement, a mutual defense treaty, or a security cooperation treaty ratified by the United States
     and the HN government.

     5-73. The long-term success of any intervention largely depends on the ability of the military to protect and
     maintain critical infrastructure until the HN can resume that responsibility. Measures that control critical
     infrastructure directly support the primary stability task of establishing civil security. The initial aim of the
     response is to create a safe and secure environment that allows the HN to sustain and further develop its
     infrastructure capabilities.

     5-74. Military police implement countermeasures to enhance security. These measures may include
     conducting vulnerability and threat assessments, developing procedures to predict and prevent terrorist
     actions before they occur, hardening likely targets, and carrying out offensive operations to destroy enemies.
     Military police units actively establish checkpoints and roadblocks to control the movement of vehicles,
     personnel, and materials, thereby preventing illegal actions that could assist the enemy. These control
     measures serve as a deterrent against terrorist activities, sabotage, and other threats.

CIVIL SECURITY SUPPORT

     5-75. Key to the control of national resources is the primary stability task—establish civil security. A crucial
     aspect of establishing civil security is effective border control and safeguarding the borders of the nation and
     its points of entry and embarkation. For more information, see FM 3-07.

     5-76. Border controls regulate immigration, manage the movements of local residents, collect excise taxes
     or duties, and limit smuggling. When it comes to safeguarding national resources, emphasis must be placed
     on the illegal export of assets, which can occur through smuggling or the corruption of border officials.

     5-77. Planning resources control measures must consider illegal activities and their impact on resource
     management within the operational environment. Examples of such criminal activities include black market
     operations, smuggling, theft, and the corruption of HN officials. These actions can negatively affect the

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  115

Chapter 5

     availability of critical resources. Criminals involved in the trafficking of contraband items, such as arms and
     ammunition, pose significant security threats to the population, the HN government, and deployed military
     forces.

     5-78. An essential element in controlling criminal activity in the area of resources is border security and
     control. Border security activities involve managing land borders, airspace, coastal and territorial waters, and
     exclusive economic zones. Effective management of border areas and crossings deters smuggling, prevents
     the movement of irregular forces into HN territory, and controls the flow of DCs. Border security forces are
     responsible for monitoring, detecting, and preventing crimes in border regions, including illegal entry and
     the trafficking of goods, services, and human capital.

BORDER OPERATIONS SUPPORT

     5-79. Military police may occasionally be assigned to support border operations along the U.S. borders as
     part of DSCA. This support can include using technical equipment such as thermal imaging, night vision,
     and infrared detection to aid in detecting individuals illegally entering the United States. The Posse Comitatus
     Act may prohibit Regular Army and Army Reserve Soldiers from enforcing civil law, allowing them to
     respond only to defend themselves or law enforcement officials from imminent danger. However,
     nonfederalized National Guard military police, operating under the control of their respective state governors,
     are not restricted by the Posse Comitatus Act. Once mobilized (federalized), National Guard Soldiers are
     subject to the limitations of the Posse Comitatus Act unless other valid legislation is enacted to remove these
     restrictions.

     5-80. Borders outside of the United States may require support from military police units. Military police
     may be needed to operate control posts, conduct border patrols, and supervise crossing points at international
     borders. Many countries regulate the movement of military personnel and CIVs at their borders for various
     reasons, including security; customs and tariff enforcement; protection of the CIV economy; and the
     apprehension of criminals, absentees, and individuals of intelligence interest. Control is maintained through
     establishing authorized road or rail crossing points, border patrols, control posts, and collaboration with
     authorities from neighboring countries when feasible. Prohibited or restricted zones are often created to
     further manage movement at the borders.

     5-81. In conducting border control, military police typically coordinate with indigenous police forces and
     HN counterintelligence and CA units. They monitor individuals and items involved in criminal activities or
     customs violations. Additionally, they establish the identity and purpose of U.S. forces crossing borders and
     inspecting vehicles and travel documents. Supporting border control is crucial to prevent adversaries from
     moving weapons, supplies, and personnel across borders, which could be used to attack or disrupt friendly
     forces.

     5-82. Military police may also be responsible for enforcing customs laws and regulations to support local
     government or HN officials. The enforcement of customs laws by the U.S. military in countries where U.S.
     forces are stationed often occurs as part of agreements, such as the North Atlantic Treaty Organization status-
     of-forces agreement between the United States and the respective HN.

     5-83. In controlling national resources, emphasis must be placed on the illegal export of assets through
     smuggling and the corruption of border officials. Ineffective border control and management systems can
     hinder efforts to detect and prevent organized criminal activity. This often leads to increased trafficking of
     illegal arms, goods, and human trafficking, and it negatively affects the ability of the government to generate
     revenue from duties and import and export fees. While conventional military forces can provide immediate
     border security, they are not suitable for law enforcement unless they are acting as an occupying force.

                                                Appendix A

                                 Assessment

    Assessment is an essential, continuous activity that occurs throughout the operations process.
    It involves comparing expected outcomes with actual events to determine the effectiveness of
    security and mobility support tasks. Assessment helps commanders gauge progress toward
    achieving the desired end state, achieving objectives, and determining the effectiveness of
    security and mobility support within the context of larger Army and joint operations. While
    failures in executing security and mobility support tasks are usually straightforward to identify,
    measuring and quantifying successful applications can be challenging.

CONTINUOUS ASSESSMENT

     A-1. Assessment is a continuous process that precedes and concludes the operations process and is integral
     to modifying and adjusting the focus and direction of security and mobility support. Assessment is the
     determination of the progress toward accomplishing a task, creating a condition, or achieving an objective
     (JP 3-0). Figure A-1 shows the activities of assessment, which include—

          • Monitoring the current situation to collect relevant information.
          • Evaluating progress toward attaining end state conditions, achieving objectives, and performing

                tasks.
          • Recommending or directing action for improvement.

                                          Figure A-1. Activities of assessment

     A-2. Commanders typically base assessments on their situational understanding, which is usually a
     combination of various information sources and their intuition. These assessments help them gauge progress
     toward attaining the desired end state, achieving objectives, and performing tasks. The process involves
     monitoring and evaluating the operational environment to identify changes impacting operations. However,
     operations may not proceed as visualized during planning.

MONITORING

     A-3. Monitoring is continuous observation of those conditions relevant to the current operation (ADP 5-0).
     Monitoring enables military police commanders and staffs to gather information about the current situation,
     including threats (such as crime), freedom of movement, DCs, and overall security. This information is
     essential for assessing whether current strategies and approaches effectively accomplish the commander’s
     intent and operational concept or if adjustments are necessary. Effective decision making relies on a clear
     and accurate understanding of the current situation, as progress cannot be evaluated without it.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  117


---

Appendix A

     A-4. During planning, commanders assess the situation to establish the facts and assumptions that form the
     basis of the plan. Throughout the preparation and execution stages, commanders and staffs continue to
     monitor the situation to ensure that the established facts are still applicable, that their assumptions are still
     valid, and that new conditions affecting the operation are identified.

     A-5. CCIR and decision points guide the monitoring activities of the staff and prioritize the information
     collection efforts of the military police. Information requirements related to the enemy, terrain, weather, and
     civil considerations are identified and prioritized through reconnaissance and surveillance. These activities
     are part of the overall information collection process. Military police conduct reconnaissance and surveillance
     using their policing capabilities and the application of PIO, as outlined in ATP 3-39.20. This enhances their
     effectiveness in performing these tasks. Conducting PIO—including collecting, assessing, developing, and
     disseminating police intelligence products—is a primary responsibility of commanders and the PM.

     A-6. Staffs monitor and collect information from the common operational picture and friendly reports. This
     information includes operational and intelligence summaries from subordinate, higher, and adjacent
     headquarters, and communications and reports from liaison teams. Staffs also identify information sources
     outside military channels, such as local law enforcement, and monitor their reports. These additional sources
     may include products from CIV agencies, HN entities, and other government agencies. Staffs apply
     information management and knowledge management practices to ensure that this information reaches the
     right people at the right time.

     A-7. Staff sections are responsible for documenting relevant information in running estimates. Each staff
     element begins to develop their respective running estimates during mission analysis. The commander and
     each staff section must continually update and maintain these estimates throughout the entire operations
     process. Staff sections conduct ongoing assessments of current operations to determine if they are aligned
     with the commander’s intent, mission, and concept of operations. In their running estimates, staff sections
     utilize new information and updated facts and assumptions as the basis for evaluation (see figure A-2).

     Figure A-2. Sample security and mobility support running estimate

A-8. During planning, commanders assess the situation to establish the facts and assumptions that will shape
the plan. Throughout the preparation and execution phases, commanders and staffs continue to monitor the
situation to verify if the facts remain relevant, if their assumptions are still valid, and if new conditions have
arisen that could affect the operations.

EVALUATION

A-9. Staffs analyze relevant information collected through monitoring to evaluate the progress of the
operation. Evaluating is using indicators to judge progress toward desired conditions and determining why
the current degree of progress exists (ADP 5-0). Evaluation is central to the assessment process, where
analysis occurs. It helps commanders identify what is effective and what is not, providing insights into
improving mission accomplishment.

A-10. Criteria in the form of measures of effectiveness (MOE) and measures of performance (MOP) help
determine the progress toward achieving end-state conditions, meeting objectives, and performing tasks. An
MOE helps to determine whether a task is producing its intended results, while an MOP assesses whether a
task has been completed correctly. It is important to note that MOEs and MOPs are merely criteria; they do
not constitute the actual assessment. To evaluate these measures, relevant information must be gathered in
the form of indicators. MOEs and MOPs are developed during the planning phase, refined during preparation,
and monitored throughout execution.

A-11. The assessment plan is enabled by monitoring and evaluating MOE and MOP criteria that stem from
security and mobility support tasks. These criteria serve as clear, relevant, and responsive benchmarks that
are applicable across all operations. They may include CCIR, essential elements of friendly information, and
can generate specific information requirements. MOEs and MOPs are valuable decision-support tools that
can influence transition periods, resource allocation, and other critical decisions (see table A-1, page 120).
For more information on MOPs and MOEs, see FM 6-0.

     • MOE. A measure of effectiveness is an indicator used to measure a current system state, with change
          indicated by comparing multiple observations over time (JP 5-0). An MOE evaluates changes in
          both positive and negative conditions, is focused on mission accomplishment, and considers the
          results or consequences of an action. It is used to assess changes in the operational environment (for
          example, a 33 percent reduction in attacks on MSR TINA over the past seven days). MOEs are often
          subjective as they tend to measure long-term outcomes. They are typically documented and
          monitored in formal assessment plans. Examples of MOEs for the objective of providing a safe and
          secure environment may include—
           An increase in the freedom of movement and maneuver for friendly forces.
           A decrease in attacks on base camps.
           A decline in threat activities along transportation routes.
           An increase in knowledge regarding the activities and resources of enemy or adversary forces.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30                                                      119


---

Appendix A

     • MOP. A measure of performance is an indicator used measure friendly action that is tied to
          measuring task accomplishment (JP 5-0). MOPs help answer questions such as, "Was the action
          taken?" or "Were the tasks completed to standard?" or "Are we doing things right?" An MOP
          confirms or denies whether a task has been properly performed. MOPs are typically tracked at all
          levels within execution matrices and are also commonly used to evaluate training effectiveness.
          Using MOPs to assess task accomplishment is relatively straightforward, often resulting in a simple
          yes or no answer. Examples of MOPs include─
           Route X cleared.
           Generators delivered, operational, and secured at locations X, Y, and Z.
           $15,000 spent for schoolhouse completion.
           Aerial dissemination of 60,000 leaflets over village D.

     • Indicator. In the context of assessment, an indicator is an item of information that provides insight
          into an MOE or MOP. Indicators can come from reports submitted by subordinates, surveys, polls,
          and information requirements. They help answer the question, "What is the current status of this
          MOE or MOP?" A single indicator can influence multiple MOEs and MOPs. Examples of indicators
          for an MOE are the decrease in threat activity by the—
           Number of attacks along MSR TINA each week.
           Number of times that DCs restricted movement on MSRs each day.
           Number of reports of criminal activity by the population per area each week.
           Number of explosive hazards found along routes each week.
           Number of attacks on base camps each week.

     A-12. Significant changes in some operational environment conditions can be subtle and may occur over a
     long period. However, many security and mobility support tasks are continuous, including reconnaissance,
     surveillance, area security, physical security, AT, and support to PRC. The constant nature of these tasks can
     lead to complacency or inattentiveness. As a result, leaders need to remain focused on identifying,
     monitoring, and evaluating accurate indicators and warnings. This vigilance is necessary to maintain
     situational awareness and to alert them to potential hazards and associated risks.

     A-13. Commanders monitor MOEs and evaluate variances and change indicators to understand their causes
     and effects. This helps forecast potential failures or identify critical points of failure in activities or operations.
     Based on this assessment, resources can be reallocated to reduce the overall risk to the mission or to support
     specific security efforts. The goal is to anticipate the need for action before a failure occurs rather than merely
     responding to an unplanned loss. Thorough staff planning during the military decision-making process

120                                    ATP 3-39.30              25 March 2026


---

                                          Assessment

enables commanders to accelerate decision making by preplanning responses to anticipated events through
battle drills, branches, and sequels. War-gaming critical events allow commanders to focus on their critical
information requirements and effectively coordinate supporting information collection efforts. Information
gathered during this process can be used to develop essential elements of friendly information and indicators
or warnings that inform the development of protection priorities.

A-14.   If an action seems to be failing to achieve its desired effect, the reasons may include—
     •   Personnel or equipment system failures.
     •   Insufficient resource allocation at critical points.
     •   Variations in the expected threat-combat power ratio, which may increase the overall risk.
     •   Ineffective supporting efforts, leading to a cumulative failure of more critical elements.

A-15. Assessment identifies the magnitude and significance of variances in performance or conditions
compared to expectations based on prior forecasts. This evaluation helps determine whether an adjustment is
necessary. Commanders oversee the ongoing operation to ensure that it is progressing satisfactorily according
to the current plan, including fragmentary orders that have modified it. The staff assesses the situation against
established protection criteria. This assessment ensures that both facts and assumptions are still accurate and
identifies new facts or assumptions that may arise. Assessment reduces reaction time by anticipating and
aligning future requirements with current plans.

A-16. Based on the evaluation of progress and effectiveness, the staff brainstorms possible improvements.
They assess the merit of these changes and identify those that are most promising. These recommendations
are presented to the commander or implemented within their delegated authority. Commanders review the
recommendations from the staff, subordinate commanders, and other mission partners, combining them with
their own assessments. Based on this gathered information, they decide whether and how to adjust the
operation to better accomplish the mission.

A-17. Throughout Army operations, recommendations to the commander may involve several options,
including continuing the operation as planned, executing a branch plan, or making unanticipated adjustments.
Adjustments can entail assigning new tasks to subordinates, reprioritizing support efforts, modifying
information collection assets, or significantly changing the COA. During security and mobility support,
recommendations might indicate that additional TCPs and checkpoints are needed to manage DCs on MSRs
(output) to ensure freedom of movement (outcome). There may be a need for additional reconnaissance and
surveillance efforts (output) to identify and destroy threats (outcome) before they can attack friendly forces
or disrupt operations in support areas.

ASSESSMENT DURING EXECUTION

A-18. Execution is the act of putting a plan into action by applying combat power to accomplish the mission
and adjusting operations based on changes in the situation (ADP 5-0). During execution, commanders
(supported by their staffs) focus on turning decisions into actions. This process involves determining whether
to execute planned activities, such as transitioning to different phases or executing a branch plan. Execution
also includes adapting the plan in response to changes in the situation and continuously assessing the progress
of the current operation.

A-19. Assessment during execution is essential and involves a deliberate comparison of forecasted
outcomes with actual events, using specific criteria to evaluate operational progress toward success.
Throughout execution, commanders (supported by their staffs) continuously assess the progress of the
operation through monitoring and evaluation. They make decisions and direct combat power to seize, retain,
and exploit the initiative. It is essential for commanders and their staffs to consistently evaluate the expected
outcomes of the operation. This allows them to determine if changes are needed, take advantage of
opportunities, or respond to unexpected threats to accomplish the mission.

ASSESSMENT TOOLS

     A-20. Throughout the operations process, commanders work closely with their staffs, subordinate
     commanders, and other unified action partners to integrate their assessments. The main tools used to evaluate
     the progress of the operation include the OPORD, the common operational picture, personal observations,
     running estimates, and the assessment plan. See FM 6-0 for more information.

25 March 2026                ATP 3-39.30                                                             121


---

Appendix A

     A-21. During planning, staffs can develop an assessment plan by following the six steps outlined below.
     This assessment plan ensures that the continuous assessment process (monitoring, evaluating, and
     recommending or directing ongoing assessment) is applied throughout the preparation and execution of
     operations.

          • Step 1. Gather tools and assessment data.
          • Step 2. Understand the current and desired conditions.
          • Step 3. Develop an assessment framework.
          • Step 4. Develop the collection plan.
          • Step 5. Assign responsibilities for conducting analyses and generating recommendations.
          • Step 6. Identify feedback mechanisms.

     A-22. Staff members develop running estimates that illustrate significant aspects of security and mobility
     support discipline over time. Commanders use these estimates to maintain situational awareness and make
     necessary adjustments. Significant changes or discrepancies in the running estimates can indicate either a
     threat or an opportunity, alerting commanders to take action. The importance of these estimates increases as
     time becomes more limited. Decision making in time-sensitive situations typically occurs once a unit has
     entered the AO and is actively conducting operations. Therefore, the IPOE, an updated common operating
     picture, and portions of the running estimates should already exist.

     A-23. Rehearsals are a tool for commanders to ensure that their staffs and subordinates comprehend the
     commander’s intent and the concept of the operations. Conducting a rehearsal helps commanders and staffs
     identify weaknesses in the plan that may not have been recognized earlier. Rehearsals enhance external and
     internal coordination as staff members pinpoint further coordinating requirements. A well-planned and
     executed rehearsal allows leaders to incorporate lessons learned during the rehearsal assessment into existing
     plans and orders or future rehearsals, ultimately improving mission success. For more information on
     rehearsals, see FM 6-0.

LESSONS LEARNED INTEGRATION

     A-24. How organizations and Soldiers learn from their mistakes is key for mission success. While the
     evaluation process occurs throughout the operations process, it also occurs during the after-action review and
     assessment following the mission. Leaders at all levels must ensure that Soldiers and equipment are combat-
     ready. They are responsible for practicing sound stewardship and adhering to risk management principles to
     minimize the loss of resources and military assets due to hostile, nonhostile, and environmental threats and
     hazards.

     A-25. Key lessons learned are promptly applied and shared with other commands. Commanders establish
     systems to rapidly disseminate approved lessons learned, along with proven TTP that protect forces and
     achieve mission success. Staff at each command level evaluates the integration of these lessons and
     coordinates with other staff elements within and across different levels of command. Postoperational
     evaluations typically—

          • Identify threats not identified during the initial assessment or reveal new threats that developed
                during the operation or activity. For example, reevaluations are necessary when changes occur in
                personnel, equipment, the environment, or the mission.

          • Assess the effectiveness of operational goals and objectives. This includes determining whether the
                controls positively or negatively impacted mission accomplishment and whether they aligned with
                existing doctrine and TTP.

          • Evaluate the implementation, execution, and communication of controls.
          • Assess the accuracy of residual risk and the effectiveness of controls in eliminating hazards and

                controlling risks.
          • Ensure coordination throughout the integration processes.

                 Was the process integrated throughout all phases of the operation?
                 Were the risk controls effective?
                 Were risk decisions made at the appropriate level?
                 Was the process cyclic and continuous throughout the operation?
                 For units with a staff, assessments become more formal at higher echelons. The resources

                     available for assessments (such as the expertise of staff officers and the time they can dedicate)

                                                                                                                            Assessment

               increase proportionally from battalion to brigade, division, corps, and theater army. Small units
               that lack a staff typically conduct assessments informally. Leaders of these small units focus on
               evaluating the readiness of their unit in terms of personnel, equipment, supplies, morale, and
               their ability to complete assigned tasks. As these units assess their performance and learn from
               their experiences, they adapt their TTPs accordingly. Therefore, even the lowest echelons of
               the Army adhere to the assessment process.
