                                                Appendix B

                       Dislocated Civilian Camps

    The establishment of DC camps may be required during various military operations. HNs have
    the primary responsibility for constructing and operating these camps, and providing basic
    assistance and services to the affected population. They receive support from the United
    Nations, international organizations, and NGOs. However, U.S. military forces may also be
    assigned to perform this mission. The purpose of this appendix is to supplement the doctrine
    contained in the manual by emphasizing the planning, design, and operational considerations
    specific to DC camps. Creating efficient and effective camps is essential for conserving
    resources, minimizing liabilities, and reducing the overall logistical burden during extended
    operations.

PLANNING CONSIDERATIONS

     B-1. The CCDR identifies the facilities that meet operational requirements. Planning involves determining
     when, where, and why a DC camp is needed. It outlines the purpose of the camp; functional requirements;
     and connections with other camps, operational forces, and agencies. Additionally, planning generates the
     information needed to implement all life cycle phases of the camp. The planning process for DC camps takes
     place at all levels of warfare and command echelons. Planning activities cover a continuum that ranges from
     conceptual to fully detailed. Effective planning for DC camps relies on expertise from various fields, much
     of which may only be accessible through coordination with higher headquarters and supporting organizations,
     such as the United States Army Corps of Engineers.

     B-2. The planning and construction of DC camps will likely occur under significant time constraints to meet
     aggressive construction schedules. This urgency arises from the unpredictable nature of situations where DCs
     are present. While preplanning is essential for avoiding crises, planners should anticipate having little
     preplanning time. DC operations are typically long-term and require resources often not immediately
     available from DOD sources.

     B-3. Unlike base camps that are used solely for operational forces, many activities within DC camps involve
     participation from HN entities, U.S. and international government organizations, and NGOs. These include
     the United Nations, the International Committee of the Red Cross, and the International Organization for
     Migration. International humanitarian organizations, such as the International Committee of the Red Cross,
     carefully maintain their neutrality and prefer not to be associated with or dependent on the military, as this
     could jeopardize their special status within the international community. This status is vital for them to fulfill
     their mission effectively. It is crucial for camp planners to understand the interests and requirements of these
     stakeholders early in the planning process and to earn their trust and support from the outset. For more
     information on interorganizational coordination, see JP 3-08. See JP 3-28 for situations where military police
     respond to natural disasters within the United States and its territories as part of DSCA.

     B-4. Effective DC camps begin with accurately identifying the requirements for each life cycle phase of the
     camp. This includes generating supporting estimates and schedules for each phase of the operation. Estimates
     encompass the necessary resources, such as skilled personnel and organizations with the required capabilities,
     materials, and funding needed to meet the identified requirements. Commanders and their staffs use the
     planning process outlined in FM 6-0 to assess their needs for DC camps and integrate these plans into the
     overall concept of operations and base camp development planning process. The planning process provides
     a framework for coordinating the actions of the commander, staff, subordinate commanders, and others. A
     DC camp is simply a base camp developed to serve a special purpose. A key ingredient for all DC camp
     planning and design activities is the incorporation of the base camp principles described in ATP 3‑37.10.

     B-5. In rare cases, Army forces may be tasked with establishing DC camps. In these instances, the force
     must take into account legal considerations regarding the availability and ownership of land for camps.
     Additionally, logistical aspects must be addressed, including the provision of shelter, food, sanitation, and
     medical care. Security measures and crime prevention within the camps are also important, alongside

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  125


---

Appendix B

     contracting needs for construction. The primary factor in planning operations for DCs is the transition plan
     for caring for the population and transferring responsibility to a controlling agency. These agencies may
     include the United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees, the International Organization for Migration,
     the Office for the Coordination of Humanitarian Affairs, the International Committee of the Red Cross, or
     the HN. Controlling agencies are responsible for meeting the basic needs (food, water, shelter, sanitation,
     and security) of DCs. They must also be prepared to prevent the outbreak of communicable diseases among
     DCs. This consideration is crucial not only for the health of the CIVs but also for the well-being of the
     supporting military forces.

GENERAL PLANNING CONSIDERATIONS

     B-6. The primary objective of DC operations is to protect CIVs from the impacts of violence or disaster
     while minimizing their interference with military operations. DC camps serve as designated safe areas where
     DCs can be consolidated, allowing for the effective and efficient delivery of aid.

     B-7. The planning focus for DC camps, including their duration and composition, can vary at different
     command levels. This variation depends on the characteristics and location of the DC population and other
     relevant aspects of the operational environment. Establishing DC camps and implementing resettlement
     efforts require a combined arms approach since no single branch has the expertise to build, sustain, and
     protect these camps. Military police organizations provide command and control for these efforts. All
     commands and national and international agencies involved have clearly defined responsibilities. Planners
     consider the following when planning and projecting the establishment, operation, and management of DC
     camps:

          • Coordinate with CA units to collaborate with the United States, HN, and other international
                organizations to assess the appropriate levels and types of aid needed. Engaging with these
                organizations leverages their expertise and reduces the burden on U.S. Armed Forces.

          • Limit outside contributions to basic necessity items only until the DCs achieve self-sufficiency while
                encouraging them to become as independent as possible.

          • Review the effectiveness of humanitarian responses and adjust relief activities as needed.
          • Implement security measures for DCs that comply with U.S., international, and HN laws and

                regulations. While international law grants DCs the right to freedom of movement, significant
                influxes of DCs may necessitate security-related restrictions.

CAMP STANDARDS

     B-8. The CCDR specifies the facility allowances and standards for construction, quality of life, design,
     environmental conditions, and protection at base camps in-theater. These standards are tailored to the specific
     characteristics of the region, such as resource availability, local climate, the local labor market, and the
     anticipated duration of the mission. For DC camps, applicable international humanitarian standards may also
     apply. For more information on basic guidelines for facility allowances and construction standards, see
     JP 3‑34.

             Note. International humanitarian principles, minimum standards, and resources can be found at
             United Nations Refugee Agency website and the Sphere standards website.

SITE SELECTION

     B-9. Site selection involves balancing mission objectives, sustainment needs, protection measures, and
     environmental and engineering requirements. The planning team identifies potential locations for disaster
     response camps by analyzing operational and mission factors, focusing on terrain and civil and environmental
     considerations.

126  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                           Dislocated Civilian Camps

B-10. The location of a DC camp can vary widely. It may range from a spontaneous settlement spread over
a large area to a well-organized rural settlement or a concentration in a very limited space. When planning
for these camps, planners must typically determine whether establishing a few larger or several smaller camps
is more effective. Having multiple smaller camps requires them to be interconnected to optimize the use of
limited resources. Generally, smaller camps are easier to manage and control. Fewer larger camps can reduce
the number of personnel needed for command, operation, and management, allowing for consolidating
resources and capabilities. Often, the only differences in the effort required between small and large base
camps lie in the amount and type of resources used and the level of technical expertise and engineering
needed.

B-11. The security of camps should be a major consideration during the initial stages of site selection.
Implementing solutions that promote self-reliance among the dislocated population is always preferable. The
CCDR should develop a plan for PRC operations and the construction of DC camps early in the operational
plan. This approach provides timely notification of engineers, the selection and development of facility sites,
and the procurement of construction materials. Military police should coordinate with engineers, sustainment
units, higher headquarters, and the HN to determine the appropriate locations for the camps.

B-12. Existing structures, facilities, and infrastructure should be used whenever possible to minimize the
need for new construction, provided they do not pose health or environmental hazards that cannot be
mitigated. There are multiple options for constructing new facilities and infrastructure. These options include
modifying existing structures, using preengineered metal or fabric buildings, employing modular base camp
kits, and constructing buildings framed and supported by wood, steel, or concrete masonry units.

B-13. If immediate solutions to the causes of dislocation are not found, it will be necessary to engage in
long-term planning for the needs of DCs. This planning must consider all seasons (winter and summer) and
weather conditions, including rain and drought. Identifying potential weather extremes should be a key factor
in the design process. Temporary arrangements can be very difficult and costly to change once established.
Site selection, planning, and the types of shelter available all directly affect the level of assistance.

B-14. The command should analyze the logistics and operational requirements for establishing DC camps.
The first requirement is to ensure that the appropriate number and type of personnel and necessary
construction materials are onsite well before the start of hostilities. The second requirement is to develop a
long-term plan to sustain the operations and maintenance of the camps for their anticipated duration. Failing
to accurately assess the logistics and operational needs for each phase of the DC camp lifecycle early in the
process may lead to inefficiencies that waste valuable resources and ultimately undermine the overall
mission.

B-15. If a DC camp is not located correctly, the entire camp population may need to be relocated once
resources are depleted. Planners consider the following when selecting a site for a DC camp:

     • Operational and mission variables. The specific goals and requirements of the camp.
     • Accessibility. The proximity of the site to other organizations (both military and CIV) that can

          provide additional support and protection.
     • Relief organization access. The ability of CIV relief organizations to access and operate at the

          camp location.
     • Real estate viability. The feasibility of acquiring the necessary real estate.
     • Threat assessment. The threat level and aggression of adversaries in the area.
     • Local population attitude. The sentiment and responsiveness of the locals towards the camp.
     • Transportation accessibility. The accessibility of the site for support elements and transportation

          routes.
     • Hazardous structures. Proximity to potentially dangerous structures (such as dams and industrial

          chemical plants) that could pose a risk if damaged or destroyed.
     • Military target avoidance. Distance from communications centers, large military installations,

          ammunition storage areas, and other potential military targets that should be avoided.
     • Transportation networks. Accessibility to MSRs, transportation hubs, and distribution centers.
     • Existing facilities. The availability of suitable existing facilities to minimize the need for new

          construction.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  127


---

Appendix B

          • Natural disaster susceptibility. Vulnerability to natural disasters such as flooding, earthquakes, or
                fires.

          • Public health management. Management and control of food and water resources, and the presence
                of vermin, vectors (disease carriers), and drainage issues affecting public health.

          • Potable water supply. Access to an adequate source of clean drinking water to meet consumption,
                food sanitation, and personal hygiene needs.

          • Infrastructure availability. The existence of essential infrastructure, including power, sewage,
                water supply, and waste disposal systems.

          • Construction resources. The availability of labor and materials for construction.
          • Soil drainage. Considerations regarding the soil drainage capabilities of the site.
          • Environmental considerations. Any other relevant environmental factors.

SECURITY

     B-16. The security of U.S. forces, assets, and information is a high priority, and this importance remains
     consistent during DC operations, just as it does during offensive or defensive operations. Risks such as
     banditry, vandalism, and various levels of violent activity from criminal or unruly crowds can still arise even
     in stable environments. During humanitarian missions, there should be a balance between the necessary
     security measures and the right to live for the population of the camp without feeling like they are in an armed
     camp. Overly stringent security tactics can be intimidating and may undermine the overall purpose of the
     camp.

COMMUNICATIONS

     B-17. Effective communication is vital for mission success. Interagency organizations, international
     groups, and NGOs will each have their own communication networks. Planning must include procedures that
     ensure that these networks are interoperable and compatible among all participants. Achieving
     interoperability may require unclassified communication methods, which can be quite challenging for U.S.
     military forces.

DESIGN CONSIDERATIONS

     B-18. Design is an extension of planning. During design, the construction means; construction standards;
     quality-of-life standards; the level of camp capabilities; on-site conditions; and adaptable, scalable designs
     are matched against facility and infrastructure requirements. Planning and design are interdependent. The
     effectiveness of the design relies on the accuracy of the information gathered during planning, especially
     regarding facility and infrastructure requirements, available resources, construction methods, and site
     location.

CONSTRUCTION MEANS

     B-19. Construction may be performed by joint and multinational engineer units (troop construction),
     contractors, or a combination of both. See FM 3-34 for more information on contract construction.

     B-20. Sustainable DC camps leverage resources easily accessible through local means, established supply
     channels, and operational contract support. (For more information on operational contract support, see ATP
     4-10; for guidance on integrating Logistics Civil Augmentation Program support into these operations, see
     ATP 4-10.1.) The cost-effective use of materials and labor primarily relies on local resources, including
     selected DCs, when appropriate. Utilizing local resources tends to be less expensive and helps avoid the
     complications associated with international shipments. However, the cost-benefit analysis must consider the
     quality of materials and services rendered.

128  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                        Dislocated Civilian Camps

CONSTRUCTION METHODS

     B-21. There are multiple options for constructing facilities and infrastructure. These options include
     modifying existing structures, using preengineered metal or fabric buildings, using modular base camp kits
     (such as the Force Provider system), and building framed or supported structures using wood, steel, or
     concrete masonry units. The choice of construction method depends on factors such as the climate, the
     expected lifespan of the facility, the number of facilities to be built, and the availability of construction
     resources, including labor and materials.

Preexisting Facilities and Infrastructure

B-22. Existing facilities and infrastructure should be used whenever possible to save time, conserve
resources, and reduce the overall logistical footprint. However, using these preexisting structures comes with
certain protection risks related to survivability, safety, and force health protection. These risks must be
addressed through structural assessments and occupational and environmental health site evaluations.
Documenting the current environmental conditions is essential to limit liabilities. In some instances, existing
facilities with minor damage may be quickly repaired to make them immediately usable. For example, leaking
roofs can be temporarily fixed using plastic sheeting.

B-23. Facilities are categorized into six broad groups prioritizing using existing assets over new
construction. To the maximum extent possible, facility or real estate requirements should be met from these
categories in the following order of priority:

     • U.S.-owned, occupied, or leased facilities, including captured facilities.
     • U.S.-owned facility substitutes that are prepositioned in-theater.
     • HN and multinational support, where there is an existing agreement for the HN; multinational

          nations are to provide specific types and quantities of facilities at designated locations and times.
     • Facilities available from commercial sources.
     • U.S.-owned facility substitutes that are stored in the United States.
     • The construction of new facilities only after assessing the availability of existing assets and

          identifying shortfalls.

B-24. Public buildings and community facilities (such as schools, barracks, hotels, gymnasiums, and
warehouses) found in urban areas can provide excellent temporary or transitional accommodation. Using
these existing facilities may offer the following advantages:

     • They are not continuously occupied during regular use, allowing for the immediate accommodation
          of DCs without disrupting the existing occupants.

     • Essential services like water and sanitation are readily available, although they may be insufficient
          if a large number of DCs are present.

     • The need to construct additional structures specifically for DCs is avoided.

B-25.   The disadvantages associated with using existing facilities might include the following:
     •   Facilities can quickly become overcrowded.
     •   Sanitation and other services may become overwhelmed.
     •   Equipment and structures can suffer damage.
     •   Facilities may no longer serve their original purpose, disrupting public services for the local
         population.
     •   There may be a lack of privacy and increased protection risks.

B-26. Accommodations in public buildings should be a temporary solution. The supporting infrastructure
of these buildings (water, electricity, and sanitation) can quickly deteriorate due to concentrated use, leading
to dangerously unhealthy living conditions. The rapid decay of the buildings is largely due to their
unsuitability for housing large numbers of people and the lack of necessary infrastructure and utilities.
Additionally, the low sense of responsibility among the inhabitants contributes to this deterioration.
Moreover, since the normal use of the building must be suspended, this creates various social and economic
repercussions, which makes both local and national governments hesitant to convert public buildings into
designated facilities. If such a conversion is allowed, it is crucial to remember the need for a rapid evacuation
of the building, as the government may request this.

25 March 2026                              ATP 3-39.30  129


---

Appendix B

                                            Hurricane Katrina

          When Hurricane Katrina struck, the Superdome seemed like a suitable existing facility
          to accommodate evacuees. However, the number of people needing shelter quickly
          exceeded initial estimates. The Superdome was understaffed, undersupplied, and
          lacked accessibility due to high floodwaters, which made resupply efforts, evacuations,
          and other operations difficult. Power was lost, and the backup power provided only dim
          lighting. Conditions inside the stadium deteriorated as the population grew,
          compounded by a lack of air conditioning, running water, and proper waste
          management. Ultimately, the Superdome was declared uninhabitable and ordered to
          be evacuated as soon as possible. However, state and local officials did not have the
          necessary transportation assets to evacuate without additional support.

Tentage

     B-27. The primary advantage of using tents is that they provide a quick way to establish basic capabilities.
     However, the long-term use of tents comes with important considerations. The lifespan of a canvas tent
     depends on several factors, including its manufacturing quality, the duration of storage before deployment,
     climate conditions, and the care taken by those using it. When tents are used for long durations, provisions
     for repair materials should be considered. The longer a tent is exposed to the elements, the more difficult it
     becomes to repack, store, and reuse. Tents used outside of the United States typically cannot be returned
     unless they meet the strict cleanliness requirements mandated by Executive Order 13122. Furthermore, when
     you factor in shipping costs to remote areas, the overall expense of using tents may exceed that of employing
     local materials and labor to build base camp facilities. Other disadvantages of tents include their susceptibility
     to damage from high winds and their flammability unless made from fire-retardant materials. Additionally,
     tents may not offer adequate protection against extreme weather conditions.

     B-28. Figure B-1 shows a DC camp that utilized tents. Hundreds of tents were set up on the plains near
     Fier, Albania, as the United States established a tent city called Camp Hope on May 23, 1999, as part of
     Operation Sustain Hope. This operation aimed to provide food, water, medicine, and relief supplies while
     creating camps for refugees fleeing the Former Republic of Yugoslavia. The camp was designed to
     accommodate up to 20,000 refugees.

     Figure B-1. Example of a DC camp using tents

130  ATP 3-39.30                                   25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                 Dislocated Civilian Camps

Prefabricated or Manufactured Buildings

     B-29. Prefabricated or manufactured buildings are structures made up of several components constructed
     in a factory and then assembled onsite to complete the building. The main advantage of prefabrication is the
     time it saves during construction, which can be particularly important when timelines are affected by tactical
     needs or weather conditions. However, there are some potential disadvantages, such as the need to carefully
     handle prefabricated components (like concrete panels) and the possibility of leaks at the joints where these
     components are connected.

Traditional Construction

     B-30. Traditional construction methods using wood, steel, or concrete masonry units provide design
     flexibility, allowing for the integration of essential safety measures and the ability to adapt to specific site
     conditions. However, these methods require significant time and effort to design and construct individual
     facilities, especially on a large scale. The environmental impacts, such as soil degradation, associated with
     sourcing or using local construction materials (like timber harvesting) must also be considered.

BASIC DESIGN

     B-31. The overall physical layout of the camp should reflect a decentralized, community-based approach
     that focuses on Families, communities, or other social groups. The camp should be subdivided into sections
     for ease of administration and the relief of camp tension. Each section can function as an administrative
     subunit for managing camp operations. Major sections typically include a camp headquarters, a clinic, a
     dining facility, personal hygiene facilities, sleeping areas, and animal compounds. Sleeping areas should be
     further divided into separate sections for Families, unaccompanied children, unattached females, and
     unattached males. Cultural and religious practices should also be considered, and efforts should be made to
     keep Families together when possible.

     B-32. Site planning should adopt a bottom-up approach that begins with the characteristics and needs of
     individual Families while reflecting the wishes of the community as much as possible. Each community
     should be designed to include essential services such as latrines, showers, water points, waste collection, and
     laundry facilities. This approach fosters a sense of ownership, encouraging the community to maintain
     facilities better. Site planning and design should consider the potential transition to humanitarian
     organizations or NGOs in the future.

     B-33. The basic design for a DC camp consists of an administrative area and up to 16 enclosures, each
     capable of housing 500 individuals. The facility is intended to be expandable in increments of 500, allowing
     for greater capacity as needed. Initially, the camp is constructed with an administrative area and two 500-
     person enclosures, and it can be expanded by adding additional enclosures until reaching a maximum capacity
     of 8,000 people. The target population of the camp is approximately 8,000 CIVs. This number helps maintain
     control measures and allows for efficient administration of the camp and its population.
     Figures B-2 and B-3 on page 132 illustrate a DC facility with 16 enclosures, each accommodating 500
     persons. Additionally, each enclosure can be divided into two sub-compounds of 250 people. Each 500-
     person enclosure must be self-contained, equipped with electricity and water, and ready for immediate
     occupancy upon completion. The flexibility of the design enables various separations within the larger camp,
     accommodating single men, single women, Families, and other potential segregation requirements.

     B-34. The U.S. Army Corps of Engineers Joint Construction Management System contains basic plans,
     specifications, and material requirements for DC facilities based on the expected population size. These plans
     can easily be adapted for different climates, including temperate, frigid, tropical, and desert conditions.
     Additionally, the system offers specifications and material requirements for facilities once the dimensions
     and population needs are provided. Facilities can be quickly constructed using locally available materials,
     which allows for the use of existing supplies and local craftsmen. This approach significantly reduces costs
     and saves time. The facilities and components outlined in the Joint Construction Management System meet
     many of the construction requirements for DC camps identified during planning. Ultimately, this system aids
     units at lower tactical levels in designing DC camps, even if they lack advanced design skills and capabilities.

                                                                                                                 Dislocated Civilian Camps

OPERATIONS

     B-35. The skills required to operate a DC camp are not concentrated in a single branch or functional area.
     A base operations center is established to bring together the necessary capabilities to create synergistic
     effects. The size, composition, and configuration of the base operations center for a DC camp may vary based
     on factors such as the camp population, the complexity of facility operations and maintenance requirements,
     and the characteristics of the DC population. For smaller DC camps with simpler facilities and infrastructure,
     the base operations center may be integrated into the command post of the owning unit or function as a
     standalone operations center. The organizational structure of base operations centers and the tasks they
     perform are detailed in ATP 3-37.10. Some of the key tasks or areas of focus that are particularly relevant to
     operating DC camps include—

          • Camp control.
          • Information dissemination.
          • Liaison.
          • Initial processing.
          • Mistreatment or abuse.
          • Basic conduct.

CAMP CONTROL

     B-36. Controlling the camp population is essential for successful camp operations. DC camp commanders
     must swiftly and fairly establish and uphold discipline within the camp. They are responsible for publishing
     and enforcing rules of conduct as needed. Camp rules should be brief and kept to a minimum.

     B-37. Due to the high number of DCs that require care and control, it is preferable to use HN CIVs as staff
     for camp administration. DCs should become as involved in the administration of the camp as is viable.

INFORMATION DISSEMINATION

     B-38. Effectively sharing instructions and information with the camp population is essential.
     Communication methods can include notices on bulletin boards, posters, public address systems,
     loudspeakers, camp meetings and assemblies, or even a camp radio station. CA public information teams and
     area MISO units may be able to assist.

LIAISON

     B-39. Liaison involves coordinating with all interested agencies. The USG, military authorities, allied
     liaison officers, and representatives from local governments and charitable organizations may assist in relief
     and assistance operations.

INITIAL PROCESSING

     B-40. The initial processing of DCs begins with their transportation to a DC camp. In coordination with
     NGOs, international humanitarian organizations, and other international organizations, the HN usually assists
     in arranging this transportation. However, military assets may be necessary to move DCs from the initial
     point of contact to established collection points and camps. Processing must be conducted in a supportive
     manner, as many CIVs may be fearful and in shock. These individuals must understand the processing
     procedures and what to expect at each stage. Upon arrival, the facility commander ensures that all DCs, HN
     representatives, and other officials receive an entrance briefing. This briefing is provided in the native
     language of the DCs, and if multiple languages are represented, the facility will accommodate this by offering
     briefings in those languages. To minimize difficulties and facilitate a smoother experience, the facility
     commander may implement the following measures:

          • Maintain separation of different national and cultural groups within distinct facilities or sections.
          • Keep Families together while separating unaccompanied males, females, and children under 18,

                according to the laws of the HN regarding the age of adulthood.
          • Provide necessary information about the status and future of the DCs.
          • Allow DCs to communicate freely with camp officials.
          • Involve DCs in the administration, work, and recreational activities of the camp.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  133

     these organizations and agencies are unavailable. The number and type of processing stations can vary
     depending on the specific operation. Table B-1 also lists the stations that are generally required during DC
     operations.

     HN

     B-42. The commander of the DC camp establishes the procedures and requirements for accountability
     during operations within the camp. Translators are available throughout processing. A senior staff member
     welcomes new arrivals and ensures that they feel welcome. The DCs receive a briefing on facility policies
     and procedures, and screenings are conducted to identify security or medical concerns. They are also provided
     access to personal hygiene facilities. Family integrity is always maintained, if possible.

     B-43. Searches of arriving DCs are conducted to take control of items that may pose a risk to camp personnel
     and residents. During these searches, all prohibited items that noncombatants are not allowed to possess,
     according to U.S. or HN policy, are confiscated and labeled. Units have several options for the disposition of
     controlled property. Depending on the category of the property, units may choose to retain control of it, return
     it to the individual from whom it was taken, or hand it over to other agencies, such as local law enforcement.
     The owner should receive an official receipt, such as DA Form 3161, along with an explanation of their rights
     and the procedures for retrieving their confiscated items. Camp commanders must ensure that there is proper
     accountability for all confiscated property.

     B-44. Same-sex searches should be conducted whenever possible. Strip searches are only performed with
     special authorization and only in exceptional circumstances. Due to considerations of speed and security,

134                                   ATP 3-39.30                    25 March 2026


---

                                                                                                                 Dislocated Civilian Camps

     mixed-sex searches may be required. If such searches are necessary, they must be conducted respectfully,
     using all possible measures to avoid actions that could be perceived as sexual molestation or assault. The
     onsite supervisor must closely oversee Soldiers conducting mixed-sex searches to prevent allegations of
     sexual misconduct. Utilizing personnel from HNs, NGOs, or international humanitarian organizations to
     conduct searches may help avoid negative situations.

     B-45. Military police are responsible for classifying DCs during processing. They coordinate with CA
     personnel, NGOs, international humanitarian organizations, and other international bodies to determine the
     appropriate classifications for these CIVs. Military police personnel should anticipate a continuous need for
     reclassifying and reassessing DCs. The initial classifications of DCs are based on their statements and the
     information provided on their identification papers. Individuals who may be considered DCs, such as
     agitators or enemy plants, are identified through their activities. Reclassification of DCs may occur based on
     their true identity and/or ideology, facilitated by a CIV internee review tribunal. If DCs are reclassified as
     detainees, they will be transferred to either a theater internment facility or a strategic internment facility.

     B-46. When processing DCs, military police should use the reporting and tracking systems provided by the
     HN, other U.S. agencies (such as the DOS), or international governmental organizations. If these systems are
     unavailable, the National Detainee Reporting Center can assist commanders by granting access to the DRS,
     the official database of the DOD for detainee operations. The National Detainee Reporting Center is
     responsible for training personnel, maintaining the system, and administering access to the DRS to ensure
     accurate accountability of detainees. An identification card is issued to facilitate the identification of a DC.
     This card includes the name, photograph, and control number of the DC. The control number can be an
     internment serial number or a unique sequence number specific to the DC. Identification cards or bands help
     categorize DCs for identification purposes. Identification bands allow for rapid and reliable identification of
     individuals and may be used during resettlement operations. Although DCs cannot be prevented from
     removing or destroying these identification bands, most will accept their use for identification. If
     identification bands or cards deteriorate, they should be replaced immediately.

ADDRESSING MISTREATMENT OR ABUSE

     B-47. All DOD personnel, including military members, CIV employees, and contractors, are required to
     identify, report, and document incidents or situations that may involve the mistreatment or abuse of DCs.
     Acts and omissions that result in inhumane treatment could violate U.S. laws, U.S. policies, and the laws of
     war. Immediate action must be taken to rectify these violations. Simply reporting them is not enough. If a
     violation is ongoing, it is the responsibility of Soldiers to take action to stop it and to inform their chain of
     command.

     B-48. All personnel who observe or have knowledge of potential abuse or mistreatment must immediately
     report the incident through their chain of command or to their supervisor. Reports can also be submitted to
     military police, a judge advocate, a chaplain, or an inspector general, who will then forward the report through
     the appropriate chain of command or supervision. Reports made to other officials will be accepted and
     promptly forwarded through the chain of command of the recipient, with an informational copy sent to the
     appropriate CCDR.

     B-49. Any commander or supervisor who receives credible information regarding actual or potential abuse
     or mistreatment must report the incident immediately through the appropriate command or supervisory
     channels. This should be directed to the responsible CCDR or another relevant authority, such as CID or the
     inspector general, particularly in cases involving personnel not assigned to a CCDR. In such cases, an
     information report must also be sent to the CCDR responsible for the location of the alleged incident.

BASIC CONDUCT REMINDERS

     B-50. The following basic conduct reminders should be observed from the initial contact with DCs, during
     in-processing, and throughout DC activities. All DC camp personnel will—

          • Maintain rigorous self-discipline.
          • Uphold a professional and impartial attitude.
          • Adhere to the established guidelines in the rules of interaction and ROE.
          • Remain calm with hostile or unruly behavior or incidents.
          • Respond fairly and promptly to issues that arise.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  135

Appendix B

     B-51. The commander of the DC camp takes proactive steps to establish daily and periodic routines that
     promote discipline and control. DC camp personnel—

          • Enforce policies and procedures to maintain order among camp residents.
          • Provide clear and decisive instructions to DCs in a language they understand.
          • Display camp rules, regulations, instructions, notices, orders, and announcements in an easily

                accessible area for residents to follow. This information must be printed in a language that the DCs
                understand. Those who cannot access the posted materials will receive a printed copy (see figure
                B-4).
          • Ensure that DCs comply with orders, rules, and directives.
          • Report DCs who refuse to follow an order or regulation.
          • Enforce a policy of nonfraternization with DCs.
          • Refrain from accepting gifts from, or engaging in commercial activities with, DCs.

     Figure B-4. Example of camp rules

136  ATP 3-39.30                        25 March 2026

                                                Appendix C

Battlefield Confinement of United States Military Prisoners

    This appendix discusses the considerations for battlefield detention and confinement that a
    CCDR must take into account if they decide to retain U.S. prisoners in-theater. Battlefield
    detention and confinement facilities are crucial in the U.S. military justice system, enabling
    commanders to maintain discipline, law, and order. These facilities are equipped to provide
    both pretrial and posttrial confinement for U.S. military personnel, DOD CIV employees, DOD
    contractor personnel, and other individuals accompanying or authorized to work with the U.S.
    military during declared war and contingency operations.

BATTLEFIELD FACILITY

     C-1. The field detention facility and the field confinement facility provide a uniform system for
     incarcerating individuals and offering limited correctional and rehabilitative services to those who have
     violated established rules of discipline. Field detention facilities can operate at the BCT level. A field
     confinement facility is established at the theater level and is responsible for longer-term confinement before
     the evacuation of U.S. military prisoners from that theater. The process of evacuating U.S. military prisoners
     from a field detention facility to a field confinement facility, or from a field confinement facility to a
     permanent facility, is conducted according to established and coordinated guidelines and the availability of
     appropriate facilities.

FIELD DETENTION FACILITY

     C-2. Military police use field detention facilities to temporarily hold prisoners in custody. These facilities
     are designed to keep prisoners until they can be tried, sentenced, and transferred away from the immediate
     area. Prisoners awaiting trial remain with their units whenever possible instead of being placed in a field
     detention facility. According to the legal requirements established in Rules for Courts-Martial 305(k) (see
     the Manual for Courts-Martial), prisoners can be placed in pretrial confinement and retained by military
     police. This rule requires a probable cause belief that a court-martial offense has occurred, that the prisoner
     is responsible for it, and that a more stringent form of restraint is necessary to ensure that the prisoner attends
     pretrial proceedings or the trial, or to prevent serious criminal misconduct. PMs are responsible for the
     location, setup, and operation of these field detention facilities.

     C-3. When operating a field detention facility, military police use DD Form 2707 (Confinement Order) to
     sign for each prisoner and DA Form 4137 to document the property of each prisoner. Follow established
     policies and procedures regarding the care and treatment of prisoners and safeguarding their personal effects
     in both field detention and field confinement facilities. If preexisting structures are available, they should be
     used as field detention facilities. Tents should be no smaller than the medium general-purpose tent in cases
     where they are necessary. Probable equipment and supplies required for the establishment of a field detention
     facility include, but are not limited to:

          • Barbed wire (roll and concertina).
          • Fence posts.
          • Gates and doors.
          • Floodlights and spotlights.
          • Generators.
          • Food service and cleaning equipment.
          • Water cans and/or lister bags.
          • First-aid supplies and equipment.
          • Field sanitation equipment and supplies.
          • Clothing and bedding.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  137


---

Appendix C

FIELD CONFINEMENT FACILITY

     C-4. Military police may need to establish a field confinement facility in the theater to detain prisoners for
     a short term, whether for pretrial, posttrial, or until they can be transferred to another facility outside the
     theater. Accompanying the prisoner will be DA Form 4137 and DD Form 2707. The field confinement
     facility can be either semipermanent or permanent and should be better equipped and resourced than a field
     detention facility (see figure C-1 for an example of a field confinement facility). The unit commander and
     staff will use the military decision-making process to identify the tasks necessary to accomplish the mission.
     These tasks may include—

          • Selecting a suitable location and constructing the facility.
          • Determining processing, classification, and identification requirements.
          • Providing clothing and meals.
          • Providing medical care and sanitation facilities.
          • Exercising discipline, control, and administration.
          • Conducting emergency planning and investigations.
          • Enforcing ROE and RUF.
          • Providing transportation.
          • Overseeing the transfer and disposition of U.S. military prisoners.

                                  Figure C-1. Field confinement facility example

     C-5. The location of the field confinement facility is influenced by several factors, including the availability
     of sustainment assets such as transportation and medical treatment facilities, the terrain and existing
     structures, the enemy situation, existing LOC, the layout of the battlefield, and mission variables. Before
     selecting a site, the PM must collaborate with engineers, the staff judge advocate, HN authorities, and other
     coordinating staff. The facility should be positioned away from perimeter fences, public thoroughfares, gates,
     headquarters, troop areas, dense vegetation, and wooded areas.

138  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                   Battlefield Confinement of United States Military Prisoners

     C-6. The construction of the field confinement facility depends on the availability of existing structures,
     workforce, and materials. Existing facilities will be used to the fullest extent possible. If no preexisting
     facilities are available, the PM should coordinate with the engineering staff to construct a facility based on
     designs in the Joint Construction Management System database.

PROCESSING, CLASSIFYING, AND IDENTIFYING

     C-7. Processing, classifying, and identifying U.S. military prisoners are critical for operating a correctional
     facility. Accurate documentation ensures that the classification and identification processes can run smoothly.
     The accountability of all prisoners in field confinement facilities is maintained using the Army Corrections
     Information System module of the Army Command Information System, when available.

Processing

     C-8. Each time the control of a U.S. military prisoner is transferred, the receiving organization uses
     DA Form 4137 to acknowledge receipt. This documents the property of the prisoner during the transfer
     process.

     C-9. Upon arrival, prisoners undergo in-processing at the field confinement facility, which is managed by
     a military police detention company, prisoner operations section. This process helps to integrate newly
     confined prisoners into the correctional environment. Newly confined prisoners are processed according to
     the guidelines to ensure that—

          • DD Form 2707 is accurate.
          • Property is searched and segregated (authorized and unauthorized).
          • Prisoners are strip-searched.
          • Prisoners are issued the appropriate health and comfort supplies and complete a DD Form 504

                (Request and Receipt for Health and Comfort Supplies).
          • Prisoners are photographed and fingerprinted.
          • Prisoner DNA samples are taken.
          • All documents are completed. Use the Army Corrections Information System Centralized

                Operations Police Suite (see AR 190-47).
          • Prisoners are informed of mail and visitation rights.

     C-10. A medical officer examines each prisoner within 24 hours of confinement and completes
     DD Form 503 (Health Assessment Certificate for Segregation) or SF 600 (Chronological Record of Medical
     Care). Newly admitted prisoners are segregated from the general population during their initial processing.
     Tattoos, scars, and identifying marks are recorded on DD Form 2710 (Prisoner Background Summary). The
     personal property of the prisoner (clothing, money, official papers, and documents) is examined.

     C-11. Newly confined prisoners complete training that explains the facility rules and regulations, counseling
     procedures, the Uniform Code of Military Justice disciplinary authority and procedures, and work assignment
     procedures as soon as possible. The rights of prisoners and the procedures for presenting complaints and
     grievances, as detailed in AR 20-1, are fully and clearly explained. Pretrial prisoners receive careful
     instruction regarding their privileges, status, and rights. They participate in the correctional orientation or
     treatment program phases determined necessary by the facility commander to ensure custody and control,
     and to support their employment, training, health, and welfare. Confined officers and noncommissioned
     officers do not exercise command or supervisory authority over others while incarcerated and must adhere
     to the same facility rules and regulations as other prisoners. They are not granted special privileges typically
     associated with their former rank.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  139


---

Appendix C

Classifying

     C-12. U.S. military prisoners in a field confinement facility are classified into two categories:
          • Pretrial prisoners. These individuals have been ordered into pretrial confinement by a competent
                authority while awaiting trial by court-martial. Pretrial prisoners must be kept separate from those
                who have already completed their trial. Additionally, they must be segregated by sex and classified
                according to rank, including officer, noncommissioned officer, or enlisted Soldier.
          • Posttrial prisoners. This category includes individuals who have been found guilty and sentenced to
                confinement. Posttrial prisoners also encompass in-transit prisoners being transferred to another
                facility, and those who remain at the field confinement facility for short-term sentences.

Identifying

     C-13. All prisoners have individual identification photographs taken. The last name, first name, and middle
     initial of the prisoner are displayed on the first line of a name board, while the prisoner registration number
     is placed on the second line. Two front-facing photographs and two profile pictures of the prisoner are
     captured. Fingerprints are obtained according to AR 190-47.

CLOTHING, MEALS, AND DINING FACILITIES

     C-14. One of the many challenges that military police commanders and leaders face when operating a facility
     is ensuring that the basic treatment standards for U.S. military prisoners are upheld. These standards include,
     but are not limited to—

          • Proper clothing for all seasons and weather.
          • Meals that are properly rationed and distributed.

     C-15. Dining facilities must address special security concerns. Military police guarding U.S. military
     prisoners need to remain vigilant in areas where prisoners gather, such as dining facilities. Proper planning
     is critical for establishing an effective system that meets supply needs and ensures that those requirements
     are fulfilled.

Clothing

     C-16. Prisoners at a field confinement facility wear the uniform of their military Service. Certain clothing
     items, as specified in AR 700-84, and additional articles, as determined by the facility commander, are
     returned to the prisoner. Rank insignia is not worn while in confinement. The issuance and cost of clothing
     supplied to prisoners (excluding officers) follow the guidelines in AR 700-84 and CTA 50-900.
     DA Form 3078 (Personal Clothing Request) is maintained for personnel with less than six months of active
     duty service and those receiving clothing on an issue-in-kind basis. Within the allowances specified in
     CTA 50-900, organizational clothing may be provided to prisoners according to AR 710-2. Clothing for
     prisoners, except for officers on pay status, is laundered or dry-cleaned at no charge (see AR 210-130). The
     disposal of clothing and personal property is conducted according to AR 190-47.

Meals

     C-17. Prisoners receive nutritious and sufficient meals prepared according to the Army Master Menu. They
     are typically provided with all necessary eating utensils. However, the field confinement facility commander
     must approve the withholding of utensils. Prisoners in close confinement, those who have lost privileges, or
     those undergoing approved disciplinary actions may be denied supplemental rations as outlined in the Army
     Master Menu. To ensure safety and prevent injuries to staff and prisoners, the field confinement facility
     commander or a designated representative may implement alternative meal control procedures if a prisoner
     is suspected of tampering with food. A medical officer must document and approve these procedures and
     should not exceed seven days.

140  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

                                                                   Battlefield Confinement of United States Military Prisoners

Dining Facilities

     C-18. Dining facilities may be part of the unit operating the field confinement facility or established through
     appropriate contracting procedures. The commander of the field confinement facility must determine the
     most effective method for providing meals to the prisoners, considering the available dining facilities,
     logistics, and support from the HN.

MEDICAL CARE AND SANITATION

     C-19. Medical personnel at a field confinement facility provide medical and behavioral health care, referrals,
     limited counseling, and social services. Medical officers, clinical nurses, or physician assistants conduct
     medical examinations to assess the fitness of newly confined prisoners and those outside military control for
     more than 24 hours. These examinations are completed within 24 hours of the initial arrival of the prisoner
     or return to confinement, typically at the field confinement facility. Dental services are also available to all
     prisoners as needed. A medical officer, clinical nurse, or physician assistant examines each prisoner in close
     confinement daily. Except in cases that require the protection of medical information, the facility commander
     is informed of medical observations and recommendations regarding the correctional treatment needs of an
     individual prisoner.

     C-20. Prisoners are tested for human immunodeficiency virus and screened for tuberculosis within three duty
     days of their initial confinement. The human immunodeficiency virus test and the tuberculosis screening
     results are recorded on DD Form 503 or SF 600.

     C-21. The medical commander or a designated representative, such as preventive medicine personnel,
     conducts a monthly inspection of the field confinement facility. This inspection ensures that the facility
     operates according to established preventive medicine standards. The field confinement facility commander
     receives a copy of the inspection results immediately after completion. Additional medical guidance can be
     found in AR 190-47.

     C-22. The commander of the field confinement facility must enforce strict sanitation standards. Preventive
     medicine personnel provide direct oversight and support to the field sanitation teams as needed.

     C-23. Prisoners must maintain personal hygiene and bathe regularly while in custody to prevent the spread
     of communicable diseases. The commander of the field confinement facility is responsible for enforcing high
     sanitation standards in facilities where prisoners share common latrines and showers.

DISCIPLINE, CONTROL, AND ADMINISTRATION

     C-24. Establishing discipline, control, and administrative procedures for military police managing
     correctional facilities is crucial for the success of U.S. military corrections operations. Military police leaders
     must implement procedures that comply with U.S. laws and policies to guide personnel working in these
     facilities. These procedures ensure that prisoners are allowed the full range of privileges afforded to persons
     of their status if facility standards are consistently applied.

Discipline

     C-25. Field confinement facility commanders are authorized by public law and AR 190-47 to—
          • Restrict the movement and actions of prisoners.
          • Take necessary measures to maintain control.
          • Protect the safety and welfare of prisoners and other personnel.
          • Ensure the orderly operation and administration of the field confinement facility.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  141


---

Appendix C

             Note. A prisoner is considered on duty except during mandatory sleep periods, meal times, and
             reasonable periods for voluntary religious observance, as determined by the facility commander
             in coordination with the facility chaplain. Therefore, a prisoner involved in administrative
             disciplinary action or deemed ineligible for recreation time privileges may be required to perform
             other duties during that time. This performance of duty does not constitute extra duty. Privileges
             are withheld from prisoners individually, without regard to custody requirements or rank, and only
             as a disciplinary measure authorized by AR 190-47. The living conditions and the type or quantity
             of personal items prisoners can possess may vary based on their custody rank to incentivize
             elevation in custody status. Prisoners are not permitted to render military salutes. However, pretrial
             prisoners must salute when they are in the appropriate Service uniform.

     C-26. The only authorized forms of administrative disciplinary action and punishment for military prisoners
     are outlined in AR 190-47 and the Manual for Courts-Martial. Procedures, rules, regulations, living
     conditions, and other factors influencing discipline are regularly reviewed to determine the appropriate
     disciplinary measures. Physical or mental punishments are strictly prohibited. The authorized administrative
     disciplinary actions include the following:

          • Written or oral reprimand or warning. This includes formal notifications regarding inappropriate
                behavior.

          • Deprivation of privileges. Visits may be denied or restricted only when the offense directly
                involves violations of visitation privileges. However, restrictions on mail are not permitted as a
                disciplinary action.

          • Extra duty. Prisoners may be assigned extra duties not exceeding two hours per day for a maximum
                of 14 consecutive days. This extra duty cannot interfere with regular meal times, sleeping hours, or
                attending scheduled religious services.

          • Reduction of custody grade. Prisoners may face a decrease in their custody classification.
          • Disciplinary segregation. This measure can last up to 60 consecutive days. Prisoners will be

                informed of the reasons for their segregation and released after serving the specified time. During
                segregation, they will receive the same diet as nonsegregated prisoners; however, nonessential items
                (such as soft drinks and candy) not part of the Army Master Menu will not be provided.
          • Forfeiture of good conduct time. Prisoners may lose all or part of their earned military good
                conduct time or extra good conduct time according to AR 633-30 and DODI 1325.07. The forfeiture
                does not need to specify whether the time lost is from good conduct time or extra good conduct
                time.

     C-27. The commander of the field confinement facility is authorized to administer punishments but may
     delegate this authority to a subordinate officer (captain or above) for minor punishments. Major punishments
     are imposed by the first field grade commander in the chain of command when they have been delegated
     authority by the first general officer in that chain of command. Prohibited punitive measures include, but are
     not limited to—

          • Clipping hair excessively short on a prisoner.
          • Instituting the lockstep.
          • Requiring silence during meals.
          • Having prisoners break rocks.
          • Using restraining straps and jackets, shackles, or hand or leg irons as forms of punishment.
          • Removing the underclothing or clothing of a prisoner or subjecting them to other degrading

                practices.
          • Flogging, branding, tattooing, or cruel or unusual punishment.
          • Mandating strenuous physical activity or requiring a prisoner to hold a body position that induces

                undue stress on the body.
          • Using hand or leg irons, belly chains, or similar restraints to create the appearance of a chain gang.

142  ATP 3-39.30  25 March 2026


---

               Battlefield Confinement of United States Military Prisoners

C-28.   Prohibited security measures include, but are not limited to—
     •     Employing chemicals to subdue or incapacitate prisoners (except riot control agents).
     •     Employing machine guns, rifles, or automatic weapons at guard towers, except to protect the field
           confinement facility from enemy or hostile fire. Selected marksmen equipped with rifles may be
     •     used as part of a disorder plan specifically authorized by the higher echelon commander (other than
     •     the field confinement facility commander).
           Using electrically charged fencing.
     •     Securing a prisoner to a fixed object. This is prohibited, except in emergencies or when specifically
           approved by the facility commander to prevent potential danger to the field confinement facility
           staff and/or the outside community. Medical authorities should be consulted to assess the health risk
           to prisoners.
           Using MWDs to guard prisoners.

Control

     C-29. The commander of the field confinement facility adheres to the custody and control guidelines outlined
     in AR 190-47. The facility commander, or a designated representative, conducts physical counts of prisoners
     daily. The report submitted by the inspecting officer includes verification of DD Form 506 (Daily Strength
     Record of Prisoners). Physical counts must include, at a minimum, the following:

          • A roll call or a similarly accurate accounting method during morning, noon, and evening formations.
          • A head count immediately upon the return of prisoners from work details.
          • Bed checks conducted between 2300 and 2400 hours, and between 2400 and 0600 hours.

     C-30. The appropriate level of custodial supervision for each prisoner is determined by reviewing all relevant
     records, including the DD Form 2713 (Prisoner Observation Report), DD Form 2714 (Prisoner Disciplinary
     Report/Action), DODI 1325.07, and recommendations from corrections supervisors and support personnel.
     Prisoners are not assigned to a permanent custody grade solely based on the offenses for which they were
     confined. Instead, prisoner classification is established at the minimum custody grade necessary while
     adhering to sound security requirements and the guidelines outlined in DODI 1325.07. The custody grades
     include trustee, minimum, medium, and maximum security. Field confinement facility commanders may
     further subdivide these custody grades to implement additional security controls.

Administration

     C-31. The commander and staff of a military police detention company or battalion operates a field
     confinement facility. In addition to fulfilling personnel and service requirements during processing, they
     perform the following duties:

          • Shift supervisor. The shift supervisor keeps the commander of the field confinement facility
                informed about matters affecting the custody, control, and security of the facility. The commander
                must select a shift supervisor who directly oversees correctional and custodial personnel within the
                facility. Shift supervisors ensure compliance with rules, regulations, and the SOP. They directly
                supervise facility guards and are responsible for overseeing prisoner activities. Additionally, they
                monitor custody, control, and security measures; ensure adherence to scheduled calls; and initiate
                emergency and control procedures. Supervisory personnel assigned to the facility may also carry
                out these duties.

          • Facility correctional officers and guards. Facility guards report to the shift supervisor and are
                responsible for the custody, control, and discipline of prisoners. They manage activities according
                to the established schedule and oversee the execution of emergency action plans. Their
                responsibilities include conducting periodic inspections, searches, head counts, roll calls, and bed
                checks. Table C-1, page 144, outlines the specific duties that facility guards must perform.

25 March 2026  ATP 3-39.30  143

Appendix C

                 Table C-1. Facility guards duties and actions

    Duties                                                              Actions

Close            Close-confinement Soldiers are responsible for maintaining custody and control of
confinement      prisoners who are separated from the general population for reasons such as in-
                 processing, administrative issues, or disciplinary actions. They ensure that activities are
                 carried out according to the schedule applicable to the close-confinement area. When a
                 DD Form 509 (Inspection Record of Prisoner in Segregation) is required, close-
                 confinement Soldiers must conduct checks every 30 minutes. Special-status prisoners are
                 checked every 15 minutes, while prisoners identified as suicide risks are monitored
                 continuously. Guards are also responsible for obtaining all required signatures on the DD
                 Form 509 daily.

Dining facility  Dining facility Soldiers are responsible for the custody and control of prisoners during
                 mealtimes. They ensure that the dining facility traffic plan is followed to prevent prisoner
                 congestion in high-traffic areas. Silverware is counted before and after the meal. Prisoners
                 are searched before leaving the dining facility.

Detail           Detail supervisors maintain custody, control, and supervision of prisoners on assigned
                 details. They ensure that work is completed and that safety precautions are observed.
                 They maintain strict accountability of equipment and tools. Detail supervisors assist with
                 frisking and/or strip-searching prisoners who are returning from details. They account for
                 prisoners on details according to the schedule of calls. They constantly track the location
                 of the prisoner(s) while they are on a detail.

Prisoner         Prisoner escorts provide custody and control while moving prisoners to and from
escorts          designated places. If required and authorized by the facility commander, each may be
                 armed with a pistol. If armed, escorts are qualified with a pistol and trained in RUF, ROE,
                 and firearms safety procedures for transporting prisoners by land, air, and sea.

Main gate        Soldiers assigned to the main gate and/or sally port ensure that only authorized persons
and/or sally     enter the field confinement facility, provide custody and control of prisoners, and inspect
port             vehicles entering and leaving the field confinement facility. They provide security by
                 inspecting packages, conducting inventories of items entering and exiting the facility, and
Visitor room     requiring noncustodial personnel to register on sign-in logs.

Medical          Visitor room Soldiers are responsible for the custody and control of prisoners during visits
treatment        authorized by the field confinement facility commander. They are to detect violations of
facility         rules and regulations, improper behavior, and contraband delivery. They position
                 themselves in an inconspicuous place and observe the conversations rather than listen to
                 them. Any identified infractions are reported to the shift supervisor and may be grounds for
                 termination of the visit.

                 Health care escorts provide custody and control while escorting prisoners to and from
                 medical appointments and during specified hospitalizations within the medical treatment
                 facility. They ensure that rooms are clear of contraband and prevent unauthorized
                 communications with other individuals. For those prisoners evacuated through medical
                 channels, the echelon commander or supporting military police commander must provide
                 guards/escorts, as health care personnel cannot perform guard duties.

Tower watch      Soldiers assigned to duty in towers provide custody and control by observing specific
                 perimeter sectors. The Soldiers are briefed on the use of force and are qualified with a 12-
                 gauge shotgun and/or their assigned weapon. They ensure that contraband is not passed
                 through the fence and protect Soldiers in the compound/enclosure.

Note. The facility commander may adjust the number and types of guards based on available personnel.
